INTERNATIONAL VIEW OF THE VIOLENT
REBELLION IN TOKYO.
We do not agree with the view that Japan's forelsm
relations will be abruptly changed beeause of this ineident.
The reason is that Japan's policy has long been decided by
the Military Headquarters.
From the point of view of the international effeets,
this incident is naturally disadvantageous to Japan. Great
Britain, the United States, and Russia will have to make every
effort to expand their armaments. When they were considerimg
their foreign policy toward Japan on the basis of the speeshes
and activities of the political leaders who could be considered
representatives of Japan, it was unknown whether those leaders
could protect their lives until the next morning. Then how
can sineere diplomacy be condueted? In any event this is
certainly a heavy loss to Japan.
Officials and people of the various countries are paying
special attention to the reorganization of the Japanese
Government. In order to pacify the soldiers, Japan will have
to use Araki to form a Cabinet.
Chinese people who can thoroughly examine this matter
will not consider this violent rebellion as having any special
effect on the present Sino-Japanese relations. China is
accustomed to hearing about an active and strong poliey and

I have the honor to enclose a translation made in
this office of an editorial published in the Nanking
HSIN MIN PAO, of March 3, 1936, entitled "The Future of
Japan".
Comment on this incident published in the Chinese
press seems to have been restrained in tone, possibly
by order of the censors. The editorial enclosed with
this despatch is fairly typical of comment in general
and is submitted for that reason.
F/FG
It will
11

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
It will be observed that the writer points out that
the mutiny of February 26 is symptomatic of serious deterio-
ration in a state governed by law, such as is Japan; that
the revolt did not have the justification of a well-organiz-
ed party behind it, as decisive revolutionary movements in
European countries have had, and had the appearance of be-
ing the wilful act of a military faction, not the logical
effect of a conflict between socialist and capitalist
forces.
The conclusion of the editorial is that since "Japanese
society is comparatively well-organized and its people are
comparatively educated", the outcome of the struggle be-
tween military and civilian leaders may quite possibly be
the submission of the military faction.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Milla I Peh
Willys R. Peck,
Counselor of Embassy.

Original and four copies to Department.
One copy to Peiping.

Editorial published in Hsin Min Pao of Maroh 
Trans. Li.
THE FUTURE OF JAPAN
It has been a week since the coup d'etat of Fobrumry
occurred in Japan, and the impressions given us conserning the
incident are three-fold: Firstly, the revolting soldiers
only surrendered under the strict orders of the Emperer and
after they were compelled to do so by armed force. Seeonaly
only the lower military officers of the revolting army were
dismissed and puhished while the higher officers in command
of the coup remained in their positions and acted as mediators.
Lastly, the present cabinet tendered its resignation, and it
is reported that the candi ates for the new cabinet must be
selected in full compliance with the ideas of the Ministry
of War.
The coup has now become an event of the past and al-
though no signs of future developments have been revealed
leading to definite conclusions, yet the outlook of Japan's
politics is certainly dark.
Japan is a law governing state. Everything she does
must be according to law and everything must be governed
by law. The open murder of the highest officials and the
forcible occupation of important official quarters by men
in active military service are certainly detrimental to law
Moreover, the faot that rehabilitation measures taken after
the incident must  first be the subject of consultation
with the military officers responsible for the coup, cor-
tainly marks the deoline of the power of the Govornment.
Although

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.95)
-2
Although it was stated in the proclamation of thp
Law Commander that the Emperor had ordered the revoltime
soldiers to return to their camps, yet the solaiors pro
claimed that the order was a forged one. They surrendered
only after they had been forced to do so by guns and air-
planes.
The supreme leader of the Japanese Government is the
Emperor and the military youths have declared themselves
loyal and patriotic. Being loyal and patriotic, they
should absolutely obey the orders of the Emperor and should
not be compelled to obey by force of arms. Therefore the
coup not only damages the constitutional government of
Japan, but is also not agreeable with their aims of loyalty
and patriotism.
A coup d'etat in any country may be accomplished by
violent actions, as in France and Russia, but they all have
the object of upsetting feudalism. The ocoupation of Rome
by fascists in Italy and the seizure of political power by
the National Socialist Party in Germany all have had as their
support well organized parties. They were not merely un-
restrained actions of a certain number of military men. The
aots of the Japanese military youths show the ambitions of
the fascists, but they do not have a well organizel party
like that of the fasoists. They say that they are loyal to
their Emperor and that they desire to oust the fimeneieps
and oapitalists. Their coup d'etat is not ltme th
Franoe, neither that of Rumsia, nor those of
hiion
eap11am

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.97)
The motive of the coup d'etat was
military mon to usurp political power by violone
manager the government as the fasoists do. The
Russia and Italy all have well organized politioal par
as their supports, but the military youths in Japan have
only armed force. They hope to gain the confidenoe of th
peor e, so they use the words "loyal to the Emperor" as
their pretext.
The lower military orficers of the revolting army have
been dismissed and punished, but the higher officers respon-
sible for the coup are acting as mediators. It remains
question of doubt whether they will be punished also.
Regarding the formation of the new cabinet, all eyes
are focused on the Ministry of War. The outoome may be a
cabinet of military fascists. If the elder statesmen and
political parties of Japan unite and suppress the military,
still further violence will not be avoided. If, on the
other hand, the military are given a free hand to aot as
fascists, constitutional government in Japan will be ruin
and the military discipline will not be maintained. It will
be extromely dangerous for Japan to allow the. military to
become fascists.
Nevertheless, Japanese society is a comparatively
well organized sooiety and its people are comparatively
educased. The present struggle between the military and
the elde statesmen and financiers of the
party may cause them to wake up

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 20, 1936.
EHD:
Shanghai's despatch No. 77, March 13,
1936, forwards newspaper comment on the
new Japanese Cabinet.
The English language press, with the
exception of one paper under Japanese
control, feels that the Army will dominate
the Cabinet. The SHANGHAI EVENING POST
AND MERCURY (American) remarks that "un-
abashed by any responsibility for the
recent assassinations" the Japanese military
chieftains have blocked efforts to consti-
tute a new government, evidencing "an un-
blushing willingness to take advantage of
any strategic gain in which the coup may
have resulted."
The SHANGHAI TIMES (British but alleged
to be under Japanese control) expresses
disappointment in the appointment of Baba
to succeed Takahash1 because of his
opposition to the late Finance Minister's
moderate financial policies. The paper sees
in Hirota the "bright spot in the whole
picture" and expects him to find difficulty
in holding "the new team together". The
opinion is expressed that Japan will not
adopt a provocatively hostile attitude in
her

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
her foreign relations but rather that
she is going to take note of all the
realities of the situatlon."
The Chinese wernacular press fore-
sees a government, under military in-
fluence, pursuing an aggressive policy
1n China and predicts that the new
Cabinet will fall because of budgetary
difficulties.
Remarks made by T. V. Soong to a
newspaper correspondent are of interest:
(1) recent developments would only
result in a speeding up of the Japanese
program, (2) matters appeared to be
approaching a crisis, and (3) hostilities
were expected to break out in some
quarter in the near future.

military and naval provision in the

Perhaps now that the responsibility of
the Exchequer is his he will have to
realize the practical difficulties of
financing, for increased taxation will
not be popular and there is obvious
danger in any further overloading of
the public debt. It remains to be seen
how he will proceed, but Mr. Hirota, at
the helm, will doubtless exert his own
moderating influence. The fact that
Mr. Hirota is the Premier is the bright
spot in the whole picture, and every-
body will wish him well in his most
difficult task.
In an interview with press repre-
sentatives Mr. Hirota has declared his
belief that, in view of the present
internal and external situation, all
past maladministration should be
eradicated while Japan's foreign rela-
tions need to be placed on an independ-
t and active basis. One need not
terpret this as indicating that Japan
is going to adopt a provocatively
hostile attitude in her foreign relations
but rather that she is going to take
note of all the realities of the situa-
tion. The new Premier has pleaded for
all political parties, the military, and
the bureaucrats to discard their differ-
ences and to establish a firm national
policy and carry it out, and if he can
manage to get co-operative agreement
between all those interests then he
will be able to make an undisputed
claim to speak in 'the name of the
nation as a whole. It is that need which
has been felt wanting in Japan for a
long time past, and it is the "national"
character of the new Cabinet which,
provided it can be worked, gives it such
potential strength. We have seen in a
good many countries in recent years
the closing of party ranks in face of
national difficulties and how much has
thereby been accomplished which other-
wise would not have been possible, and
it is very much to be hoped that Japan
will likewise profit from the formula-
tion and execution of a policy which
represents the pooled views of different
factions and interests.

There are several ways in waten one
reaction on the part of the averag
would be one of complete astomimmemt at the
Army's temerity in aking sucha position in
the very shadow of
the rve "incident" which
has shocked the world.belleve that such
reaction must be by many clylia
Japanese'who have net ben carried away in
the fast-running current of Japanes imperial-
istic policy.
Another reaction, more clinical in nature
would be to discern the Army's usuat tenaeity
in capitalizing even the most adverse turm of
fortune to its advantage. Certainly there can
be few outside the Army who are at this tim
in a mood to sympathize whole-heartedly with
the mutineers who engineered the coup; the
Army itself stands officially opposed; yet it is
obvious enough that this coup represented
something for which many sections of the
Army teel sympathy. and there is an unblush
ing willingness to take advantage of any
strategic gain in which the coup may have
resulted whether those who so take advantage
are openly with the mutineers or aganst th
Mr Hirota himself is regarded as close to
the Army in his attitude toward policies both
within and without the nation. Although 
leading member of the last Cabinet, his personal
safety was not at any time in jeopardy durtng
the recent coup which is a signfficant detai
when it is considered how widespread the plot
was, even extending to include the person of
the revered last of the Genro. It would seem
that the Army is happy to see him as Premier
but desires to see under him a thoroughly hand
picked military-minded Cabinet, with the Army
doing the picking.
Events of the last few hours and the next
few hours may be, therefore, of extraordinary
significance in pointing Japan's future courses.
If the military prevail without check, the world
cannot but take that fact as officlal endorse-
ment of the recent coup in all its aspects. It
is indeed surprising that the Army feels itself
able to ask such a supreme vote of confidence
at a time when it might well regard itself as
in disgrace; but on the other hand, it is hard
to discern factors of opposition in Japan which
now possess sufficient strength to admimister a
check.
No matter how often Japan's spokesmen
may repeat that the recent coup was of purely
internal significance, this Army challenge to the
formation of the new Government, coming as
it does immediately following the coup, un-
mistakably makes the coup of  international
significance. For the Government as a whole
will shape Japan's policies both at home and
abroad, and a Goverament largely of, eertaimly
by and for the Army will wahus quest
strengthen Japanese military aggresslon. abroad
There is no other interpretation open.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 10, 1936.
STH:
Tokyo's 1735 of March 19, 1936,
analyzing "the meesure of success achieved
by the February 26 insurgents", is an
interesting despatch prepared by Andrews.
It indicates in outline the aims which the
insurgents sought to accomplish, enumerates
developments subsequent to the outbreak
which appear to be favorable or unfavorable
to the accomplishment of the aims of the
insurgents, and concludes with an expression
of the opinion that "the aims of the in-
surgents which failed are outweighed by
those that succeeded."
That opinion was expressed in regard
to the aims as a whole of the insurgents.
No attempt was made to divide these
aims into those which fall into the field
of foreign policy and those which relate
to domestic policy. The opinion is, there-
fore, not a conclusive reply to the cues-
tion (in which we are peculiarily interest-
ed) whether developments arising out of
the incident indicate a rise or decline
of chauvinism in Japan.
This well-prepared despatch is well
worth reading in full.
EHD/DLY

It is true that the insurrection was successfully quelled
in a military way, that the radical or "direct action" group
in

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.98)

in the Army and its civilian sympathizers are at presont in
disfavor and are being rounded up by the authorities for
eventual trial*, that severel of the revolting officers have
committed suicide, and that the remainder have been expelled
from the Army. On the other hand the actions of the younger
officers who took part in the revolt have definitely borne
fruit in line with the program of grievances of these offi-
cers.
It has been stated recently by Army leaders now in
control that not only must stricter discipline in the Army
be established but that also the "fundamental causes" of the
insurrection must be wiped out. For example, General Tere-
uchi, the newly appointed War Minister, issued a statement
on March 9 in which, among other things, he said:
"As for
the Incident, the causes of it were widespread and deeply
rooted.
It should be .ade the occasion for endeavors to
clarify the principles of the foundation of the Army and
remove the evils with wnich it is affected.
Effor ts must
be made to make it manifest that the Army is under the dir-
ect commend of the Emperor."
Incidentally, an "Army under
the direct command of the Emperor" was one of the battle-
cries of the insurgents and the banners flown from their
headquarters stated:
"Loyalty to the Emperor and up with
the pure Army."**
General Terauchi went on to state that
when discipline in the Army had been perfected, efforts
should be made to clarify the national polity, promote
the welfare of the people, make administrative reforms and
strengthen national defense. The adoption of such a program
was an importent part of the rebels' plans.

Furthermore, according to the ASAHI of March 7, on that
day General Terauchi told Mr. Hirota that the Army insisted
upon utilizing the February 26 incident as a turning point
in Japanese policy and on establishing a fundamental policy
based on state control and complete reform of the national
administration, with the rejection of the liberal policy pur-
sued since the Meiji Restoration.
There are distinct indica-
tions that the Army's desires as expressed by the War Minister
will meet with fulfilment at the hands of the Cabinet. Mr.
Hirota has reportedly stated that his views are not at variance
with those of the War Minister.
In his "verbal statement" of
March 9, Mr. Hiro ta implied that the "present unfortunate in-
cident" was fundamentally connected with past laxity in ad-
ministration and lack of a positive and independent foreign
policy. Finally, many of the ideals of the defeated younger
ofricers, later endorsed by the loyal Army, are embodied in
the statement of policy issued by the new Government on
March 17.*
A parallel can be drawn between these develop-
men+s ana
the actions since September, l031, of the more in-
temperate
Army officers in Manchuria and North China which,
as "faits accomplis", received the endorsement of the Japanese
Government.
Before analyzing the ains of the insurgent officers in
connection witn the success or failure of these aims, it is
pertinent to remark that a large section of the Army and of
the civilian population were and are in sympathy with the
ideals that prompted the February 26 affair, though opposed
to the violent and undisciplined methods adopted. This fact
helps to explain the fundamental success of the insurgent
movement.
The
* Embassy's despatch No. 1733 of March 19, 1936.

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)
50-4
-4-
The motives and plens of the "direct action"group in
and outside the Army are revealed by the actions and state-
ments of this group since the Incident of May 15, 1932, by
the mimeographed statement left by the insurgent soldiers
at newspaper offices on the morning of February 26 last, and
by the official document issued by the War Office on the evening
of February 26. According to the statement of the insurrection-
ists, they desired the fell of the Okada Government because it
had "been drifting away from the true spirit of Japan and had
usurped the prerogatives of the Emperor."
The Okada Govern-
ment fell owing to its responsibility for the Incident. Evi-
dences given for the above charge against the Okada Cabinet
were the signing of the London Naval Treaty, for which Viscount
Admiral Saito was alleged to have been largely responsible,*
and the dismissal of General Masaki as Inspector General of
Military Education on July 16, 1955, for which Saito was also
blamed. Saito was one of the victims of the recent outbreak.
The insurrectionaries also desired the abolition of the system
whereby the senior statesmen offered counsel to the Emperor
and to
the Government without being responsible for their ac-
tions.
In this connection it is interesting to note that the
Hirota Cabinet is reported to be seriously considering abolish-
ment of the Cabinet Deliberative Body, which since its organi-
zation on May 10, 1935, ** had been largely under the influence
of such senior statesmen as Admiral Saito, its President, and
Ir. Takahashi, its Vice President. The official statement of
the War Office issued on the evening of February 26*gave
among the purposes of the uprising the elimination of states-
men

men close to the Throne (Saito was killed, Admiral Suzuki,
Court Chamberlain, was seriously wounded and at any rate
deprived of political influence, and Baron Ikki resigned
from his post of President of the Privy Council on March 11).
Also listed for "elimination", according to the War Office
announcement, were the military clique (General Watanabe,
Inspector General of Military Education of the War Office,
was killed), and bureaucrats (Mr. Takahashi, Finance Minister,
was killed).
A partial motive for the assassination of these
men was the need felt by the younger officers to "clarify
great principles and to protect and make manifest the national
polity." Clarification of the national polity and the ex-
tinction of ideas incompatible with national principles (such
as the Minobe theory) are set forth as the first principles
of the new Governnent's policy in the official statement is-
sued on iarch 17.
Also among the known aims of the radical army element
and of a great part of the Army as well was the establish-
ment of
a kind of state socialism, with the imposition of
higher taxes on capitalists, the reduction of taxes on the
poorer people, and general administrative measures for the
welfare of the masses, particularly in the rural cormnunities
These objectives were touched on in the Army pamphlet of
October 1, 1934, which contained such phrases as:
"So long
as economic profits, and especially unearned incomes, are
enjoyed by one section of the nation alone the majority of
the people will be left in utmost misery and distress......"*
A program of state reform resembling that desired by the
Army is now under consideration by the Hirota Cabinet as
evidenced in general terms in the Hirota Cabinet's policy
statement

statement of March 17.*
After the assassinations of February 26, according to
information received by the Imbassy, the insurgents demanded
of the provisional Government the appointment of either Baron
Hiranuma or Admiral Kanji Kato as Premier.**
While Baron
Hiranuma was not made Premier, he was appointed to the post
of President of the Privy Council on March 13 and formally
installed in office on that day. It will be recalled that
Hiranuma has long been known as a chauvinist, an anti-liberal,
and a favorite of the radical group in the Army; that the ap-
pointment of Baron Ikki as President of the Privy Council on
May 3, 1934, was made in violation of the precedent that the
Vice President of the Council be promoted to the Presidency
upon the resignati on of the former incumbent; and that the
supporters of Baron Hiranuma were greatly annoyed because,
although Vice President of the Council, he had not been ap-
pointed to the Presidency.***
The younger of ficers who engineered the insurrection
wanted larger defense appropriations and a stronger policy
against Soviet Russia. Judging fron current repor ts and the
present Cabinet's statement of policy, an inerease in defense
appropriations seems assured.
As stated above, on March 9 Mr.
Hirota mentioned the necessity of positive and independent re-
adjustment of international relations. Four days later, in a
conversation with me, Mr. Hirota stated that the "positive
diplomacy" frequently mentioned in the press applied exclusive-
ly to Soviet Russia and China and simply meant the expe diting
of his former policy as Minister for Foreign Affairs.****

II. Aims of the Insurgents which Failed.
Prince Saionji, the Genro, escaped assassination and
in fact subsequently recomnended to the Emperor the appoint-
ment of Mr. Hirota as Fremier. However, in choosing a can-
didate for Premier, the Geno avoided consulting the other
elder statesmen near the Tarone, a custom which had been
established by him imnedietely after the May 15 Incident
and which was distasteful to the radical element in the Army.
On February 27 the leaders of the rebellion were reported to
have demanded a military dictatorship for Japan. This demand
was immediately rejected by the Government.*
However, the
establishment of a military dictatorship was not among the
objectives of the insur ents as disclosed by their mimeo-
graphed statement of February 26 and by the official state-
ment of the War Office on that day. The probable reason for
the issuance of this demand was to enable the insurgents to
make a better bargain with the Government in accordance with
the well-known Japanese habit of demanding more than can be
obtained for the purpose of striking e future compromise.
Seven Army members of the Supreme War Council resigned on
March 3, assuming responsibility for the Incident. Among
those who resigned were Generals Masaki and Araki, who had
been an inspiration to the plotters and whose ideas un-
doubtedly influenced the latter. However, General Hayashi,
a member of the military clique which the insurgents wanted
to get rid of, also resigned from the Supreme War Council.
In return for certain concessions mentioned elsewhere in
this despatch, General Terauchi is reported to have agreed
with
* Embassy's telegram No. 41, February 27, 1936.

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
with Mr. Hirota to establish stricter control in the Army.
The disciplinary measures thus far taken by the Army have
already been mentioned. In addition, more than one hundred
civilians are said to have been arrested by the Tokyo gendarm-
erie and the Metropolitan Police for alleged complicity in
the February 26 Incident. If the Amy and the Home Office
continue to take vigorous steps against the "direct actionists"
and if the Government supplements these steps by carrying out
the reforms desired by the Army, the danger of another uprising
in the near future will probably be greatly lessened.
III. Conclusion.
From an exanination of the foregoing considerations it
would appear that the aims of the insurgents which failed are
outweighed by those that succeeded.
In general, the insurgents
wanted a liquidation of the status quo.
The Okada Cabinet,
which had been upheld at tne polls on February 20, gave no in-
dication of any intention to alter its policy or to adopt a
reform progran similar to the one announced by the Hirota Cabi-
net on March 17. While the changes desired by the insurgents
have not all come about, measures toward effecting them gradu-
ally are under definite consideration. It is probably true that
the reforms desired would have had to be adopted in the course
of time owing to widespread popular sympathy for the Army's
ideas. Nevertheless, it can be safely said that the insurrection
of February 26 hastened the break-up of the status quo.
Respectfully yours,
Sosio
Joseph C. Grew.

Supplementing the Embassy's despatch No. 1707 of
March 5, 1936, concerning the trial of Lieutenant-
Colonel Aizawa for the murder of Major-General Nagata,
I have the honor to submit a report on developments
that have come to light since the writing of that des-
patch, with particular reference to the relation between
F/FG
the trial and the Incident of Februery 26.
Recently

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.99)
51-2
-2-
Recently the Enbassy's Military Attaché was informed
by an official of the War Cffice that former Captains Koji
Murenaka and Asaichi Isobe, former Lieutenant Zei Nishida
and Kazuteru Kita (once a "ronin" in China), all of whom
were prominently mentioned in the Aizawa trial as close
associates of the defendant, had been the real ringleaders
in the revolt although the nominal and active leaders had
been Captains Ando and Nonaka.
This information was sub-
stantiated by an announcement of the Mertial Law Head-
quarters on March 11.
According to newspaper reports, Kazuteru Kita was a
Socialist during the early part of his career and later
became corverted to out-and-out nationalism. About 1920
he  ublished a book in Shunghai entitled "Japan's Reconstruction",
which was prohibited from distribution in Japan on account
of its pernicious intermingling of leftist and rightist
ideas and its insistence upon direct action. It is said
that the book, "Tapan's Reconstruction", had a great in-
fluence on the men accused of perpetrating the May 15
assassination. Zei Mishida resigned as a lieutenant in
the Army in 1924 on account of illness and subsequen tly
joined the chauvinist movement, becoming a member of sev-
eral ultra-nationalist societies.
In 1926 he was arrested
in connection with a plot against the life of Count Makino
and others and was sentenced to five months' penal servi-
tude in l930. For the past five years he has been working
closely with Kazuteru Kita.
Furthermore, according to the ASAHI of March 5, ex-
Captain Muranaka and Zensuke Shibukawa, a graduate of the
Military Academy and one of the civilians arrested for par-
ticipation in the February 26 Incident, attended seven
sessions

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)
51-3
-3-
sessions of the Aizawa trial, carefully taking notes, and
on February 17 ceased to appear at. the hearings. The dis-
closures made during the trial may thus have influenced the
cons irators in the choice of the date on which to act and
of the persons to be assassinated.
It was reported by the ASAI of March 12 that Lieuten-
ant-Colonel Aizawa would have to be completely retried be-
cause his trial had already been suspended fifteen days,
the legal limit under the Court Martial Law. DOMEI (News Agency)
stated that no date for a new trial had been set and that it
would be postponed indefinitely. In this connection it is
of interest to note, according to newspaper accounts and to
the Military Attaché's informant mentioned above, that the
trial of the officers and civilians involved in the Febru-
ary 26 Incident will be distinctly different from the trial
of the men involved in the assassination of Premier Inukai
on May 15, 1952, and from the Aizawa trial in that it will be
held strictly in camera, the accused will be denied the bene-
fit of counsel, the decisions civen will be final with no
appeal allowed, and there will be less airing of political
views and more attention paid to establishing the guilt of
and the punishment for the defendants.
A five-day conference of Anmy and Div sion Commanders
is scheduled to begin at Tokyo on April 7. Although such
conferences of Army and Division Commanders are routine
matters and take place from time to time under ordinary
circumstances, the newspapers report that the purpose of
the coming conference is to strengthen Army discipline and
to unify the control of the Amy. It remains to be seen
whether

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.93)
51-4
-4-
whether any extraor dinary measures will be taken at the
scheduled conference.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Nanking's despatch No. 112, March 6,

between H. H. Kung and the Ambassador. Mr.
Kung remarked (1) that Japanese militarists
envisaged a Japanese Empire as far-flung as
the British Empire (2) that through friendly
rather than aggressive methods the Japanese
might have accomplished their "Asia for the
Asiatics" program (3) that the Japanese Navy
desired expansion towards the South Pacific
in opposition to the Army's plan for con-
tinental expansion (4) that Japanese armed
strength would be used to break down tariff
and other barriers against Japan and (5)
that "collective security" would require
814
collective action to curb Japan.

I have the honor to enclose herewith a memorandum of

Executive Yuan and Minister of Finance, in the course of

accompanied by Mr. Peck.
The nature
F/FG

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.98)

52-2

The nature of the business which occasioned the

and regards himself as & member of the "European-American
faction" of Chinese officials, as distinguished from the

that China's position in the future should be that of an
independent nation seeking prosperity on terms of inter-
national equality with Japan and the principal Powers,
rather than that of a nation whose security is guaranteed
by Japan and whose development is achieved through guid-
ance and essistance exclusively Japanese.

Subjeot: Japanese Imperialistio Ambitions.
Dr. H. H. Kung, Minister or Finance and Vice Presldent

The American Ambassador.
Mr. Peok.
In the course of a general conversation Dr. Kung said
that he was some times a little surprised at the lack of
appreciation of the United States and of European Powers of
the serious threat to international peace and security of-
fered by Japanese imperialistic ambitions.
Dr. Kung remarked that in pursuit of these ambitions
China was an instrumentality through which Japanese militar-
ists hoped to acquire the power necessary to extend the
beneficent rule of the Japenese Empertor, not only over Asia,
but actually over the world. Looking upon the far-flung
British Empire, Japanese militarists did not perceive why
the Japanese sun should not shine over an equally extensive
chain of possessions.
If Japan had taken pains from the beginning to cultivate
friendly feelings on the part of the Chinese, the materiali-
zation of their dream might have been perceptibly advanced
by this time. Admitted to friendly cooperatfon, the Chinese
might heve seen something in the idea of "Asia for the
Asiatics". Owing to the realities of Japanese operations
however.

I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's telegram
1935
No. 65 of LMerch 9, 10 p.m., and to report on the events
leeding up to the formation on March 9 of the present
Japanese Cabinet under the premiership of ir. Aoki Hirota.
From the time of the occurrence of the February 26
incident, it seemed generally taken for granted that a
change of Cabinet was in order. To tide over the inter-
F/FG
val necessarily required to create a new cabinet, how-
ever, the former one continued to function.
The

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
The Okada Cabinet (with the exception of Premier
Okada and Finence Minister Takahashi whose deaths had
been officially announced by the /ar Office) tendered
their collective resignation the evening of February
26 but continued in office in accordance with the orders
of the mperor pending the organization of a new cabinet.
An official announcement appearing in the OFFICIAL GAZETTE
extra of February 26 stated that Home Minister Fumio
Goto had been ordered to assume the post of provisionel
acting Premier and the announcement of the assignment
to Commerce and Industry Minister Chuji Machida of the
Finance portfolio appeared in an OrFICIAL GAZETTE extra
of February 27.
Although news of the safety of Premier Okada was not
made public until 4:50 p.m., rebruary 29, the press re-
ported that he called at the Imperial Pelace on February
28 after having earlier the same day submitted his re-
signation to the mperor. An ite dated February 28,
which appeared in the OFFICIAL GAzETTE of February 29,
stated that Home Minister Fumio Goto was relieved of his
additional post as provisional acting Premier.
Thenceforth until the formation of the Hirota Cabinet
on March 9, the Okada Cabinet continued to function with
its personnel unchanged(with the exception of the loss of
Finance Minister Takanashi and the assumption of his port-
folio by the Minister of Co merce and Industry).
The Imperial conmand of March 4 to Prince Konoye
to form a new cabinet, which he declined on grounds of
ill-health, followed by a smiliar command on march 5 to
Foreign Minister Hirota have been previously reported. *

"The Cebinet which is to be organized to liquidate the

Despatch No. 1733 from the Embassy at
Tokyo discusses in considerable detail the
statement of policy issued by the Hirota
cabinet and gives an interpretation of the
aims of the new Japanese government as set
forth in that rather vague document. It is
suggested that you may wish to read this
interesting despatch, which was prepared by
Mr. Dickover. However, the following
summary outlines the Embassy's comments and
conclusions.
The statement of policy by the Hirota
government corresponds in the main with the
demands made by the army as conditions pre-
cedent to army cooperation with the new
cabinet. These conditions w ere (1) the
stamping out of internationalism and
liberalism in Japan, (2) strengthening of
Jananese military fcrces, (3) remedial
economic measures, and (4) the adoption
of a "positive" foreign policy directed
presumably tcward China and the U.S.S.R.
Until concrete plans have been drawn
up by the government it will be difficult
to estimate the manner in which these
policies will be carried cut. However, the
statement indicates that certain definite
trends may be predicted. The new govern-
ment

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
ment will be more nationalistic than
previous governments; it will endeavor
to correct sone of the maladjustments in
the economic, political and social life of
the nation, but it will undertake no hasty
or radical reforms.
It anpears that most of the emphasis
in the statement of policy is placed upon
the necessity for reforms in the economic
life of the nation: chenges in the tax
systen to effect a more equitable distri-
bution of wealth, state control of key
industries and provisions for unemployment
and cld age insurance anpear to be under
consideration. These predicted measures are
causing considerable worry in Japanese
business circles.
r. Hirota has repeatedly declered that
the foreign policy of the new government
will not differ in any essential respect
from that previously followed by Mr. Hirota.
The new "positive" fcreign policy applies
only to the U.S.S.R. and China and will be
"positive" only in that it will mean the
speeding up of policy already enunciated
and followed. In regard to the United
States Mr. Hirota has again declared that
good relations with the United States would
be the "corner stone" of his nolicy even
more than before.

The public in Japan has greeted the
statement of nolicy with no great enthusiasm.
The public attitude seems to be that the

will of the government to give effect to
its declarations.

I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's telegram
61L
No. 75 March 18, 11 a.m., concerning the statement of
FILED
1/
policy issued by the Mirota Cabinet late on March 17, and
1936
to enclose herewith a clipping from the JAPAN TIMES AND
MAIL of March 18, 1936, containing the text of the state-
ment, as translated by Domei. The translation has been
checked by the mbassy and found to be substantially correct,
except that, where Domei translated the characters referring
to

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
to the foreign policy as "a consistent and coherent
positive foreign policy", the Embassy believes that a
better translation would be "an independent and positive
foreign policy". This translation has, therefore, been
used throughout this despatch.
Basis of the Policy.
When the Hirota Cabinet was in process of formation,
the Japanese newspapers reported that the Army authorities
had agreed to cooperate with r. Hirota in the formation
of the new Government if the latter would accept and en-
deavor to execute the four basic desires of the military,
as follows:
1. Clarification of the national polity. This refers
to the so-called "inobe" or"Emperor-organ" theory, to which
the Army is bitterly opposed, and to the stamping out of
internationalism and liberalism in Japan.
2. Repletion of nationel defense. This refers to
the Army's desire for a better cir force, capable of en-
gaging the Soviet air force in the Far ast: for the com-
pletion of the mechanization of the Army; and for the
strengthening of the Japanese forces in "Manchukuo" and at
home.
3. Stabilization of the livelihood of the people.
This refers to remedies for some of the economic and political
maledjustments in Japen; to state control of industries and
finances; and to readjustment of taxation.
4. Reformation of diplomacy and the adoption of a
"positive" foreign policy. This refers primarily to the
Army's

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3-
Army's dissatisfaction with the alleged "weak-kneed"
diplomatic methods employed in dealing hitherto with
China and the Soviet Union.
A clipping from the OSAKA MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI
NICHI, English edition, of march 15, 1936, giving the
2/
views of the military in greater detail, is enclosed.
Upon examining the statement of policy as issued
by Premier Mirota on arch 17, it will be found that
the platform corresponds in the main with the demands
of the Army. In regard to the first demand of the
Army, for the clarificetion of the national polity
issue, the statement of policy declares
"Therefore, it is the first duty of the
Gover ment to clarify the concept of our
immutable national polity(the solidarity
of all the subjects under one Sovereign)
at the seme time eradicating
thoughts incompatible with our national
polity."
The statement of policy has little to say on the
subject of the strengthening of national defense, ex-
cept to state that
"The Government intend to . . . provide
fully for national defense".
The statement also connects the foreigr policy and
national defense vith the national policy of culti-
vating cordial relations with all countries and of
carryins out Janan's mission as the stabilizing force
in East Asie. This is probably a reflection of Mr.
Hirota's reported theory thet a nation's diplomacy
is

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
is only an expression of the strength, political, eco-
nomic, cultural and especially military, of the state,
and that the silent power of the actual strength of a
state determines the success or failure of a diplomet's
efforts.
In regard to the Army's demand for stabilization
of the life of the people, Mr. Hirota was somewhat more
explicit:
"It is the urgent necessity of the moment
to enrich the basic sources of our national
strength by expending trade and industry
and also by meking such financial end economic
improvements as reforms in taxation and banking
systems in conformity with the progress of the
country. Of late, unhealthy social trends have
multiplied, exerting a heavy pressure on the
people's livelihood, and producing conflicts
of interests in all uarters."
The Army's fourth demand was for the reformation of
diplomacy ana the adoption of a "positive" foreign policy.
In the statement of policy Mr. Hirota lists the main points
of Japan's foreign policy, namely, the cultivation of
cordial relations with all countries, the promotion of
mutual well-being and common prosperity between Japan
and other coutries in ast Asia, Japan's mission as the
stabilizing force in ast Asia, and the inseparable re-
lationship between Japan and "Manchukuo". In connection
with these, r. nirota stated that
"The Government intend to inaugurate an independent
and positive foreign policy and at the same time to
provide fully for national defense."
As can be seen from the above, there can be little
doubt that the statement of policy issued by the Hirota
Cabinet was inspired by the military, or at least drawn
up in accordance with the wishes of the military in order
to

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
to reach a modus operandi which would permit the Cabinet
to remain in pover.
It cannot be assumed, however, that
r. Hirota and others of the Ministers of State are in
entire agreement with the wishes of the military. That
they have agreed with the wishes of the Army sufficiently
to issue a statement of policy in accordance with the
demands of the military indicetes only that the civil
government has adopted the only way out of a most diffi-
cult situation - e situation which would possibly have
led to radical changes in the Government of Japan had
not a workable compromise been reached. Moreover, it
should not be assumed that the civil government has
allowed itself to be dictated to by the handful of
soldiers who staged the abortive insurrection of Febru-
ary 26-29. The views held by the insurgents were in
fact the views held b
a very lerge part of the military
and by a considerable section of the civil population es
well.
The bulk of the military and practically all of the
civil ponulation disagreed with the methods
adopted by
the insurgent troops on February 26, but there undoubtedly
was and is a great deel of sympathy for the views
of the
insurgents. The civil government, faced with this wide-
spread sentiment, had no choice but to reach a compromise
with the military, in the hope of carrying out necessary
reforms by degrees and thereby avoiding the violent upset
in the life of the nation which would have been caused by
a more radical form of government.
Analysis of the Statement of Policy.
Until the various Ministries concerned have produced
their concrete plans for the execution of the policies
enunciated

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6-
enunciated by the Cabinet as a whole, it will be diffi-
cult to meke a precise evaluation of the trends indi-
cated by the statement of policy, but a study of the
document cleerly shows certain definite ideas which
may or may not be fully carried out in the future.
As is stated in the document, the Cabinet believes
that its first duty is to clarify the concept of the
national polity, which is defined as the unity of the
nation under the Emperor. The Japenese nation hes
always been renowned for its singular unity and for
its loyalty to its Sovereign, but apparently the
present loyalty end unity does not satisfy the mili-
tary and others of like mind. Therefore educetional
reforms are to be instituted and thoughts(liberelism,
socialism, and even such mild divergences from accepted
thought
the "Minobe Theory") incompatible with the
Jepanese national polity are to be eradicated. It will
be observed, however, that this is to be done "to main-
tain the sanctity of the Constitution and the laws of
the country", indicating that any changes which will
be made will not be radical but will be within the
framework of the present Constitution. It does not
augur well for the future intellectual progress of
Japan, however, if the minds of the people are to be
confined within the mentel strait-jacket of strict
national orthodoxy.
The plans for the stabilization of the livelihood
of the people are causing the greatest pertubation
among

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
among the financiers, industrialists and traders. No
student of Japanese economy can doubt the advisability
of making adjustments of the distribution of wealth
and the incidence of taxation, which at present is
bearing too heavily upon the agriculturist and small
business man and too lightly upon the great financier
and industrialist. Some changes will undoubtedly be
made, but the extent of the changes and their effect
upon the economic life of the nation are causing con-
siderable worry, which is reflected in the steady falls
on the Japanese stock exchange. It is confidently
expected that there will be changes in the taxation
system, whereby the monied class, the financiers
and the industrialists will have to bear a larger share
of the burden of taxation, but it is not known whether
or not these cnanges will be greet enough to cause a
serious upheaval in the economic life of the nation.
As a prominent financier of Osaka stated to a member of
the staff of the Embassy, We can only hope that they
will not soak us too hard". The "reform in the banking
system" it is believed refers to efforts which will be
made to grent easier money to small industrialists and
traders. Although it is not so declared in the statement
of policy, it is renorted that another nlank in the Cebi-
net's platform consists of state control of certain key
industries, notably the generetion and distribution of
electric power, the making of iron and steel, the refining
and

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
and distribution of petroleum products and the manu-
facture of automobiles. This state control, however,
is partly connected with the development of industry and
trade and partly connected with national defense. It
is also believed that systems of health, disablement,
unemployment end old-age insurance will be instituted
by the state, if the present Cabinet remains in office
for a sufficient length of time to formulate suitable
plans.
The above reported plans of the Cabinet point to
the inauguretion of a period of "controlled economy
in Japan. To a certain extent there will also be a trend
toward state socialism, which, as has been exolained
in previous desnatches from the Embassy, is curiously
interwoven with extreme reactionism in the minds of the
Japanese militery - largely, it is believed, because of
their hatred of the modern capitalist class.
As is stated in the declaration of policies of the
Hirota Cabinet, the new Government intends to adopt an
independent and positive foreign policy". No definite
announcement of the details of this policy has yet been
made and probably none will be made, but as r. Hirota
himself assured me on March 13, the foreign policy of
the new Government will not differ in any essential .
regard from that followed by Mr. Hirota when he occu-
nied only the post of Minister for Foreign Affairs.
The new "positive" foreign policy applies only to
Soviet Russia and China, and will be "positive" only in
that it will mean the speeding up of policies already
enunciated and followed. In regard to Soviet Russia,
the

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
the policy of Mr. Hirota is to avoid war by the peaceful
settlement of incidents as they erise and the Army's poliey
is to avoid the possibility of danger from the Soviet
troops by augmenting the Japanese forces in Manchuria.
In regard to China, Mr. rirota will endeavor to adjust
relations on the basis of the three-point program, namely,
the cessation of anti-Jepanese propaganda and activities,
the recognition of the existence of "vianchukuo" in so far
as factual relations ere concerned, but not necessarily
reaching to de jure recognition, and Sino-Japanese co-
operation in combating conmunism. It is generally be-
lieved, however, both in Japan and in China, that ir.
Hirota, having been compelled by force of circumstances
to relinquish his former "conciliation diplomacy" and
to adopt "independent and positive diplomacy", will in the
future take a more determined attitude in forcing the
three points upon China. Toward the Western countries,
it is believed that Mr. Hirote will continue his former
policy of friendship and conciliation. In my recent
conversation with the Prime Minister he reminded me of
his statement at our first meeting in 1933 that good re-
lations with the United States would be the "cornerstone"
of his policy and he seid that now, even more than before
this policy would "prevail".
Little is as yet known as to the details of the agreement
which Mr. Hirota reached with the military when he pro-
mised to "provide fully for national defense", but NIPPON
DEMPo reported that the Army asked for additional appro-
priations amounting to two billion yen, to be spent over
the

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
the period from 1937 to 1942 and to be used for the
expansion of the eir force, the completion of the
mechanization of the Army, the addition of several
divisions to the Army, and the arranging of mobilization
of industries in war-time. The funds for this pro-
gram are to be obtained by the issuance of more "red-ink
bonds (the Takahashi policy of gradual reduction of
such honds having apparently been abandoned with his
death) and by the increase of revenue obtained by taxa-
tion reform. The weight of this enormous program of
armaments expansion is enother factor causing worry
to the capitalists and industrialists of Japan.
In the statement of policy of the lirota Cabinet,
it will be noted that there is mention of "reforms
in administrative structure". It is assumed that
this refers to the Army's desire for the abolition of
certein governmentel departments and the creation of
new departments. It is understood that the Army wishes
to combine the present Ministries of Railways and of
Communications into one; to combine the present Minis-
tries of Conmerce and Industry and of Agriculture and
Forestry into one to be known as the Ministry of In-
dustry; to abolish the Ministry of Overseas Affairs,
whose duties would be taken over by a Bureau under
direct control of the Cabinet; to abolish the Cabinet
Inquiry Council and to enlarge the Cabinet Inquiry
Bureau; to combine the air forces of the Army and Navy
under an independent Ministry of Air, which will also
control

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
control civilian aviation; and perhaps to organize
a Ministry of ropagande.
It is believed that it can sefely be essumed that
the new Government will be more nationalistic than
previous Governments; thet it will endeavor to correct
some of the maladjustments in the economic, political
and sociel life of the nation; but thet it will under-
take no hesty, ill-considered or radical reforms(it will
be noted that in the concluding paragraph of the stete-
ment, r. irota declares that "hile over-hasty actions
will be avoided through the wisdom of the people and a
thorough understanding of the public sentiment, the
Government will take resolute steps in order to achieve
what it believes to be right"). At the same time, it
can be expected that, for some time to come and until
the inevitable reection sets in, the intense nationalist
point of view will have an even greater influence in
governnental affairs than in the past.
Official and Public Opinion of the Cabinet and its
Policics.
The designation of ir. hirota as Premier was well
received by the Japanese nation as a whole, and the Army
was criticized quietly for its opposition to the Hirota
Cabinet as originally conceived. Although a very con-
siderable section of the nation is in sympathy with the
aims of the insurgents, it was generally felt that the
Army hed brought disgrace upon Japen by the methods
employed in the insurrection of February 26 and, under
the circumstances, that the proper role of the Army
was

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
- 12 -
was to keep in the background as far es possible.
The JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL, in its issue of Maroh 7,
rather boldly and humorously explained the situation
as follows:
"His Majesty the Emperor commanded Mr. Koki
Hirota to form his Cabinet, but it seems
that the Army is insisting to select the
personnel of the Hirota Cabinet and decide
its policy. The Miyako Shimbun says that
in interfering with the plan of Mr. Hirota
to form his Cabinet, the Army is disre-
garding the Imperial Command given to the
Foreign Minister. According to the state-
ment issued by Count General Juichi Tera-
uchi, the Army objects to the liberalistic
color of the persons already selected by
r. Hirota. Liberalism is defined by a
dictionary "broad, popular, progressive,
inclined to democratic or republican ideas".
Thus what the Army desires may be said to
be something "narrow, unpopular, unpro-
gressive, and undemocratic."
The Army, however, not without considerable support
from other elements both in and out of the Govern-
ment, proceeded to dictate to ir. Hirota the personnel,
in part at least, of his Cebinet, with the natural
result that the Cebinet, as eventually formed, was
regarded as being the result of a compromise with
a section of the nation temporarily in disgrace,
and consequently was received with some misgivings.
The ASAHI congratulated Mr. Hirota upon his
success in forming a Cabinet but aptly remarked that,
had Mr. Hirota not listened to the wishes of the
military, his Government could not have been stable.
The ROUn IN thought that as the Army was the center
of the recent disturbances, it was also the back-
bone of the new Cabinet. Therefore it doubted the
strength

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
-13 -
strength of the Cabinet. The NICHI NICHI considered
the new Cabinet as strong in that it had the backing of
the Army and weak in that in itself it lacked the strength
to oppose the Army. Consequently the newspaper believed
that the Cabinet's policy would be "safety first". The
YOMIURI thought that the new Cabinet was not exactly the
type desired by the nation, but considering that it was
formed under the circumstences of stress and haste,
it wes a notable accomplishment. There was not much hope
for the future of the abinet, however, as it lacked
independence, being dependent upon the Army, end there-
fore lacked the strength to pursue strong policies.
The JIJI declared that r. Hirota had done his best in
forming the abinet and that now the duty of the nation
is to refrain from obstructing him in his difficult
task. The newspaper added that the Army's duty, now that
its wishes were in the main granted, was to re-establish
strict military discipline. The CHUGAI SHOoYO stated that
the fact that the new C binet nad to make concessions
to the Army added to its responsibility to the nation.
-.ewspaper opinion on the whole, therefore, vas not unkind,
but was certainly not hopeful of the success of the new
Government. It should be understood, however, that any
new Government in Japan is apt to be greeted with jeers
and complaints.
The statement of policies of the Hirota Cabinet was
eagerly awaited by all classes in Japan, as it was felt,
in view of the circumstances under which the Cabinet was
born

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
born, that it would be necessary for the Cabinet to
enunciate concrete policies designed to correct the
faults in the nationel structure which had brought
about the disturbances. The military and reectionary
elements, as vell as a considerable section of the
great mass of the people, eagerly hoped for some
expression of projected reforms in the political
end economic structure, vwhile the capitalists
fesrfully hoped that the innovations would not be
as bad as it seemed they might be.
The vague
and abstract statement of policy which finally ap-
peared late on march 17 therefore was a surprise to
most of the population. But, as wes aptly remarked
by one nevsnaper, if the statement was vague, it there-
fore lent itself to compromises.
According to IPPON DElPo, the military end naval
authorities were not entirely satisfied with the Hirota
statement of policy. They felt that "such stetements,
hovever nicely worded, are valueless unless those re-
sponsible for them are sincerely desirous of living
up to them". "A platform is important only when
there is a will to carry it out". "The statement
just issued merely indicates the administrative prin-
cinles of the Government. Whether we get gold or dross
depends upon the attitude of the Government toward the
statement". "We shall rely upon what the Premier and
other Ministers said at the Cabinet meeting and await
results."

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
-15 -
results." "The Navy will watch the Government". These
seem to be rather thinly veiled threats against the new
Cabinet, implying that if they do not carry out the promises
anparently made to the military, something unspecified will
happen.
The newspapers in general also denounced the statement
of policy as being too abstrect and not filling the needs
of the nation at the moment. The ASAHI stated that the
announcement did not contain eny concrete manifestations
of the policies of the Government, but excused the Cabinet
because of the haste in which it was formed, which pre-
vented the formulation of concrete policies. The JIJI
criticized the statement as being too vague and lacking
concrete policies, especially in regard to reforms in the
Army and to economic and financial questions. The CHUGAI
SfOGYo stated that the public expected the Government to

"In the present ennouncement we find, not the vigor of

different sources". The TOKYO NICHI MICHI stated that
the announcement of policy clearly showed traces of com-

but added that it did not object to compromise and was
interested in, not the letter of the abstract statement,

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
RECEIVED
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
APR 13 1930
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
March 28, 1936.
APR 1 1 1936
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
U:
The Tokyo Inoident
Department of State
Mr. Phillips.
of February 26.
Tokyo's despatch No. 1718 of March 6 gives an analysis
of the factors behind the recent outbreak in Tokyo. Men-
tioned as basic causes are mental unrest and economic
594.00
maladjustment brought on by transition of the country
from an agricultural to an industrial economy; tension
created by conflict of political ideas in regard to the
894.00/640
position of the Emperor and in regard to the role of the
army in the State; dissatisfaction over foreign policy
particularly with regard to China; and indiscipline in
the Japanese army attributable to fanatical conceptions
of duty.
Immediate causes of the outbreak are stated to be the
outcome of the recent elections which favordthe government,
believed by the military to be weak and corrupt; the Aizawa
"political trial"; and the order for transfer to Manchuria
of the disaffected regiment.
The writer of the despatch, Mr. Dickover, predicts
that in consequence of the outbreak efforts will be made
to instill a better sense of discipline in the army; that
attempts will be made to organize a Japanese "new deal" and
to correct the economic maladjustments which lie at the
root of much of the present unrest; and that there may occur
F
a swing in popular feeling away from reactionary ideas toward
F
G
the left.
It

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
It is believed that the foregoing suffices to cover
the main points in the despatch. It is suggested that you
may wish to read the memorandum appended to the despatch
of a conversation with Dr. James A. Scherer, who is on
friendly terms with General Masaki. Masaki is commonly
said to be the leader of the young officer faction in the
army which has been responsible for most of the violent
demonstrations in recent years. Scherer's views appear
colored by his association with the General and some of
his assertions are obviously unsound, but they make
interesting reading. He is, as you will recall, the author
of two recent books on Japan which have brought him some-
thing of the reputation of an apologist for Japan.
2
There is also appended Tokyo's despatch of March 6,
No. 172l, which gives a detailed chronicle of the chief
events connected with the recent uprising in Tokyo. Most
of the information contained therein has already been
reported by telegraph. You may wish to glance through
this report.

Imperial. sanction for the victim's pro-

Worried Over Conditions
The defendant then stated that he
canvassed for the appointment of a
person like Colonel Kanji Ishihara to
be the reg:r ntal conmander of the
students of the Military Academy. and
also told how he engaged himself in
the education of the public in the Hiro-
shima Prefeeture. although it may have
been in a very small way. Continuing
he said:
"From that time on I was so con-
cerned of the conditions that I could
hardly cat my meals. I requested the
cormander of my regiment to let me
go to Tokyo while my unit was en-
gaged in a sham battle. The permis-
sior. was g:ven, and with it a warning
that I should act with great care.
The defendant stated he left for To-
kyo by an afternoon express of July
17. On his way. he called on Captain
Yoriyohi Okishi at Wakayama and
spent a night w:th him.
"While in the train. I thought about
the ptriots of the Meiji Restoration."
he went on. "Then I would think
that my power alone would not prove
suffielent. Then I would encowrage
myself by thinking that such a weak

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 23, 1936.
EHD:
JOH:
SKH
The Embassy's despatch No. 1758 of
April 1, 1936, prepared jointly by Mr. Grew
and Mr. Dickover, attempts to interpret the
trends of the new government in Tokyo in
the light of statements by and actions of
members of the cabinet.
The Hirota cabinet is definitely com-
mitted to a program of reform of the
economic maladjustments and political abuses
in Japan. As yet it is too early to judge
precisely the form and extent which the
promised reforms will take.
The despatch quotes from statements
made by several members of the cabinet,
and describes a number of bills which the
government is reported to be preparing for
introduction in the Diet. None of these
statements and measures indicate that there
is contemplated any radical change in the
economic structure or in the form of
government. The Embassy does not believe
that the measures proposed by the government
will offer any very drastic variation from
the accepted system and practice in Japan.
However, one may look for changes in
two main directions: the first toward
strengthening

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
strengthening national defense, through
stricter discipline in the army; through
"thought education" of the people; and
through government control of basic in-
dustries. The second direction in which
changes may be expected is toward economic
reforms through adjustments in the tax
system; through government control and
encouragement of industry; through tariff
revision; through revision of the land and
tenancy laws; and through various agrarian
measures designed to alleviate the plight
of the farmers.
The Embassy points to its earlier
prediction that the events of February
might result in a revulsion of feeling
against the military, and states that
while a public revulsion against the army
as a whole is hardly to be expected, there
is certainly a marked tendency to decry the
outlook and methods of the more radical
elements in the army. Disapproval of these
elements has been manifested not only by
the public but also by the army and navy.
Measures taken by the Ministers of War and
Navy have demonstrated a determination to
rid the forces of disquieting elements.
I suggest that you glance through the
appended editorial article from the JAPAN
TIMES, written probably by Dr. Ashida, a
prominent publicist and former member of
Parliament. It is more informative than
the despatch.
N7
WTT/DLY

Heferring to the Embessy's despatch No. 1733,
dated March 19, 1936, on the subject of the policies
1536
of the new Government and public opinion in regard
thereto, I believe that the development of the poli-
cies of the Hirote Cabinet, which is definitely com-
mitted to a program of reform of the economic malad-
justments and political abuses in Japan, will be of
great importance in determining future trends in this
F/FG
country

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
country and I shall therefore endeavor to keep the
Department currently apprised of such developments.
On Merch 26, 1936, the prefectural governors were
called to Tokyo to receive their general instructions
from the new Cabinet. he speeches made on this occasion
by the Premier, the Minister for Finance, the Home Linister,
the Minister for Justice and the War Minister serve to
elucidete in some smell measure the general statement
of policy issued on -arch 17 and reported in despatch
o. 1733.
In the opening portion of his speech, Premier Hirota
stated, according to the TOKYO NICHI NICHI:
"It is not en easy tesk to overcome the dif-
ficulties of the prevailing netional exigency.
The fact that this denlorable incident has
taken place unexpectedly makes us realize that
in the present situation we are confronted with
many dirficulties and that the causes thereof are
very remote and deep-rooted."
Mr. Hirota then reiterated the statement of policy issued on
march 17, and stated that the Goverment would devise prac-
ticel reform schemes, to be graduelly put into execution.
He urged the need for stricter official discipline and
exhorted all governmental officials to carry out their
duties honestly and conscientiously. ne asked for complete
cooperation between the centrel end local authorities in
carrying out the necessary reforms and requested the Govern-
ors to explain the reforms to the people, in order that
national solidarity might be atteined.
Mr. Hayashi, the Minister for Justice, urged his
audience to eradicate thoughts encouraging direct action
in disregard of the law, whatever may be the motives in-
spiring such direct action. He pointed out that, under a
constitutional

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
constitutional form of gover.ment, political demands
should be fulfilled through the netional assembly and
other constitutional organs. He said that a spirit of
obedience to the law must be cultivated among the people,
in order to avoid crimes caused by direct action methods.
He urged close cooperation among the agencies concerned
for the purpose of ma ntaining public peace end order,
stated that the local officials must closely watch for
signs of communism, and urged the elimination of the
unrest following the incident of February 26 by the con-
trol of newspapers and other literature likely to mis-
guide the public. e stated thet as the methods of cri-
minel action are developing constantly and threaten
to become international in their scope, the Depertment
of Justice intends to enforce new preventive measures
in the near future. e also urged the further control
of the angs of bullies who cause much of the social un-
rest. (The reference here was to the angs of bullies
for hire, who commit many of their crimes under the guise
of patriotism.)
The Minister For Finance, r. Baba, in his speech
before the Governors, stated that it wes inevitable that
nationel exnenditures be increased, end that in oräer to
obtain the additional revenue necessary to meet the in-
creesed expenditures, it would be necessary to promote
industry and trede and to enhance the national prosperity.
He seid that it was urgently necessery to readjust end im-
prove the system of taxation, in order to ecualize the
burden of texation and to increase the revenue of the state.
However,

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4 -
However, he said, as the national finances are based
on the national economic strength, it will be imperative
to avoid the destruction of the economic power of the
people which might be csused by sudden changes in the
.economic system. He further said thet it would be ne-
cessary to adopt en adequate currency policy and a low
money rete policy, in order to promote the adjustment
of the debts of the agricultural and fishing classes.
Home Minister Ushio, in his address to the Governors,
seia that the local officials should endeavor to suppress
movements which run counter to the netional polity and
which violate the law, but tiey should tolerate move-
ments which are pure in motive and moderate in method.
The officials should also exercise stricter control over
dangerous rumors and literature designed to cause un-
rest among the people and should encourage sobriety and
sincerity.
They should endeavor to stabilize and im-
prove the national life ana to har onize conflicting
interests in the nation. ie then said that the Ministry
of lome Affairs was consiuering measures for local taxa-
tion reforms, for enforcement of a more definite social
policy, and for the encouracement of enterprises which
will increase the economic strength of the people, es-
pecially in the rural districts.
The address of General Terauchi, Minister for War,
L1
was perhaps the most interesting. He referred first
to a growing teneency emong the people to question their
duty

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
duty in regard to conscription and to exhibit an unwilling-
ness to support the Army at all times. He asked the Govern-
ors to cooperate with the military in eradicating these
undesirable tenuencies and in restoring perfect harmony
between the military and the citizenry. He also stated
that, although the insurrection of February 26 wes carried
out by a handful of young officers instigated by radical
elements outside the Army, there vere rumors that the in-
surgents were supported by high Army officers or even by
the whole Army. Such rumors, criticizing the Army and
tending to estrange the public from the Army, should be
suppressed. The local officials should also cooperate
with the Army in eliminating the lawless elements which
are instigating the young officers to direct action, and
in suppressing the "mysterious documents" which add to the
unrest in the Army. (The reference here is to certain
secretly-printed pamphlets, criticizing the Government
and the administration of the Army itself, which have
been circulated among rmy officers.)
The War Minister
also asked the Governors to assist in strengthening
the netional conception of the conscription system.
(This is the first time, as far as the Embassy is aware,
that a high Army officer has publicly stated that there
is growing opposition in Janan to the conscription system.)
It will be ooserved that throughout most of these ad-
äresses there runs a thread of similar thought, namely,
the sumpression of radical ideas, whether coming from the
ultra-netionalist or the socialist side, which tend to
create unrest and to cause incidents such as that of
February 26.

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.97)
- 6 -
八
February 26. This inaicates what will probably be the
basic activity of the new Gover ment, namely, the stricter
control of the rightist element in the population(the
leftist element already being pretty thoroughly suppressed),
at the seme time endeavoring gredually to eliminate the
basic maladjustments which give this element some excuse
for violent action.
The uestion arises, however, if
driving this element underground will not cause it to
become even more dancerous to the State than it is at pre-
sent.
The new legisletion hich vill be introduced into the
special session of the iet, Which vill be convened for
three weeks rrom ay 1, vill lso serve to give an indi-
cation of the concrete policies of the new Cabinet.
While
the bills have not yet been considered by the Cabinet as a
whole end it cannot therefore be stated definitely that
they vill be introduced into the iet, the newspapers
report thet the following legislation has been arafted
and submitted to the chief Secretary of the Cabinet for
the consideratior of the Cabinet:
By the Jabinet itself:
A bill for founding an electric
power company to assist in the industrialization of the
Tohoku district and a bill providing for the establisiment
of the Tohoku Development Company(the Tohoku, or North-
eastern, district of Japan is chronically afflicted with
femines and other catastrophes, and there has been an
urgent demand for the relief of the people of the district).
By the Finance Ministry:
A bill for partial revision
of the income tax law; e bill for revision of the import
tariff; e bill concerning the "red-ink" bond issues; a bill
for

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
for investigation of land rents; and a bill for the re-
vision of the law for the encouragement of the iron in-
dustry.
By the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry: A bill
for autonomous rice control; a bill for revision of the
existing rice control law; a bill to encourage the storage
of unhulled rice; & bill revising the existing cocoon
disposal control lew; a bill revising the silk guild law;
a bill revising the law for the encouragement of horse-
breeding through horse-racing; a bill revising the farm
debt readjustment guild law; and a bill revising the fer-
tilizer control lew.
By the Ministry of Commerce and Industry:
A bill
concerning the Central Sank for Commercial and Industrial
Guilds; a bill for revising the existing law for the control
of ey inuustries; a bill for revising the existing Petroleum
Industry Law;ana a bill for encouraging the domestic manu-
facture of cutomobiles.
By the Ministry of -ducation: A bill for state indemni-
fication of educational expenditures for young men.
It will be observed that, although none of these bills
indicates eny startling alteration in the form or procedure
of goverrment, they do indicate a start toward the correction
of defects in the present financial and economic structure and
toward the further governmentel control of key industries,
thus tending to implement Lir. firota's policy of graduel
reform of the conditions causing sociel unrest in Japan and
also imolementing the Army's policy of strengthening nationel
defense through governmental control of certain basic in-
dustries. How radical the changes provided for by these bills
are

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
are to be will not be knowm until they have been presented
to the Diet, but in view of Mr. Hirota's declared policy
of "making haste slowly" in regard to reforms in the present
political and economic structures, and in view of the fact
that the bills have probably been arafted by and represent
to a certain extent the ideas of the permenent under-officials
of the Ministries concerned, it is not believed that they
will offer any very drastic variations from the accented
governmentel system and practice in Jepan.
There is enclosed a clipping from the JAPAN TI.ES AND

and thet the struggle between liberalism and ultra-national-

place anong factions in the Army itself.

=== Page 124 === (confidence 1.00)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
May 8, 1936.
EHD:
The Embassy's No. 1784 of Apri1 17
discusses developments following the
February 26 incident.
As yet it is not easy to determine
from the welter of opinions and suggestions
advanced from various quarters the course
to be followed by the Japanese Government.
However, the Embassy states that the follow-
ing may be safely predicted:
Despite military opposition to the
political parties it appears that no
attempt will be made to abolish the system
of partisan politics. Political parties
are recognized by the most powerful states-
men of Japan to be necessary to the proper
functioning of constitutional government.
There are indications that the House
of Peers is to be reformed. The proposed
reform would make of the House of Peers
a sort of congress of representatives of
vocational groups, and would be composed
of representatives of various strata of
Japanese society rather than a congress
of peers, high taxpayers, and intellectuals,
as at present.
It

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
It appears that the Cabinet Inquiry
Council, which was brought into being by
the Okada Cabinet in order to give it the
appearance of a truly national government,
is to be abolished. It is reported that
the functions of the Cabinet Inquiry
Council will be taken over in part by 
Cabinet ministers without portfolio who
would be entrusted with the formulation
of policies. This plan is only tentative
and may not be carried out.
Disciplinary action within the army
affected twelve outstanding officers of
high rank. In addition six full generals
were placed on the reserve list. Action
taken against these officers will pre-
sumably impress on the rank and file of
the army the serious harm to the military
organization which an ill-considered act
of theirs can do.
The Embassy comments: "There seems
to be little doubt that, as a result of the
incident of February 26, the moderate elements
in the Army have obtained control of that
organization...
This rise of the moderate
element does not imply that the Army has
less influence than previously in the
councils of state . . . or that it is less
determined than previously in regard to
its policy on the Asiatic continent."
The

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
The navy likewise, although not
implicated in the February 26 event, has
embarked on a policy of reformation. A
number of high officers who are felt to
be trouble makers have been transferred
or retired.
Various economic reforms are promised
by the new government, but it appears that
they will be effected in a slow and
cautious manner.
It is believed that this summary
covers the main points of the despatch,
which need not be reed unless you have
some extra time.

Department of State

The Honorable
COPIES SENT TO
