Secretary of State,
CLVIS AO
Washington.
r:
the incident of February 26, 1936, promises to
bring far-reaching alterations in the economic and social
MAY 11
FID
struoture of the Japanese State, to the end that the deep
15.1936
seated causes leading to that incident and previous inci-
dents may be eliminated, the Embassy will endeavor to folfow
developments and to report periodically to the Department
MAY 15 1936
FILED
such developments as appear to indicate probable future
trends in the Japanese body politic. At the present time
there

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
there is a welter of opinions and suggestions, emanating
from political parties, the Army and Navy, statesmen,
industrialists and financiers, in regard to possible
changes and reforms, but as yet there have been few con-
crete steps taken which would serve to indicate the path
of future progress in the reorganization of the nation.
Such as have appeared, however, are described in the fol-
lowing pages.
Political(Domestic)
Although the military have been opposed to the poli-
tical parties for some years, principally because of the
corruption in the parties and the assumption on the part
of some of the more radical militarists that the political
parties are usurping the authority of the Emperor, it
now appears that no attempt will be made to abolish the
system of partisan politics - a step which appeared possi.
ble when the military endeavored to assume a dominant
position in the formation of the Hirota Cabinet. It will
be remembered that during the negotiations between Mr.
Hirota and the Army prior to the formation of the Cabinet,
the Army was reported to have demanded that the representa-
tion of partisan politicians in the Cabinet be limited
to one member each from the Seiyukai and the Minseito.
Mr. Hirota won this battle, and obtained two members of
the Cabinet from each of the two large political parties.
Later the question of the appointment of Parliamentary
Vice-Ministers and Counsellors for each Ministry came into
the limelight. The Army and Navy objected to the appoint-
ment of any Parliamentary Vice Ministers and Counselors,
but

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
but the political parties insisted that they were necessary
for cooperation between the executive and legislative
branches of the Government, and eventually won their point,
with the result that of the twenty-two such officials ap-
pointed on April 14, eight came each from the Seiyukai and
Minseito, five from the House of Peers, and one from the
Showakai. The Army remained firm, however, and refused
to accept a party man as Parliamentary Vice Minister, al-
though it did accept a member of the House of Peers,
but refused to accept any Parliamentary Counselor whatever.
The Navy accepted a party man as Parliamentary Counselor
but refused to accept any Parliamentary Vice Minister.
Despite these compromises, it seems certain that the mili-
tary lost this battle as well as the first battle over party
representation in the Cabinet. The political parties, moreover,
have some staunch advocates in the persons of some of the
most powerful statesmen in Japan. Prince Saionji is known
to be firmly convinced that partisan politics is necessary
for the proper functioning of a constitutional government
and is therefore opposed to the abolition of the parties
or their reorganization into one large party. Prince Fumi-
maru Konoye, the President of the House of Peers, has
also recently appeared as an advocate of the political
parties, on the ground that, as long as constitutional govern
ment remains in Japan, the political parties will be necessary.
He did, however, express the hope that politics could be
further purified.
Several of the more prominent Peers, including Prince
Konoye, have come forward with plans for the reform of the
peerage

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.98)
一4 -
peerage and of the House of Peers. It will be remembered
that in a previous despatch the Embassy brought out the
point that in the February 26 and previous incidents,
the young insurgents apparently made no attempt to attack
the hereditary nobility, despite their wealth, but con-
fined their attentions to the industrial and financial
barons, politicians and statesmen. This, it is believed,
was due to the faot that the hereditary nobility are
largely descendants of the daimyo of feudal days and as
such, in the minds of the insurgents, entitled to positions
of wealth and influence. It now appears, however, that
there has been some opposition to the Peers among the
people. It is felt that they should return to their
ancestral acres, instead of playing at politics in Tokyo,
and endeavor there to reform the conditions of rural life.
Prince Konoye himself advocates the return of the nobility
of daimyo descent to their lands.
Prince Konoye has also brought forward a plan, vague
as yet, for the reform of the House of Peers. He proposes
to abolish certain of the hereditary seats held by Princes
and Marquises; to reduce the number of seats held by re-
presentatives of the Counts, Viscounts and Barons; to
revise the system of elective and appointive members;
to eliminate the influence of political parties in the
House of Peers;and to endeavor to change that House into
a sort of congress of representatives of vocational groups,
rather than a congress of representatives of the Peers and
the

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
the high tax-payers, as it is at present. This change,
if it materializes, will constitute an interesting inno-
vation in parliamentary systems. The Lower House will then
be composed of politicians, representing geographical
districts and political parties, while the Upper House
will be composed of representatives of various strata
of Japanese life and various vocational groups.
It is reported that the Cabinet has definitely
decided to abolish the Cabinet Inquiry Couneil (also
variously called the "Cabinet Investigation Council",
the "National Policy Inquiry Council", the " Cabinet
Policy Investigation Council". etc.). This Council,
composed of prominent statesmen, industrialists, poli-
ticians and financiers, was brought into being by the
Okada Cabinet, partly, according to some, in order to
have some organization to which the Cabinet could "pass
the buck". While this may be true to some extent,
it seems that the principal reason for the Cabinet
Inquiry Council was to give the Okada Government the
appearance, at least, of a truly national government,
which it was not, as it did not have the support of the
Seiyukai, then the dominant party in the Lower House
of the Diet. By having a sort of sub-Cabinet, really
representative of the political parties, business-men
and other factions of the body politic, which would be
charged with formulating the policies to be executed
by the Cebinet, it was hoped that the Okada Government
would

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
would be greatly strengthened. The Council, however, had
no real function except the political one mentioned above,
and when the Okada Cabinet was overthrown as a result of the
incident of February 26, there was no longer any reason
for the Council's existence, as the Hirota Cabinet is more
nearly a real national government, needing no subterfuges
to justify its position. It is expected that the Cabinet
Inquiry Council will be replaced, in a measure, by Cabinet
Ministers without portfolio. One plan is to appoint four
or five such Ministers, who would be entrusted with the
formulation of policies, to be executed by the Ministers
with portfolio, who would really be administrative officers
only. This plan, however, smacks too much of the Cabinet
Inquiry Council plan, and probably will not be adopted.
Another plan is to appoint only one or two Ministers without
portfolio, who will act as Vice Premiers and one of whom
will ex officio become the chairman of the Cabinet Inquiry
Bureau, which will be retained by the Hirota Cabinet. The
Cabinet Inquiry Bureau is not entrusted with the formulation
of policies, but merely with conducting the investigations
requested by the Cabinet for the purpose of formulating
policies.
Military.
The principal development in the Army organization since
the incident of February 26, 1936, has been the enforced re-
tirement of all the higher officers in command of the units
to which the insurgent troops were attached, even though
those

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.98)
-7 -
those officers had no direct connection with the affair.
This retirement, or placing on the reserve list, was en-
forced in a chain from the first lieutenants up through
the captains, majors, lieutenant colonels, colonels and
major generals to the lieutenant generals in command of
the divisions which included the insurgent troops. Al-
together 26 officers were placed on the reserve list,
including 4 lientenant generals, 4 major generals, 4 colo-
nels, 1 lieutenant colonel, 5 majors, 6 captains and 2
first lieutenants. These officers were not, as far as is
known, directly connected with the incident, but they were
retired partly for disciplinary reasons, in order that all
officers of the Army may feel a sense of responsibility for
the actions of the men under them, and partly as a re-
straint on the younger officers and privates, who, it is
hoped, will in the future hesitate to take any action which
will bring down upon their superior officers a punishment
which they do not deserve. In the present case, the dis-
ciplinary action taken affected twelve outstanding, high-
ranking officers with excellent prospects and of great
present and prospective value to the Army, thus indicating
clearly to the young officers the harm which an ill-con-
sidered act of theirs can do to their service.
In addition to the above, 6 full generals have been
placed on the reserve list. These included 4 members of
the Supreme Military Council, the Minister for War and
the Chief Aide-de-Camp to the Emperor, all of whom assumed
responsibility

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
responsibility for the outbreak of the insurgent troops and
retired voluntarily. The fact that their resignations were
accepted, however, indicates the determination of the high-
est authorities to compel everyone in the chain of responsi-
bility, from the lowest to the highest, to bear punishment
for the misdeeds of subordinates, and thus to inculcate
a better sense of discipline in the Army.
As yet, as far as the Embassy is aware, no concrete
steps have been taken to change the ideological composition
of the Army. It is known that the Army authorities are at
present conducting investigations, with a view to determining
the identity of and weeding out all officers and men advo-
cating direct-action methods. It is not known as yet if any
of the direct-actionists (except, of course, those who parti-
cipated in the insurrection of February 26 and who are now
undergoing court martial) have been identified and forced
out of the Army, but it is believed that some action along
this line will be taken in the near future. In the meantime,
it is known, the civil authorities have been investigating and
arresting the civilian instigators of the young officers of
the Army. It is rumored that already 300 civilians have
been arrested and are undergoing examination on this charge,
and that the martial law is being continued beyond the time
originally given as the probable termination in order to
provide the authorities with every facility for apprehending
the civilian instigators.
There seems to be little doubt that, as a result of the
incident of February 26, the moderate elements in the Army
have obtained control of that organization and are endeavoring
to

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
to eliminate the influences which have brought about
the incidents of recent years. This rise of the moderate
element does not imply that the Army has less influence
than previously in the councils of state - in fact, it
probably has more - or that it is less determined than
previously in regard to its policy on the Asiatio con-
tinent. The change merely indicates that the Army as a
whole has decided to eliminate the young hot-heads who
have been bringing the Army into disrepute by their
direct-action methods . It is too early to attempt to
predict the effect of the suppression of the hot-heads
on the Army's actions in China, but it would seem reason-
able to expect that a good dose of discipline, if adminis-
tered to the Army both at home and on the continent, would
tend to prevent many of the incidents on the Asiatic
mainland which have in the past threatened to involve Japan
in war with other countries. Unfortunately, there are
no indications yet of this tendency, incidents on the main-
land having been unusually frequent during the past month.
This may be explained by a current rumor, to the effect
that the more radical element in the Army is dissatisfied
with the control of the Army by the moderate element,
as represented by General Terauchi and the men whom he is
gradually placing in positions of influence, and that the
radical element is determined to make more trouble.
The Navy was not involved in the recent incident,
except as a conservative factor which had to be taken into
consideration

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
consideration by the Army, but nevertheless the incident
of February 26 has caused the Navy to ponder deeply and
to take precautionary steps. (It will be remembered that
naval cadets took part in the incident of May 15, 1932.)
Consequently the Navy, taking the February 26 Incident
as a turning point, has embarked on a polioy of reform-
ation and expansion of activities. One of the first
. steps was to place 3 full admirals, 7 vice admirals and
6 rear admirals on the reserve list, as of Maroh 30, 1936.
This, it is believed, was done partly in order to rid the
Navy of all elements creating internal dissension, and
partly because of personal reasons of Admiral Nagano, the
Minister for the Navy, as he undoubtedly desired to eli-
minate those higher officers whom he felt would oppose
his policies. Transfers have also been made to the General
Staff of two officers who are reported to be trouble-makers,
Vice Admiral Kobayashi and Rear Admiral Mazaki, the latter
a younger brother of General Mazaki and an alleged sharer
of the views of the General. It is presumed that their
transfers to the General Staff were brought about as a pre-
liminary to their retirement when the next shift of officers
is made in the autumn.
As the next step, the Navy proposes to create a new
naval organ or council for the purpose of drawing up a new
naval defense policy, of suggesting structural reforms in the
organization of the Navy, and of compiling recommendations,
to be submitted to the Government, of general reform measures
of interest to the Navy. The organ is to be called the
Navy Organization Investigation Council" and it is reported
that

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
that Vice Admiral Hasegawa, the Vice Minister for the
Navy, is to be chairman. One of its duties will be the
consideration of methods of eliminating unnecessary ex-
pense, of promoting efficiency and of harmonizing the
execution of naval policies with the national budget.
It is reported that the new defense polioy which
the new naval organ will compile will be that of "home
defense combined with an advance southward", as well as
hegemony of the sea and air on the Western Pacific from
the Equator on the south to the l8oth meridian on the east.
The Embassy will endeavor to obtain more information in
regard to the activities of the new naval orgen and will
report at a later date, giving particular attention to the
so-called "new defense policy" and the southward advance.
Economic
As was reported in a previous despatch, the intention
of the new Government is to proceed slowly in regard to
economic reforms, in order that the economic structure
may not be endangered by unduly hasty changes. As yet,
therefore, little has been done except to take the step
of lowering the interest rates. On April 6 the Bank of
Japan, followed by the special, or semi-official banks,
lowered the discount rate on commercial bills and the in-
terest rates on loans and overdrafts by an average of about
.37 per cent per annum. According to the Minister for Finance,
this was done as a first step toward the general lowering
of interest rates in Japan, which the Minister has advocated
since his appointment. According to the Governor of the
Bank of Japan, another reason for the move was to prevent
the

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.97)
-12-
the selling of Government bonds - in other words, to
make the interest rates on bonds more attractive than
those on bank deposits and thus to create a market for
future issues of national bonds. On April 18 another
move toward lower interest rates was made, consisting
of the conversion of a block of 5% Government bonds into
3.5% bonds. The policy of converting the existing Govern-
ment bonds into lower interest bearing bonds will be
carried out gradually.
The Commercial Attaché of the Embassy had an interesting
conversation on April 9 with the Minister and Vice Minister
for Finance, in the course of which the Minister for Fi-
nance explained his policies in regard to Government fi-
nance, taxation, exchange rates and interest rates. A
memorandum of this conversation is enclosed.
Respectfully yours,
Roo
Joseph C. Grew.

Enclosure No. 1 to despatoh No.
178& of Apri1 16, 1936 from the

MEMORANDUM.
On Thursday April 9th I had the pleasure of an inter-
view with Dr. Eiichi Baba, Minister of Finance. I was re-
ceived by Dr. Baba and the Vice-Minister of Finance, Mr.
Kawagoe, at the Minister's official residence. After
explaining to Dr. Baba that I was shortly proceeding to
the United States and would therefore appreciate a state-
ment from him regarding the financial policy of the present
administration, I asked the following questions:
1. What is the general financial policy of the present
Government?
In reply Dr. Baba stated that the Government's policy was
outlined in his first statement to the press at the time of
the formation of the present Cabinet, namely: low interest
rates, increased Government expenditures if found necessary,
continuation of deficit bond issues for the present and
some form of increased taxation. Continuing he said that
the first step towards putting this policy into actual
operation was taken on March l9th for the past fiscal year.
The next step was on April 6th when the Bank of Japan an-
nounced the lowering of its discount and loan rates ef-
fective the 7th. City banks have decided to adopt the
same procedure as of April the loth.
The

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
The next move towards lower interest rates, stated
Dr. Baba, was the announcement on April 8th of the conversion
of ¥373,000,000 worth of 5% national bonds into new 3.5%
bonds. It is intended to continue this conversion poliey
but on a decidedly gradual scale.
Regarding taxation the Minister of Finance pointed
out that the preceding Government relied on an estimated
natural increase in revenue with which to reduce the
deficit bond issues. The present Administration, how-
ever, feels that existing conditions in Japan make it
necessary to somewhat expand Government expenditures,
which step will necessitate additional revenue. This
must be obtained from increased taxes. Furthermore,
there is a definite need for the readjustment of both
the national and prefectural taxation systems in Japan.
This matter is now being given considerable thought but
no definite plan has yet been adopted and none will be
placed in operation until the beginning of the fiscal
year 1937-38. Under the circumstances, it will not be
possible to reduce deficit bonds within the next two
or three years, although this will be done as soon as
possible.
The next question was, What is the present Administra-
tion's policy regarding the foreign exchange value of the
Yen? In reply Dr. Baba stated that the Yen was at present
fairly well stabilized, rates having maintained practically
the

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
the same level for the past two years. This situation
was satisfactory and no articifial means will be applied
to cause fluctuation in currency values. He pointed
out that it was his earnest desire to avoid unhealthy
inflation.
Replying to my question as to whether the interest
rate on postal savings accounts will be lowered, Dr. Baba
said that this was a very important matter inasmuch as at
present there are around 46 million small depositors.
It is hoped that no revision of the present rate will be
found necessary. The effect of the recently revised
interest rates by the Bank of Japan and city banks will
be very carefully studied. If future market rates reach
decidedly lower levels it may be found necessary to lower
the postal savings rate in order to guard against heavy
diversion of funds into that account.
I then asked Dr. Baba whether he had any plans formu-
lated for the conversion or redemption of high interest
bearing foreign currency bonds such as the 6-1/2% Dollar
issue. He replied that so far he had given very little
thought to Japan's foreign loan obligations. If curcum-
stances develop which warrant any change in the present
status of these bonds the matter will then naturally be
given serious consideration.
From this point our conversation became somewhat
general. I emphasized the importance to the United States
of her trade with Japan and pointed out to Dr, Baba that
during recent years America was taking considerably more
sundry

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
sundry goods from Japan. I explained that this fact was
largely responsible for the criticism and opposition which
Japanese manufactured goods were receiving from some quarters
in the United States. I expressed the hope that the Japa-
nese people will not allow trade relations between Japan
and America to become endangered by this opposition which
prevails in the United States to such a small percentage
of the total trade. Dr. Baba stated that the same condi-
tions exist in Japan regarding certain Amerioan and other
foreign products. He remembered seeing several press re-
ports attacking foreign goods and demanding higher tariff
rates regardless of the trade position as a whole. In this
connection Dr. Baba was quite emphatic and impressive in
speaking of a possible serious future conflict of interest
between the United States and Japan. He said that he, as
well as most all of the Japanese people, had never even
entertained an idea of any trouble between the United States
and Japan. He requested that I convey such a message
to the American people and whenever I have the opportunity
of speaking to American business men to emphasize the frienaly
feeling which exists in Japan for my country regardless
of the various criticisms which appear from time to time
in the local press. I assured Dr. Baba that I personally
very strongly reciprocated his feelings and felt that the
great majority of Americans neither anticipated nor desired
any trouble with Japan.
In conclusion Dr. Baba expressed the opinion that the
world

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
world depression was caused solely by over-production and
a decline of purchasing power. He has followed very closely
the fforts made by the American Government to increase
the purchasing power in that country and stated that the
policy of the present Administration in Japan is to increase
the purchasing power of the mass of the people in this
country.
Dr. Baba understands a good deal of English but does
not use it in his conversation and it was, therefore,
necessary for our interview to be conducted through an inter-
preter.
On the same afternoon I also called on Mr. Kurusu, former
Chief of the Commercial Bureau of the Foreign Office but
recently appointed Ambassador to Belgium. Mr. Kurusu is
sailing from Yokohama on May l4th by the "Asama Maru" and
expects to be in Washington on or about June 7th. While
he will have no authority to negotiate with our State
Department on matters pertaining to the oil and tertilo
controversies and the proposed Automobile Control Law, he
told me that he would like to discuss these questions in-
formally with Mr. Sayre or Mr. Phillips and asked if I could
arrange to be in Washington at that time. I told him I would
endeavor to arrange my itinerary so that I can meet him in
Washington.
(Signed) F.S. Williams .

PAPER:
THE TMES

CITY:

DATE: APR 27 1936
15
THE DUALISM OF
JAPAN
NEW BUILDING ON
OLD DOCTRINE
A RISKY EXPERIMENT
From Our Tokyo Correspondent
The revolt of young officers of the Japanese
Army was followed by a political crisis in which
senior officers prevented the formation of a
Government until the Prime Minister had
accepted their demands. Rebellion by juniors
and dictation to a Cabinet by seniors are both
remote from English ideas of the Army's place
in a constitutional State. One should remember
the dualism—the mingling of the medieval and
the modern in a social framework half new, half
old—which gives Japanese life its peculiar
character. The "Showa Restoration," which
inspired the young officers, and the "national
polity" issue (discussed in The Times of
September 10), which has the support of the
Army as a whole, are woven of threads drawn
from Communism and Fascism and also from
the feudal days, still so near, when Japan was
governed by a war lord in the name of a sacred
and secluded Emperor.
In its broadest aspect the movement of the
fighting services is a struggle for freedom from
controi by politicians. At the extreme left, or
right—at the point where Communism and
Fascism meet—this movement hardened into a
revolutionary conspiracy of young officers who
have been captivated by the doctrines of Marx
and Mussolini dressed in the clothes of Imperial
Japan. Their aim is a totalitarian Socialist
State under a government which is naively
represented as a restoration of Imperial rule but
would, in fact, be a revival of feudal military
government with the Army taking the place of
the Shogunate. The February revolt, like that
of May, 1932, was the work of this small revo-
lutionary wing. Their gospel is set forth in a
book by Ikki Kita called " A Reconstruction
Programme for Japan." Kita, with 150 others,
was arrested during the February rising. His
book is forbidden (except in expurgated form),
but mimeographed copies have been circulated
among Army officers. He preaches a " revo-
lutionary Empire reorganized on the principles
of State Socialism," and outlines a technique by
which the Army is to effect the revolution in
three stages: a military coup d'Etat, suspension
of the Constitution, restoration of Imperial rule.
The February affair was the second of two
attempts the young officers have made to carry
out the first stage of Kita's programme. By the
murder of statesmen and the creation of dis-
order they hoped to create a situation in which
an Imperial mandate would be given to the
Army.
THE EMPEROR'S POSITION
The Court-martial which tried Lieutenant-
Colonel Aizawa for the murder of Lieutenant-
General Nagata was converted into a platform

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
Programme for Japan." Kita, with 150 others.
was arrested during the February rising. His
book is forbidden (except in expurgated form).
but mimeographed copies have been circulated
among Army officers. He preaches a "revo-
lutionary Empire reorganized on the principles
of State Socialism," and outlines a technique by
which the Army is to effect the revolution in
three stages: a military coup d"Etat, suspension
of the Constitution, restoration of Imperial rule.
The February affair was the second of two
attempts the young officers have made to carry
out the first stage of Kita's programme. By the
murder of stateemen and the creatien of dis-
order they hoped to creale a situation in whlak
an Imperial masdate would be siven to the
Army.
THE EMPEROR'S POSITION
The Court-martial which tried Lieutenant-
Colonel Aizawa for the murder of Lieutenant-
General Nagata was converted into a platform
for the propaganda of the revolutionary wing.
"The spirit of the Showa Restoration is about
to be manifested through Aizawa," declared
Lieutenant-Colonel Mitsui, Aizawa's defender.
When Aizawa was asked to explain what he
meant by the Showa Restoration he answered:
The Emperor is the incarnation of the God who reigns
over the universe. The world is deadlocked because of
capitalism, communism, anarchism, and atheism. The
personal rule of the Emperor is firmly established in
Japan and must continue. Democracy is all wrong.
The present times are marked by arrogation of political
powers. A Prime Minister has propounded the institu-
lional theory of the Emperor. School teachers say that
their salaries are paid by the people, not by the State—a
least my child is so taught. The return of political and
financial power to the Emperor would be the Showa
Restoration.
Mitsui threw a curious light on the aims of the
group when he declared that Lieutenant-General
Nagata had one of the best brains in the Army,
but he believed in a controlled economy and
thought capitalists were needed to operate it.
The Showa Restoration demands that national
economy should come under the direct rule of
the Emperor. It need not be said that personal
rule by an autocrat is something Japan has never
known and is foreign to all her ideas of the
Emperor's unique position. The Emperor is
held to be a divinely descended sovereign who
governs the country through his servants but is
not responsible for their actions. When Aizawa
proposes the return of political and financial
power to the Emperor he is simply demanding
that power should be taken away from those
who at present exercise it.
Advocates of the Showa Restoration assert
that the Meiji Restoration (1868), which every
Japanese reveres as the most glorious episode
in the nation's history, has been turned from
its true path by the introduction of capitalism
and political democracy. The remedy for the
present evils therefore begins with the removal
of the capitalists and the politicians, their
"docile clerks," from place and power, and the
restoration of direct Imperial rule. Only a
small revolutionary group understands by that
the Imperialist-Socialist State of Kita's vision.
The great majority are content to demand
"clarification of the national polity."
FUNDS FOR THE SERVICES
In the " Minobe affair" the practical end
pursued under that abstract slogan was the
Army's right to be free of political control in
matters affecting defence. The struggle seemed
to have been won. The position now established
is this: The supreme command belongs to the
Emperor, who exercises it through the General
Staff. The supreme command determines the
size of the Army and Navy. The function
of the Cabinet is to provide the funds
required and decide how they are to be raised.
"The duty and power of ihe Services is to
decide what they want," writes Mr. T. Iwabuchi,
" while that of the Government is to decide how
to get it." But the controversy did not end
there, and Mr. Hirota has again pledged the
Government to clarify the national polity. So
far as he is concerned this will mean the
elimination of certain alien elements from
Japanese education. Others have more con-
crete ideas. "Retired Naval Officer," writing
in Contemporary Japan last December, ex-
plained that "clarification of the national
polity " meant a new kind of government. He
recounted the thrice-told tale of the London
Naval Treaty, signed by a party Prime Minister
(afterwards assassinated) with the support of the
senior statesman against the advice of the Naval
General Staff. That event, he declared, had
convinced the younger officers that " the future
of the Empire was doomed so long as Cabinets
based on political parties and statesmen of this
type were in control."
When he attempts to define a new system the
retired naval officer navigates walers strange to
English political thought. Foreign States, he says.

meam by the Showe Restoration be answereu.

PAPER:
THE TILES
NUMBER:
APR 27 1936
CITY:
LONDON
DATE:
The Cloud in the Far East
Incidents continue to multiply on the frontier
separating the Japanese vassal State of Man-
chukuo from the territory of the Soviet and of
the Russian-controlled State of Outer Mongolia.
The joint frontier Commissions which were to
investigate the responsibility for these incidents
and to prevent their recurrence have not yet
been established, though both Governments
proclaim their desire for a peacsful settle-
ment. M. VoRosLor, the Soviet Com-
missar for Defence, speaking at a dinner
given by the Japaness Embassy in
Moscow, agreed with the Ambassador that
there was no dispute between the two
countries which could not be settled by peaceful
means, and recalled the Soviet Government's
desire for the conclusion of a pact of non-
aggression. If, he added, Japan was really
anxious for peace she would put a stop to the
activities of irresponsible elements who provoke
bloody frontier clashes. This was very plain
speaking for such an occasion. The warning
implicit in it recalls the firmness with which,
a few weeks ago, M. STALiN emphasized the
Russian determination to maintain the " inde-
"pendence " of Outer Mongolia, meaning
apparently thereby its dependence upon the
Soviet Government. Mr. AritA, the new
Japanese Foreign Minister, discussing Japanese
policy with foreign Press correspondents on
Saturday, admitted the deterioration in Russo-
Japanese relations, but was confident that all
the differences could be settled by diplomatic
means. Japan, he declared, did not think of
taking action on account of frontier incidents
"unless others launch aggression upon us."
There need be no doubt that both the Govern-
ments really desire peace, at any rate for the
time being, and equally no doubt that their
differences are susceptible of diplomatic adjust-
ment. Where doubt arises is over the ability of
the Japanese Government and Foreign Office to
prevent their hand from being forced by
elements over which they have little control.
There have been occasions, and there may well
be more, upon which they have found themselves
committed by military action to courses not easy
to reconcile with their ostensible policy. An
attempt to bring about greater harmony between
the views of the Foreign Office and the
Army is now being made by the institu-
tion of regular informal conferences upon
foreign affairs between the Foreign
Minister and the two Defence Ministers, though
it is generally assumed that the "unification
"of policy" sought by this means will reflect
the military rather than the diplomatic view.
Relations between the Army and the Govern-
ment even in normal times are very different
in Japan from what they are in Western
countries. The present time moreover is far
from normal, as the world was reminded by the
mutinous outbreak of two months ago and the
political crisis which followed it. There can be
no real stability in Japanese policy until a more
permanent equilibrium is reached between the
elements now competing for control. Our Tokyo
Correspondent in an illuminating article to-day
explains the struggle of the fighting services for
freedom from control by politicians and the
revortionary movement among the young
officers in which that struggle finds its most
drastic expression. The ringleaders in this
movement, "captivated by the doctrines of
"Marx and Mussolini dressed in the clothes of
"Imperial Japan," aim at getting rid of
capitalists and politicians and setting up a
tomlitarian Sainlice Stata undes a faudal :

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.98)
anxious for yor or r
activities of irreeponelble slements who provoks
bloody frontier clasbes. This was vary plair
speaking for such as occasion. The warming
implioit hs it recalls the firmness with which
a fow weoks ago, M. STaLmm emphasleod the

be more, upon which they have found themselves
committed by military action to courses not easy
to reconcile with their ostensible policy. An
attempt to bring about greater harmony between
the views of the Foreign Office and the
Army is now being made by the institu-
tion of regular informal conferences upon
foreign affairs between the Foreign
Minister and the two Defence Ministers, though
it is generally assumed that the "unification
"of policy" sought by this means will reflect
the military rather than the diplomatic view.
Relations between the Army and the Govern-
ment even in normal times are very diffesent
in Japan from what they are in Wetn
countries. The present time moreover is far
from normal, as the world was reminded by the
mutinous outbreak of two months ago and the
political crisis which followed it. There can be
no real stability in Japanese policy until a more
permanent equilibrium is reached between the
clements now competing for control. Our Tokyo
Correspondent in an illuminating article to-day
explains the struggle of the fighting services for
freedom from control by politicians and the
revolutionary nmovement among the young
officers in which that struggle finds its most
drastic expression. The ringleaders in this
movement, "captivated by the doctrines of
"Marx and Mussolini dressed in the clothes of
"Imperial Japan," aim at getting rid of
capitalists and politicians and setting up a
totalitarian Socialist State under a feudal mili-
tary Government. In this extreme form they do
not seem to have found any widespread support
for their ideas; but it has yet to be shown that
the Army chiefs are able to control the ferment
which led to the February mutiny, and that they
are ready to cooperate with the civilian Govern-
ment in a conciliatory foreign policy.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
May 25, 1936.
EHD:
Tokyo's No. 1798 of April 30, 1936.
This despatch from Tokyo, prepared by
Mr. Dickover, discusses what is known as
the "southward advance" policy said to be
advocated by one school of expansionists
in Japan. According to the writer, the
advocates of this policy, led by the navy,
consider that the lands to the south of
Japan, presumably the islands of the Pacific,
Netherland East India, Siam, and possibly
the Philippine Islands, offer a more pro-
mising field for a Japanese "advance" than
the Asiatic continent to the north and west.
The writer states that the reasons for
this redirection of Japanese activities
appear to be the desire of the navy to play
a larger part in the expansionist activities
of the nation; the desire of the navy to
assure itself of a supply of petroleum,
presumably in Netherland India; and the
fact that the advance in northwestern Asia
has been checked by the possibility of a
major war with Soviet Russia.
You will probably wish to read the
despatch, as it contains considerable food
for

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
for thought. However, it should be realized
that the so-called "southward advance"
doctrine is largely a product of newspaper
talk based, it may be, on the views of
certain individuals in the navy.
The title of the despatch and some of
the comment might convey the impression
that there is a definitely formulated
policy of aggressive action against
territories to the south of Japan. While
the Japanese Government has unquestionably
given consideration to the possibility of
eventual Japanese dominance over present
European colonies and possessions in the
Orient, it seems highly unlikely that
there is any disposition on the part of the
Government to undertake any aggressive
action toward those territories under
present circumstances.
The chief criticism which one can
make of the despatch is that it conveys a
misleading impression. It develops a
thesis, based largely on hearsay and news-
paper talk, with more assurance than the
facts would warrant.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
It has been known for a number of years past that
JUN 2- 1936
there are in Japan two schools of thought in regard to
FILED
the direction of Japan's national expansion. One school,
the "continental school", led by the Japanese Army, ad-
vocates advance on the Asiatic continent, principally to
the west and north, in order to acquire sources of rew
material and markets for manufactured goods in Manchuria,
Mongolia,
F/FG

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
53-2
- 2 -
Mongolia, Siberia and China Proper. The efforts of this
school have resulted in the annexation by Japan of Korea,
the formation of "Manchukuo" and the establishment of the
semi-autonomous governments of North China, but they also
brought about the abortive expeditions into Shantung and
Siberia following the World War. On the whole, however,
this school has been reasonably successful in its attempts
to contribute to the expansion of the Japanese nation and
consequently has held the center of the stage for a number
of years past, thoroughly eclipsing the other school of
thought.
The second school of thought is the "oceanic" or "blue
water" school, which advocates Japanese expansion, not to
the frozen wastes of the north, where it is difficult for
Japanese to live and work, nor to the over-populated country
of Chine, where Japanese cannot compete with the local
labor, but to the south, whence, it is said, the principal
portion of the Japanese racial root stock came many hun-
dreds of years ago and where climatic and labor conditions
favor Japanese expansion. This second school, as is to be
expected, is led by the Japanese Navy, as eny expansionist
program to the south of Japan would inevitably mean that
the primary participant would be the Navy rather than the
Army. (In this connection please see strictly confidential
947.94/20
despatch No. 125 of February 11, 1936, from the Consul General
in Sydney, N.S.W.)
There has been a marked recrudescence of opinion in
recent months favoring the southward expansion theory. A
large

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.98)
533
-3 -
large part of this has consisted merely of newspaper rumors,
but it is believed that the rumors have been inspired by the
younger officers in the Navy who are dissatisfied with the
Navy's place in the shade and wish.to see the Navy take a
more prominent position in the progress of the nation.
These newspaper reports of the Navy's opinions, therefore,
are entitled to a certain amount of consideration, repre-
senting as they do the ideas of a large section of the Navy.
During September, 1935, reports appeared in the Japa-
nese press to the effect that the Navy had begun to advocate
a general southward advance. The reasons for this pro-
posed southward movement were stated as being both defensive
and economic. it was claimed that it would be necessary
for the Japanese Navy to offset the strengthening of Amer-
ican defenses in the Pacific Ocean, as represented by the
establishment of commercial air routes and the rumored
strengthening of the fortifications at Guam, by the ex-
tension of Japanese naval activities to the south. It was
also claimed that it would be desirable for Japan, for
national defense and trade reasons, to endeavor to acquire
further supplies of rice, fish, petroleum, cotton, iron
ores, wool, end rubber in the South Seas, as well as to
provide outlets in that region for excess Japanese popu-
lation.
In the early part of the year several persons, in-
cluding naval officers, are reported to have stated, in
articles in the magazines and in public speeches, that
now that "Manchukuo" is well established, the time had
●
arrived

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
53-4
-4-
arrived for the Japanese to turn their attention south-
ward, in an effort to obtain concessions and increased
trade in the Netherland Indies and the Philippine Islands.
On January 22, 1936, at a luncheon given by business-
men of Osaka, Vice Admiral Takahashi, the Commander-in-
Chief of the Combined Fleet, is said to have stated that:
"Manchukuo is experiencing sound development,
and her economic basis is firmly established. We
should, therefore, turn our attention southward,
and strive for the acquisition of new markets. .
In such a case the cruising radius of the
ships of the Japanese Navy will have to be in-
creased to reach such locations as New Guinea,Bprneo
and the Celebes."
(Note: Admiral Takahashi's speech was never pub-
lished in Japan, as far as the Embassy is aware,
but a summary, which was later claimed to contain
misquotations, was cabled to the United States by
one of the news agencies and was used by Senator
Pittman in his speech before the Senate on Febru-
ary 10, 1936. The above excerpts from Admiral
Takahashi's speech were obtained through indireot
sources by the Naval Attaché of the Embassy.)
On March 21, 1936, the OSAKA MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI
NICHI, English Edition, published what purported to be the
Navy's recommendations to the newly-established Hirota
Government for reform of the national administration.
Under the heading of "Foreign Policy", the Navy is re-
ported to have suggested the following:
"(1) Establishment of Foreign Policy.
Centralization of diplomacy and enhancement
of the self-initiative polioy through the
founding of a national policy of dealing with
foreign nations.
(2) Protection of the North and Southern
Penetration. As regards Japan's continental
development, we should not depend on Asia alone
to maintain the life of our nation. We should
expect a southward penetration for our national
development in the future. But this southward
penetration must be carried out by pacific means,
rejecting all recourse to force."
on

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
53-5
- 5 -
On Apri1 17, 1936, the YOMIURI and MIYAKO newspapers
published reports to the effect that the Premier, Mr.
Hirota, had decided to abolish the present South Sees
Government (which has jurisdiction over the former German
islands north of the equator, now being held by Japan
under mandate), and to place the islands under the juris-
diction of a bureau in the Formosan Government-General,
"as a step in the execution of the so-called 'southward
policy'". At the same time it was planned to establish
a military government of Formosa and the South Seas Is-
lands, the Governor-General to be an Army or Navy officer
and the Vice Governor-General a civilian. According to
the ASAHI of April 25, 1936, however, Admiral Nagano,
the Minister for the Navy, is opposed to the plan of pla-
cing the South Seas Islands under the jurisdiction of
the Formosan Government General, because, instead of
being abolished, the South Seas Government should be
strengthened "to promote the Navy's policy of southward
advance". The Admiral based his arguments against the in-
corporation of the South Seas Government into the Formosan
Government General on the grounds that there is little eco-
nomic connection between the South Seas Islands and Formosa,
that the people of the two administrations are different
racially and could not well be governed by the same adminis-
tration, and that, as the islands are held under mendate
from the League of Nations, they cannot be regarded as an
integral part of the territory of Japan, as is Formosa, and
cannot be governed under the same system. It does not appear
that

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.98)
53-4
- 6-
that any decision has as yet been reached upon the ques-
tion of the future administration of the South Seas Is-
lands.
There appear to be several reasons for the sudden
recurrence of propaganda in Japan for a "southward advance".
Some observers believe that the Japanese Navy is envious
of the success obtained by the Army in promoting Japanese
expansion and wishes to earn credit with the nation by it-
self undertaking new expansion schemes. With this is
bound up the question of budget appropriations. The further
afield the Navy extends its activities, the larger the appro-
priations which will be necessary for naval defense. Other
observers believe that the Navy, in advocating a southward
advance policy, is primarily in search of an assured supply
of petroleum and is planning to obtain supplies eventually
in Netherland India. This is the opinion of the Naval
Attaché of the Embassy and of the Netherlands Minister to
Tokyo, whose remarks will be given later in this report.
The First Secretary of the Soviet Embassy in Tokyo, who
naturally regards all developments in Japan from the Soviet
point of view, believes that the recent advocacy of a south-
ward advance policy is caused by the fact that the advance
of the Japanese in northwestern Asia has been halted and
cannot be resumed without serious danger of a major war.
He points out that General Chiang Kai-shek has moved a part
of his troops up into southern Shansi; that the Chinese
Communist troops are advancing eastwards through Shensi and
Suiyuan,

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
53-7
- 7 -
Suiyuan, with the slogan of "Down with Japanese Imperian-
ism"; that Outer Mongolia, backed by the Soviet Union,
is offering stout resistance to Japanese penetration;
and that the Soviet Union can effectively prevent the
Japanese Army from encroaching upon Soviet territory.
The Japanese Army on the Asiatic continent is thus fairly
thoroughly encircled and cannot advance without the risk
of a serious war. Consequently the Japanese nation has
turned its attention to the south.
hile this Embassy cannot subscribe entirely to
the thesis that the Japanese nation as a whole has turned
its attention to the south, it does appear probable that
the Japanese Navy, finding the efforts of the Army on the
continent now being fairly consistently thwarted, believes
the time ripe to make a move itself and thereby to draw
the attention of the nation to the Navy rather than to the
Army. It should be understood that there is in Japan a more
or less persistent feud between the Army and the Navy,
dating back to the days when one was controlled by the
Choshu clan and the other by the Satsuma clan. The feud,
of course, does not extend to the point where either party
would let their quarrels endanger the Empire, but it is not
only possible but probable that the Navy, finding that the
Army has extended itself to a point where it is likely to
involve the country in a war unless it stops, has taken
advantage of the situation to show the nation that "Codlin
is the friend, not Short". In addition to that, there is
no

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
53-8
- 8 -
no doubt that the Navy wished to extend its sphere of aoti-
vities to cover the entire western Pacific north of the
Equator and to include, if possible, in that sphere a
section of the Pacific south of the Equator from which
an assured supply of petroleum can be obtained under any
circumstances. The present oil situation in Japan, brought
on by the Army's demand that the foreign oil interests
carry stocks in the country amounting to six months'
supplies, is so uncertain that the Navy undoubtedly will
endeavor to find an independent source of supply as soon
as possible.
The Embassy has been endeavoring, for some time past,
to ascertain exactly what was behind the sudden appearance
of propaganda in favor of a "southward advance", what are
the objectives of the movement, and what effect the move-
ment .ll have upon American interests in the Far East and
especially in the Philippine Islands. Nothing very definite
has yet been learned, but the following summaries of con-
versations and newspaper articles may serve to throw some
light on the question.
On April 25, 1936, General Pabst, the Netherlands
Minister to Japan, called on me and stated, in the course
of our conversation, that he had telegraphed his Govern-
ment to the effect that, if conditions in Europe remained
in the present state, compelling the British fleet to stay
in or near home waters, there was a "fifty-fifty chance"
that the Japanese Navy would attempt a "coup de main"
within six months, with the objective of taking possession
of some part of the Netherlands Indies producing petroleum.
The

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.98)
53-9
- 9 -
The Netherlands Minister, however, is somewhat inclined
to take an alarmist view of affairs, and, except for the
propaganda in favor of a southward movement, I see nothing
to indicate any sudden or drastic move by the Japanese
Navy in the neer future. I am therefore inclined to
discount the Minister's statement.
In a conversation with the First Secretary of the
Embassy on April 21, 1936, Admirel Hasegawa, the Vice
Minister for the Nevy, commented on the "southward advance"
policy of the Navy. He said that the policy was largely
"newspaper talk" but that the Navy did advocate the ex-
tension of Japanese trade to the south. If important trade
routes to the islands to the south of Japan were thus es-
tablished, it would be necessary for the Navy to extend its
operations further afield for the purpose of protecting
these trade routes. The Navy's policy, however, was purely
pacific and implied no threat whatsoever against the
Netherlands Indies or the Philippine Islands. It was the
erroneous conception of the policy published by the news-
papers which had startled various persons.
On April 25, 1936, r. Arita, the Minister for Foreign
Affairs, received the foreign newspaper correspondents in
Tokyo. In the course of the conversation, one of the cor-
respondents asked the Minister to clarify the policy of
southward expansion which had been mentioned in the press
of Japan. According to the JAPAN ADVERYISER of April 26,
1936,
nThe

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.97)
53-10
- 10 -
"The Foreign Minister said that he knew that
the papers were ascribing an alleged intention
of southward expansion to naval circles, but he
did not know whether they were accurate. He
could see nothing beyond economic expansion,
for Japan has no ulterior ambitions.
On April 30, 1936, Mr. Shigeru Yoshida, recently ap-
pointed Japanese Ambassador to Great Britain, called and
stated, in the course of our conversation, that the population
problem in Japan was becoming increasingly difficult and
that foreign countries should realize the seriousness of the
situation and endeavor to help Japan in finding an adequate
outlet. He said that it was principally a matter of finding
outlets for Japan's trade, with opportunity for Japanese
subjects to follow that trade. Upon being asked, he said
that it meant peaceful penetration. Again upon being asked,
he said that he thought that the "blue-water school" ( i.e.,
the school advocating expansion to the south) would win out
eventually over the "continental school".
The Embassy will endeavor to obtain further clarifica-
tion of this alleged policy of the Japanese Navy.
Respectfully yours,
800.
Joseph C. Grew.

Sir:
I have the honor to enclose the English text, as
translated by the JAPAN ADVERTISER, of a speech made on
JUN 8- 1936
May 7 in the House of Representatives by Mr. Takao Saito,
FILED
a member of the Minseito. This speeoh is noteworthy be-
cause of the liberal views expressed and the general ac-
claim with which it has been received by the press and
the people. It also represents a departure from speeches
made in the last several Diet sessions by reason of its
courage
F/FG

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
courage, its clarity, and its lack of political chi-
canery.
Among the interesting and pertinent points brought
out by Saito were the following: that et the present
time those persons who did not speak of reform and reno-
vation were apparently considered unpatriotic; that
everyone advocated reform, some riting about it, some
making speeches about it, and others even resorting
to violence to carry it out; that meny of those who
shouted for reform and the "Showa Restoration" did not
even know whst this reformation should accomplish;
that he (the sneaker) had no antipathy toward the Army
or the ar Minister or any idea of bringing about alien-
ation of the people from the Army; that there may have
been many social causes for the February 26 Incident
but that one of them was the continual participation
of the military in political affairs; that the Meiji
Imperial Rescript to the Army forbade participation
of the military in political affairs; that if "fight-
ing men" were allowed to debate on politics and eco-
nomics it was only natural that tney shoula eventually
resort to force to carry out their views; that the
germ of such incidents of violence as those of May
15, 1932, and February 26, 1936, 1ay in the puerile
gullibility of the younger officers, who believed in
ideas about which they knew nothing; that the hushing
up of past incidents by the Army was one of the
causes of the February 26 Incident; that, apropos of
the

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
the May 15 Incident, "such an action as murdering a
Premier entrusted with the affairs of state by His
Majesty the Emperor in his Official Residence in
broad daylight can never be permitted"; that if
radical leftist ideas were harmful to the nation
extreme rightist ideas were equally pernicious; that
the assassination of Finance Minister Takahashi,
General atanabe, and Viscount Saito could not be
adequately denounced nd that the coup was "futile
and meaningless"; and that the Japanese people had
many things to complain about but that everyone must
stifle his remarks owing to the restriction of the
freedom of speech. According to the nevspapers, ir.
Saito's speech vas reeted by the Lower House members
with sreat appleuse.
The reply of Premier Hirota to Mr. Saito's inter-
pellation a peared to sidestep the issue. The sist of
the Premier's remarks was that in the present inter-
national situation reforms were needed to fit a state
of "armed peace" ana not one of "peacetime". On the
other hand, the .ar inister's reply was unhesitating
and to the point.
He stated that he was completely
in accord with the interpellator's views, that the
Army's ideas were embodied in the Meiji Imperial Re-
scrint to the Army, and that he had conveyed his view
to the Army to the effect that all political actions
are prohibited in its ranks. The answers of the Educa-
tion and Justice Ministers to Saito's interpellation
were

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.97)
一 4-
were without interest.
The newspapers were unanimous in praising Mr.
Saito's speech and General Terauchi's frank and clear-
cut reply and various members of the mbassy have heard
a similar oxpression of views from Jepanese. The TOkYo
ICHI ICHI of May 8 stated that r. Saito had said what
the nation had long wanted to say but had failed to
do. The JAPiN TI.S of May 8 and 9 gave voice to tho
same oninion and deplored the fact that the Japanese

after the tragic decth of Premier inukai. In an edi-

marks, the YOMIURI of May 9 asserted that the February
26 Incident would not have occurred if the authorities
had taken appropriate measures to curb extreme views
held by certain elements in the Army.
Mr. Saito's speech has been the highlight in the
Diet

Tokyo's 1833 of May 14, 1936, reports
further in regard to the aftermath of the
February 26 incident. The report is
divided into two parts -- one concerning
developments in the army, and the other
concerning economic, social and administra-
tive reforms.
The army unquestionably suffered a
considerable loss of sympathy in consequence
of the February 26 incident. Numerous
critical interpellations were directed at
the Minister for War in the special session
of the Diet. The press showed itself in
sympathy with the critics of the army.
The army has obviously taken to heart
the criticisms directed against it and has
taken steps to eliminate those elements
which have called down the wrath of the
public. The sentence of death imposed
upon Colonel Aizawa, slayer of General
Nagata, is an indication of the deter-
mination of the army to put its house in
order.
At the same time the army is unshaken
in its determination to dominate in the
councils of the nation. Rather than being
crushed by the shame of the February 26
incident, the army has in fact tightened
its hold upon the government.
Following

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
Following the February 26 incident
there was much talk of the necessity of
far-reaching reforms in the economic,
social and administrative affairs of the
nation. This far there has been little
evidence of any concrete measures being
undertaken, although many proposals are
being discussed. Observers are coming to
believe that the Government is deliberately
procrastinating, in the expectation that
the agitation for such measures will die
down. Such tactics are fraught with
danger in view of the state of mind in
Japan at present.
894.00/651

WTT/DLY

ith reference to the Embassy's despatch No. 1784,
dated April 17, 1936, concerning the results flowing
from the incident of February 26, 1936, I now have the
honor to report the following developments in the situ-
ation.
Position of the Army.
The Japanese Army, and particularly that portion
of the Army which has been concerning itself with political
questions
F/FG

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.98)
552
- 2 -
questions and advocating ultra-nationalistic princi-
ples, does not appear to be in favor in Japen at the
moment. Since the convening of the special session
of the Diet on Mey 1, numerous critical interpella-
tions have been directed at the Liinister for War, the
most striking one being that of r. Takao Saito, a
Minseito member of the Lower House, delivered on May
7 (Embassy's despatch No. 1826, dated Mey 13, 1936).
In the course of his interpellation, r. Saito cri-
ticized the teniency of young Army ofiicers to meddle
in political affairs and described their ideas as
"naive", "laughable" and "simple and pure but puerile".
He condenned the Army authorities for not having taken
steps at an earlier date to eliminate erroneous ideas
in the Army and declared that the May 15 and February
26 incidents could have been prevented if the higher
authorities in the Army had endeavored from the first
to remove the causes of the evil.
He further declared
that extreme rightists were as dangerous to the state
as extreme leftists and advised the higher military
authorities to guide properly the younger officers,
in order to prevent the crowth of dangerous idees.
His speech was greeted with loud and prolonged applause
and was later conmented upon most favorably by several
newspapers, indicating clearly that the general senti-
ment in the country is favorable to the critics of the
Army and opposed to that section of the Army which has
advoceted extreme reactionary principles.
The Army has been somewhat embarrassed by these
demonstrations of lack of syanathy, and consequently
1s

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.98)
55-3
- 3 -
is apparently taking strong measures to eliminate
from the Army those elements which have called down
upon the Army the wrath of the public. The most
striking example of these measures is the sentencing
to death of Lieutenant-Colonel Aizawa, the murderer
of Lieutenant-General Nagata in August last (see
Embassy's despatch No. 1821, dated May 12, 1936).
This sentence, imposed by the First Division Court
Martial (held in camera) on iay 7, 1936, is much
more severe than the sentences imposed upon the young
Army and  avy officers involved in the murder of
Premier Inukai on may 15, 1932. 1t wil1 be remembered
that the young officers involved in that affair re-
ceived prison sentences of from one to fifteen years,
with, of course, conmutation of sentence for good be-
havior and by Imperial clemency, although the civi-
lian particinents in the Liey 15 affair, who were
tried in the civil courts, received life sentences.
The sentence imposed upon Colonel Aizawa is also
an indicetion of the severity of the sentences which
will be imposed upon the young officers involved in
the incident of February 26 and of the determination
of the Army to rid itself of those who are subjecting
the organization to the criticism of the public.
At the same time, the Army does not seem to be
greatly worried over the criticisms in the Diet end
the newspapers. It is proceeding calmly and smugly
on its way, believing thoroughly in its mission as
the savior of Japan. It continues to keep Tokyo in
the

=== Page 181 === (confidence 0.98)
554
- 4 -
the grip of martial law, has asked for larger appro-
priations than ever, has formulated end proposed bills
for the mobilization of industries, for the preserva-
tion of national industrial mobilization secrets and
for the control of "seditious literature". These meas-
ures, if passed, obviously would increase the power of
the Army to control the internel administration of the
country. In addition there have been inaugurated
so-called "Three-inister Conferences", composed of
the Ministers for Foreign ffairs, War and Navy, for
the purpose of discussing Japan's foreign policies.
The Three-inister Conferences have received criti-
cism in the Diet, but the Army and avy maintein that,
from the broad vievpoint of national defense, their
Ministers should have the right and op ortunity to
express their views in regard to foreign policy as
well as to internel administration.
It would aupear, therefore, that the military,
rather than being crushed by the shame of the February
26 incident, have in fact taken advantage of the inci-
dent to tighten their hold upon the Japanese Govern-
ment and to further the so-called "Showa Restoration"
(which in fact means the restoration of some form of
govermnent resembling the Shogunate). At the seme time,
it shoula not be forgotten that the militery sincerely
believe that the nation stands in grave danger of in-
volvement in a serious var in the not distant future
and that it is therefore of pressing necessity that
the

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.98)
555
- 5 -
the defense forces be augmented and the nation
unified under the mperor(with, of course, the
leadership of the military). The military's in-
sistence upon dominance in the Government can be
ascribed quite as ell to the immediate necessity,
real or fancied, of defense, as to the more vague
idea of a return of military government in Janan.
Economic, Social and Administrative Reforms.
As will be observed from a perusal of the ad-
dresses of the Premier and the Ministers for Foreign
Affairs,Finance and Jar before the iet on May 6
(Embassy's despatch 1.o. 1830, dated Mey 14, 1936),
no concrete measures ere announced for the execution
of the economic, social end administrative reforms
promised the nation by the Hirote Cabinet upon its
inauguration. The policies outlined in the speeches
before the Diet were vague and not very convincing,
and the Cabinet has not introduced any reform measures
of importance, the bulk of the bills introduced having
been carried over from the previous government.
The
only social reform bill of importance introduced by
the Hirota Cabinet is one compelling industrialists
to build up retirement allowance funds for their
employees.
this lack of concrete reform measures
has diseppointed the nation, which apparently hed
expected much of the present national or "trenscen-
dental" Cabinet. Several of the prominent newspapers,
including the ASAHI, the KOKUIN, the YOMIURI end the
JIJI

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.98)
556
- 6 -
JIJI, have conmented editorially upon the vagueness
of the speeches of the Ministers, the unsatisfactory
nature of their replies to interpellations and the
failure of the Cabinet to carry out the promised
administrative reforms. he TOKYO NICHI NICHI of
May 13 steted that the Army also is dissatisfied
with the lack of zeal for administrative reform
in the civilian government. The Army, according
to the newspaper, feels this esnecially strongly
because the Army itself has been endeavoring , with
success, to purge itself of the elements leading
to unrest and indiscipline. It believes that the
civilian autnorities should show the same zeal in
effecting reforms, instead of issuing vague promises
only.
Ir. Hirota's announced nolicy, however, was to
undertake economic and edministrative reforms slowly
and gradually, in order to avoid any great disturbance
of the life of the people, and in reply to interpella-
tions in the iet, the Cabinet Linisters involved have
stated that administrative reforms and changes in the
economic system will be studied after the present
session of the iet. Observers of the situation are
becoming of the opinion, however, that the Hirota
Cabinet is deliberately procrastinating in regard to
reform measures, in the expectation that the agitation
for such measures will die out, and that the Government
then will be able to maintain the status quo , with
only

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.96)
55-7
-7-
only such minor reform measures as may be foroed
upon the Government by discontented elements in
and out of the administration. The possibility
always exists, however, that if the Government
adopts such procrastinating and half-way tactics,
and instead of earnestly endeavoring to correct
the maladjustments and abuses in Japan, attempts
to suppress by use of force all symptoms of unrest
and discontent, there will arise other and perheps
worse incidents than those of .ay 15, 1932, and
ebruary 26, 1936.

FILED
April 30, 1936, in regard to the so-called "southward
advance" policy of Japen, now receiving some attention
in the Japanese press, I have the honor to report the
following developments in re ard to this policy.
Nevy Pamphlet.
The Japanese Navy has published a pamphlet which is
to be distributed to the public on avy Day (lay 27th).
An
F/FG

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
An advance copy has been received, however, by the Naval
Attaché of the Embassy, and a partial translation has
been made. The pamphlet discusses the international
situation, the dangers which beset Japan and the necessity
for the strengthening of naval armaments, and is ob-
viously designed to serve es propaganda for larger naval
eppropriations. The pemphlet, however, also discusses
briefly the "southward advance" policy under the heading
of "Urgont Mecessity for Oceanic Development". A trans-
lation of this paragraph, made by the ADVERTISER from a
IHOCHI review of the panmphlet, is as follows:
"Japan is small in area, poor in natural
resources end in much of the naterials necessary
for the livin of the people. Development of
nationel fortunes end netional defense depends
on imports from abroad. All of such materials
are produced by Japan's oceanic neighbors.
Consequently.to maintain the safety of the seas
and free importation of raw materials and ex-
change of products is to stabilize the living
of the people and assure replenishment of war
materials. In this respect, development of the
vast South Seas area, which still remains a
treasure trove awaiting exploitation, will not
only make a far-reaching contribution to the
solution of our population end food problem
but will accelerate the increase in Japan's
wealth. It constitutes a measure of the ut-
most urgency."
There is no indication in the pamphlet that the Navy
contemplates territorial acguisition in the South Seas,
although a hint of the probable use of force in ac-
quiring advantages in the South Seas is contained in an
earlier paragraph in the pemphlet, discussing Japan's
foreign policy, wherein it is stated that the authority
behind

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.95)
- 3 -
behind Japanese diplomacy should be a foreign policy
based on "justice and strength".
Statement of the Australia-Japan Society of Tokyo.
In order to allay misgivings felt in Australia in
regard to Japan's "southward advance" policy, the Aus-
tralia-Japan Society, whose president is Baron Yoshiro
Sakatani, on May 12, 1936, issued a statement in Japanese
and English, containing certain resolutions passed by
the directors of the Society. A clipping from the JAPAN
ADVERTISER of May 13, 1936, containing the text of the
1/
statement anu resolutions, is enclosed herewith. The
statement end resolutions review the frienaly relations
previously existing between Japan and Australia; regret
that these relations have been disturbed because of fears
in Australia that Japan is aibitious to acquire terri-
tory in the South Pacific; deplore the irresponsible
utterances and publications in Janan which have given
rise to these fears; state Janan's need for economic
expansion in the South Pacific; and deny any ambition
for territoriel ex ansion in that part of the world.
Respectfully yours,
Gor 6. iew
Joseph C. Grew.

factions, bureaucratists and political
parties" are declared to be the destroyers

Naval Treaty and ... the shifting of the

(Mazaki)". Mention is made of the "self-
sacrifice" of Lieutenant Colonel Aizawa.
The leaders of the coup declare that "the
time is ripe at last for manifestation and
realization of the national mission all
over the world".

DECLARATION
It is our humble belier that the divinity in our eot
is found in the essential character of the mation, which i
destined to grow and develop, in perfeet unity under the
whose line is unbroken for ages eternal, unt il it establint
the "One Universe Under one Crown". Such national dignity
has no parallel anywhere. Our country has steadily growm up
toward the achievement of its ideal ever sinee it was orested
by the Heavenly Aneestor and nationalized by the Emperor Jimmu,
and, coming through the Restoration of Meiji, has attained such
quick and finishing development that now the time is rips at last
for manifestation and realization of the national mission all
over the world.
Notwithstanding this, refractory and villainous men have
appeared one after another, indulging in the pursuit of their
self-interests and the fulfillment of their self-desires,
slighting the absolute augustness of His Majesty; going beyond
their powers, hampering the nation's growth, throwing people
into intolerable miseries, inviting foreign insults and causing
difficulties with foreign countries which are growing more ser-
ious day after day. The so-cal led Genro, chief vassals, cer-
tain military factions, bureaucratists and political parties are
the leaders of these destroyers of the national glory.
They trespassed upon the prerogative of the Emperor's
supreme command, among others, in the conclusion of the London
Naval Treaty and in the shifting of the Inspector-General of
Militayy Education; they attempted secretly to steal the
supreme command in the March incident; they united with dis-
loyal professors in rebellious plots. These are a few of the
most remarkable inatances of their  willainies. Thm ir drimes
make us shed bloody tears in uncontrollable anger. No wonder
that Nakaoka, Sagoya and the Blood Confederacy first set
examples, followed by the self-saorificing attempt of the May
15 incident, and lately by Lieutenant-Colonel Aisawa. Yet the
repeated bloody sacrifices have not proved enough to makm the
villains reflect, who still continue to remain in their self-
appointed high positions.
From outside our divine country, entrusted to us by our
Ancestors, is in danger of being exploded to complete ruin by
just one friction after another with Russia, China, England
or America.
The country is in grave danger from within and from with-
out. If the disloyal and untrustworthy villains who are caus-
ing destruction of the country are not promptly suppressed, if
the traitors who could the august virtue of His Majesty and
prevent the restoration are not eliminated, the Imperial rule
will be made vain.
The first army division, stationed in the capital, has
received Imperial Orders to go abroad. We of one mind in the
determination to saerifice our lives in service to Imparial
cause of Restoration are ordered to start on a distant ex-
pedition. But our anxiety over the unspeakable conditien at
home is too great. We believe that it is t he duty of His
Majesty's servants to remove the traitors and evil-deers clome
to the Throne, and to destroy their headquerters. If we he
tate to disoharge our first duty as trusted retainers now

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
Athe opportunity for averting ruin and vice will be
Hereby we of one mind rise for the common cause an
sasrifice everything in the punishment of the treaoheroms su
jeets with death in the olarification of the relations of
sovereign and subjeots, and in defense of our country
pray for approval and assistance of the Spirits of Imperial
Ancestors.
Captain Shiro Noguchi and others.

The attached paper is from

(Initials) N.T.T.

Subjeot:
Further Developments in the Situation
Created by the February 26 Incident.
No. 1900, June 12, 1936.
Concise resumé of contents:
This despatch contains little new in the
way of information, but it gives a very good
picture of the stresses among the elements in
control or seeking control of the nation; of
the conviction on all sides that some fundamental
change is needed within the nation, and the groping
for a program which will bring about that change;
of a perplexed civil government faced by serious
problems of diplomacy and finance, goaded by a
military machine that not only challenges the
authority of the civil government but threatens
to get out of control of its leaders; of a people
accustomed to follow docilely its leaders,
bewildered by the strife among those leaders and
disturbed by demonstrations of unrest at home
and by dire predictions of trouble abroad.
894.00/658
Recommendation of action:
Secretary's instruotion:
