The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
In the past month there has been no change in the
general tendency of the internal political situation
which resulted from the February 26 Incident and which
was reported to the Department in the Embassy's des-
645
6.51
patches No. 1784/of April 17 and No. 1833 of May 14, 1936.
The developments of the last four weeks have demonstrated
the

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the changed yet nebulous situation that arose as an after-
math of the insurrection. Specifically, the Government has
continued to express its desire for social and economic re-
form though it has as yet given very little indication of
lhaving evolved any definite plan; the authorities of the
Army have repeatedly reiterated their intention of purging
its ranks of unruly elements and at the same time have ex-
pressed their disapproval of the Government's vague program
of "renovation" and the failure of the political parties to
carry out needed reforms in their midst, particularly in
view of reforms being carried out by the Arny; the Navy,
which occupies a different position fron that of the Army
by virtue of its lack of guilt in the February 26 affair,
has showm concern lest in the future it be contaminated by
political intriguers such as the naval cadets involved in
the May ls affair; and the political parties have remained
tolerant of the Government's delay in action while expressing
the conviction that the Cabinet must soon bring about a re-
organization of the social and economic order and that the
Arny must carry out a thorough "catharsis" to avoid serious
trouble in the future.
In the Army the more moderate element has gained con-
trol. However, recent statements of higher Army officials
and the general trend of events have conclusively shown
that the Army as an entity is more in the saddle now than
The ASAHI of
it was before the February 26 Incident.*
May 25 reported that Army leaders were preparing proposals
for the renovation of Japan's administrative structure be-
cause of their dissatisfaction with the progress made thus
far by the Government for the attainment of the drastic re-
forms
* Embassy's despa tch No. 1833, May 14, 1936.

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
forms needed to tide over the delicate aftermath of the
February 26 Incident.
While administrative reforms continued to be a major
part of the military platform, the Army seemed to be more
interested at the moment in the strengthening of national
defense. In explaining this attitude, the YOMIURI of May 28
stated that the Army had decided to give precedence to the
defense program because definite figures on future military
expenditures would be essential for the working out of the
Governient's financial arrangements. According to the same
newspa er, the Army plans to submit to the Government about
the middle of June a six-year program of military expansion
with an expenditure of 2,000,000,000, this progran to in-
clude a strengthening of the air force, un increase in modern
armanents, and
improvemonts in the military machine in
Manchuria.
Aside from concessions demanded for its ovm direct
benefit, the Army is reported to be insistent upon the
esteblishment of a firmer diplomc tic policy which will
apply not only to Soviet Russia and to China but to the
whole world. It will be recalled that the execution of
a "positive diplomatic policy" was one of the conditions
laid down by General Terauchi for the Army's cooperation
with the present Government.
As reported by the JAPAN ADVERTISER of June 3 and the
JAPAN CiRCMIOLE of June 4, the War Minister told his Cabinet
colleagues at a meeting held on June 2 that while the Army
was making efforts to enforce stricter military discipline,
the political parties had done nothing to bring about
parallel reforms in their own ranks.
Outside

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Outside of the declarations of the War Minister con-
cerning the purging of the Army now alleged to be in
progress, the Embassy has been able to gather little in-
formation in this respect in the past month. The forced
resignation of higher Ary officials, the wholesale arrest
of civilians suspected of complicity in the February 26
insurrection, the condemnation to death by Court Martial
of Lieutenant Colonel izawa, and the rigid stipulations
made for the trial of the officers involved in the Incident
have already been reported to the Department. As yet noth-
ing is known concerning the results of the Court Martial
now being held for the February 26 rebel officers. However,
the Lnbassy expects to obtain some infommation on this sub-
ject within a few days.
In keeping with the position taken by the Army ever
since the present Cabinet was formed, the Army is now
reportedly more carnest than ever in its desire for politi-
cal reforms. DoiiI of June 7 asserted that unless Premier
Hirota drafted plcis for submission at the next Diet session
for the political reforms demanded when the Cabinet was
formed, military support of the. Government would be with-
drawn. The rmy's rinei1 demands, it will be remembered,
were the followin: clarificetion of the nati onal polity;
adequate national defense; stabilization of the livelihood of
the people, particularly by a fundamental readjustment of
the system of taxation with the object of reducing the
burdens on the masses and increasing the burdens of the
wealthier classes; and renovation of Japan's diplomatic
policy.
The

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
The Navy Minister was likewise reported to be desirous
of maintaining strict discipline in the Navy and of having
the Government reform the general administration and read-
just the financial and taxation systems on the basis of
"defense first". According to the JAPA ADVERTISER of
June 9, at a meeting on the previous day, Admiral Nagano
isaii
told chiefs of naval staffs that he had already issued
instructions regarding the February 26 outbreak; that all
officers and men must remember these instructions and make
discipline more stringent; and that his hearers must not
be led by arguments into taking part in political movements
but must devote thenselves exclusively to their duties as
naval officers.
No change has been noted in the attitude of the politi-
cal parties toward the pre cnt Government since the con-
clusion of the Diet session.* The parties are still toler-
ant of the Government yet are o inous in their warnings
for the future should the Govermnent fail to carry out its
promises. For example, Dr. Suzuki, President of the Seiyukai,
Made a speech on May Be before 2o0 me.ibers of his Farty in
which he stressed the Government's need to reform the exist-
ing political and social order and to bring about a purging
of the Army, addine that any further delay in these matters
micht prove fatal to the nation.
Officials of other Departments have also civen promises
to maintain public order so as to avoid further direct action
outbreaks. In an address delivered on June 8 before ju-
dicial officers, including the President of the Supreme
Court, the Minister of Justice stated that however right
or
* Embassy's des atch No. 1869, May 29, 1936.

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
or noble might be the motives of law violators any attempt
on their part to resort to irrational or direct action meth-
ods should be strictly punished in accordance with the laws
on the statute books.
The ASAiI of June 5 reported that the
Police Bureau of the Home Office was worried about possible
seditious political activities upon the lifting of martial
law and was making preparations for adequate preventive
measures. It is understood that Prelier Hirota, in his
speech before the Frefectural Governors at a Conference to
be called on June 15, will emphasize the necessity of ab-
solute suppression of vild or seditious rumors, so as to re-
move any possiblc public unrest and fear that might result
from the ending of martial law.
There is no doubt that Cabinet members, higher Army
officials and officials of the Justice and Home Affairs
Ministries are fully cocnizant of their responsibilities at
the present juneture. They have repeatedly asserted that
the reforns desired by a large section of the Army and of
the people would be carried out and that every precaution
would be taken against the recurrence of an affair similar
to that of February 26. It still remains to be seen whether
these authorities will be able to meet the present internal
political situation with success, particularly in view of
the almost chaotic state of public opinion. Reforms of the
kind desired by the February 26 insurrectionists are ap-
parently in vogue, yet the insurrectionists themselves are
still heartily condemned; the Governnent, both upon its
organization and during the recent Diet session, has con-
sistently adnitted the need for a change in the status quo
yet

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
yet has done nothing in a concrete way toward effecting
this; and the Army, more firmly entrenched than ever on
account of the repercussions of the February 26 Incident
and the uncertain international situation, is continually
balancing the reform within its own ranks against the
Governnent's pronise of social and economic reform to
fulfil the imnediate demands of the emergency situation
of the past three months.
Under such circunstances it should never be for-
gotten that regardless of all official asseverations
and official  recautions it is possible for a handful
of properly inspired and well-organized "petriots" to
upset the predictions of any political observer.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 222 === (confidence 1.00)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE

August 5, 1936.
Tokyo's 1935 of July 8, 1936,
"Aftermath of the February 26 Incident":
This is a very useful and valuable
despatch, but it is somewhat too long and
the information contained in it might well
have been compressed.
Tre Embassy points out that the men
found guilty in connection with the
February 26 Incident were condemned not on
account of the assassinations but for
infraction of military discipline and for
use of the army without Imperial sanction.
The sentences were received by the press
and by the public favorably, one paper
remarking that "there is satisfaction and
relief that proper punishment has now been
meted out". The Embassy then proceeds to
examine the extent to which the "platform"
of the Hirota cabinet, which was called
into being on account of the Incident, has
been carried out. The platform, it is
reported, has been clarified by the setting
up of the following policies:
1) Increase of defensive
equipment;
2) Readjustment of taxation,
including a tax increase;
3)

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
3) Nationalization of electric
power;
4) Central control of fuel;
5) Extension of the term of
compulsory education;
6) Revision of the agricultural
village and land system.
With regard to defensive equipment,
it appears that a five-year scheme involving
the expenditure of two billion yen for the
improvement of the army, was later modified
to involve the expenditure of three billion
yen. It is not clear whether the latter
amount is to be expended over a period of
five years or over a period of ten years.
The problem of readjustment of taxation
is not of immediate interest to us.
With regard to nationalization of
electric power, a plan has been formulated
to set up one large company, to be controlled
by the government, which will absorb the
existing electric power companies. In
this connection press reports mention another
plan, which is that there is to be
organized an operating company which will
rent the power plants of the existing
companies and have a monopoly of the sale
of industrial power.
In

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
In connection with the control of fuel,
special emphasis is being laád on extraction
of oil by liquefaction of coal, manufacture
of synthetic gasoline, and the utilization
of substitute fuels, such as alcohol.
There is some discussion of the need
for reforming the administrative side of
the government; one plan put forward by
the army would call for the creation of an
independent ministry of air. There is also
talk of the creation of a ministry of health,
the army authorities being somewhat concerned
over the increasing number of young men
unfit for military duty.
A brief portion of the despatch is set
aside for a discussion of the foreign
policy of Mr. Hirota (see page 12) but it
is couched in somewhat vague language.
Perhaps one of the most interesting
portions of this despatch is that which
describes the effect of the Incident on the
commercial firms. Mitsui has replaced many
of its older principal officials with
younger men. They also announce that they
will give the public an opportunity to
acquire shares in Mitsui firms. Several of
the large financial houses state that they
will cooperate with the army in maintaining
the national defenses by operating aviation
and other similar enterprises without undue
regard

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.95)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 4 -
regard to the profit motive.
The despatch need not be read.

February 26, had on July 5, 1936, pronounced judgment upon
those indicted. The sentences included 17 death penalties,
13 imposed on officers of the Army and 4 on civilian insti-
gators; 5 sentences to life imprisonment, all imposed on
officers; and 54 prison sentences of from 18 months to 15
years, imposed on one officer, 44 non-commissioned officers,
3 privates, and 6 civilians. Thirty of the shorter prison
sentences, however, are only nominal, having been imposed
with stay of execution.
In imposing these sentences, according to the War of-
fice announcement, the Court Martial took into consideration
the purity of the motives of the young officers involved,
but could not release them from responsibility for having
infringed the Imperial Rescript on military discipline and
for having employed units of the Imperial Army without Im-
perial sanction. The non-commissioned officers who were
sentenced to imprisonment without stay of execution, were
found to have engaged deliberately in the insurrection, while
the non-commissioned officers and privates who were sentenced
with stay of execution (which means that they are placed on
probation) were found to have been forced into the insurrec-
tion against their wishes. The 47 non-commissioned officers
and privates who were acquitted were found to have simply
obeyed the orders of their superior officers without any
knowledge that they were engaging in an insurrection.
With the announcement of the judgment the War Office
issued

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
issued two statements, one of which gives the details of
the plot(the purpose of which was to renovate the nation
by removing certain representatives of the privileged
classes) and the other of which describes the motives of
1/
the insurgent troops. There are enclosed clippings from
the JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 8, 1936, giving details of
the judgment of the Court Martial and translations of the
other two statements. It will be observed that the motives
as discovered by the Court Martial agree very closely with
those found by the Embassy in conversations with Japanese
officials and others shortly after the Incident and reported
to the Department in previous despatches.
It is believed that the recent execution of Lieutenant
Colonel Saburo Aizawa for the murder of Lieutenant General
Nagata in August last, and the sentencing of the 13 officers
to death because of the February 26 Incident, create a pre-
cedent in Japan. As far as the Embassy is aware or able to
ascertain, no Army or Navy officer in the past has been
sentenced to death because of a patriotic assassination. The
execution of Aizawa and the sentencing of young officers,
therefore, indicate a realization on the part of the Army
authorities of the dangers involved in permitting ultra-
nationalists to instigate irresponsible persons to direct
action methods and shows a determination on the part of the
nation as a whole to do away with government by assassination.
It should be noted, however, that the judgment of the Court
Martial does not mention the assassinations and imposed the
sentences

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
sentences upon the participants in the uprising on the
grounds of infringement of the Imperial Rescript on military
discipline and utilization of the Imperial Army without Im-
perial sanction.
The sentences imposed by the Court Martial upon the
participants and instigators of the uprising of February 26
were received with unanimous approval by the local newspapers.
Following are a few excerpts from editorials, illustrating
this fact:
ASAHI, July 7.
"It is easy for the public to see the care which
the authorities took to deal with the case correct-
. From the attitude of the authorities
toward the case and the manner in which the people
regarded the handling of the case by the authorities,
it may not be too much to hope that great things
may come of the effort to settle the aftermath of
the incident and put a new face on the situation."
JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL, July 7.
"There is deep regret that the February tragedy
took place; there is satisfaction and relief that
proper punishment has now been meted out. .
Japan can and will breathe easily and with a light-
er conscience now than it has for some time past."
JIJI, July 8.
"The present sentences reflect a correct inter-
pretation by the Army of the national laws and will
certainly aid in putting the public mind at rest. It
is no exaggeration to say that public expectations
and reliance on the Army's determination to carry out
a thorough-going reform have increased considerably
as the result of the Court Martial."
HOCHI, July 8.
"Those involved in the uprising were undoubtedly
driven by ideals of patriotism and loyalty to the
Emperor, but their reckless abuse of the Imperial
troops and weapons in an attempt to upset the national
construction is absolutely unpardonable. . .
The present sentences will satisfy the Army and the
public."
NICHI

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
NICHI NICHI, July 8.
"When the Court's attitude toward the incident
is considered, one finds it impossible to be dis-
satisfied with the decision.
Post-Incident Policies.
It will be remembered that during the formation of the
Hirota Cabinet shortly after the Incident of February 26,
the Army was reported to have demanded of the new Cabinet
the adoption of certain basic policies as a price for the
Army's co-operation. These were reported to be, (1) clari-
fication of the national polity by rejection of the "Minobe
theory", (2) completion of national defense, (3) stabiliza-
tion of the livelihood of the people through economic and
social reforms, and (4) the adoption of a "positive" foreign
policy. After the formation of the Cabinet, Mr. Hirota on
various occasions announced his policies, which can be sum-
marized as follows: (1) clarification of the national polity
and upholding of constitutional Government, (2) perfection of
national defense, (3) strengthening of national finances
through the reform of taxation, renovation of the banking sys-
tem and the enhancement of the nation's economic strength,
(4) reform and unification of administrative organs and (5)
the adoption of a unified, independent and positive foreign
policy. However, in accordance with Mr. Hirota's announced
policy of "making haste slowly", the Hirota Cabinet has as
yet undertaken few concrete measures for the execution of the
above policies, and has consequently been subjected to consi-
derable criticism from the military, the newspapers and other
sources. Since the close of the recent Diet session, however,
Mr. Hirota has taken the problem of administrative and other
reforms

=== Page 231 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
reforms in hand. It was announced some weeks ago that the
Cabinet would consider reform measures proposed by the
various Ministries at a series of meetings beginning July
10, and since that time the newspapers have published
pages of reform measures, allegedly emanating from the
Ministries concerned, to be placed before the Cabinet. It
is difficult to judge the truth in the welter of conjecture,
but DOMEI reported that Cabinet bureau chiefs had decided
to accept and consider the following "truly important" poli-
cies:
1. Increase of Defensive Equipment, submitted by the
War and Navy Ministries.
2.
Readjustment of taxation, including a tax increase,
submitted by the Finance Ministry.
3.
Nationalization of electric power, submitted by
the Communications Ministry.
4.
Centl control of fuel, submitted by the Commerce
and  dustry Ministry.
5.
Extension of the term of compulsory education, sub-
mitted by the Education Ministry.
6.
Revision of the agricultural village and land sys-
tem, submitted by the Agriculture and Forestry
Ministry.
(1). Increase of Defensive Equipment.
Shortly after the Incident, it was reported that the
Army's plan for completion of national defense involved the
expenditure of ¥2,000,000,000 over a period of five or six
years, causing an increase of annual expenditure of from
¥300,000,000 to ¥400,000,000. Later it was reported in the
newspapers that the Army defense scheme would cost ¥3,000,000,
ooo, which, spread over five years, would cause an increased
annual

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
annual expenditure of ¥600,000,000. It now appears that
it has been decided that such an additional annual ex-
penditure would be too great for the finances of the coun-
try, and the HOCHI of July 9 stated that the ¥3,O00,000,000
five-year plan will be extended to a ten-year plan, thus
making the annual increase of expenditure only ¥300,000,000.
Details of the Army's plan are not available but it is believed
that a large part of the increased expenditure for the first
five years will be devoted to the strengthening of the Army's
air force and the increasing of the Japanese military forces
in Manchuria to approximately the same strength as that of
the Soviet Far Eastern army.
2. Readjustment of Taxation.
The complete plan of the Minister for Finance for the
readjustment of taxation has not been made public but
various newspapers have published alleged summaries of the
plan. From these it appears that the personal income, corpo-
ration income, business profits, capital interest, inheri-
tance, and extraordinary profit taxes will be raised, as
well as the various excise taxes. It is also proposed to
levy new taxes on articles of luxury, gasoline, matches,
wheat gluten and saccharine. It is reported in the newspapers
that the house taxes, the proceeds of which now go to the
prefectures, will be transferred to the central government
and that the Minister for Finance wishes to transfer to the
central treasury the profits made by the railways, post of-
fices, telephones and telegraphs, and to increase those
profits

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
profits by raising the postal rates and railway fares.
It is also reported, however, that the Finance Minister
will encounter considerable objection in the execution of
the above plan. The prefectures and other local governmental
organs object to the appropriation by the central government
of the house tax, unless the central government will re-
imburse them in some way. It is also reported that the rail-
ways and communications organs object strenuously to the ap-
propriation of their entire profits, alleging that such ap-
propriation will mean deterioration of their services and
that, moreover, the welfare of the employees of these organs
should be considered before appropriating all profits for
the central government's use.
3. Nationalization of Electric Power.
The Minister for Communications has a plan (which is
meeting with considerable opposition from the capitalists)
for the nationalization of all existing and propsective electric
power plants. The plan contemplates the formation of a large
company, which will be controlled by the Government, and the
amalgamation into this company of all the existing electric
power companies. The purpose of the plan is to effect econo-
mies in electrical generation and transmission, to provide
abundant and cheap electrical power for the industries of
Japan, to assist national defense by developing industries,
and to promote the welfare of the rural districts by supply-
ing them with cheap electrical power for local industries.
4. Central Control of Fuel.
The Minister for Commerce and Industry, it is reported,
plans

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
plans the organization of a strong, independent central
bureau to develop an effective fuel policy. This bureau
would be composed of officials of the Ministry of Commerce
and Industry but also would have as advisers high officials
of the Departments of War, Navy, and Finance. The bureau
would have jurisdiction over all matters pertaining to fuel,
with especial emphasis on liquefaction of coal, the manu-
facture of synthetic gasoline and the utilization of substi-
tute fuels such as alcohol. The basic aim is to achieve
self-sufficiency in liquid fuels, but, as the oil resources
of the nation will not permit this, the bureau is to encourage
the liquefaction of coal and the manufacture of alcohol. It
is planned that it will be made compulsory by law to mix al-
cohol with gasoline at the rate of 30% alcohol and 70% gaso-
line. To obtain funds for the purpose of subsidizing the
undertakings mentioned above it is proposed to increase the
import duty on gasoline. The bureau would also supervise
and encourage the development of oil resources at home and
abroad.
It is reported that the defense ministries approve this
plan in view of the need for a more effective national fuel
policy and that the plan is supported particularly by the
Navy.
5. Extension of the Term of Compulsory Education.
The Minister for Education, it is reported, has a plan
for the extension of the period for compulsory education
from the present six years to eight years, with the purpose
of

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
of increasing the general education and the resultant effi-
ciency of the people, and also to provide more time for the
inculcation of correct ideas of the national polity. The
plan is meeting with some opposition, particularly from the
Minister for Finance, on the ground that national finances
at the present time will not permit the additional expendi-
ture required.
6. Revision of the Agricultural Village and Land System.
No announcement has yet been made of this plan but from
various speeches made by the Minister for Agriculture and
Forestry, it is assumed that the plan envisages measures to
improve agricultural management, to promote rural small-scale
industries, to minimize the effect of national calamities,
and to improve generally the economic status of the farm-
ing communities.
It is probable that, after deliberation of these and
other plans by the Cabinet has commenced, more detailed and
accurate information in regard to the schemes will be pub-
lished in the newspapers. The Embassy will watch the develop-
ments carefully and will report the outcome in regard to each
of the schemes by separate despatch.
Administrative Reform.
It is reported in the press that the Prime Minister,
Mr. Hirota, is very enthusiatic in regard to reforms in the
administrative organization of the Government. He has
found, however, that there are so many conflicting ideas as
to the reforms necessary, that he has decided, for the present
at least, not to take any action. As was previously reported,
the

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
the War Office has a plan for a Minister without portfolio
to act as Vice-Premier and chief of the Cabinet Investiga-
tion Bureau. The purpose of this plan is to coordinate re-
form measures and to avoid the separation of the spheres of
influence of the various Ministries. The ASAHI of June 19
reported that Mr. Hirota had agreed to this plan, but later
reports have stated that the Cabinet as a whole does not
favor the scheme.
The Army also has a plan for the creation of a Ministry
of Air, for the encouragement and promotion of civilian avia-
tion. The Army, it is reported, is very anxious to see this
plan materialize and is expected to press the other Ministries
concerned for its adoption. A third plan of the Army contem-
plates the creation of a Ministry of Health, for the purpose
of promoting the welfare of laborers, the general maintenance
of health among the populace, and physical training for the
youth of the nation. It is believed that the Army's enthusiasm
for this plan is based on the fact that, during the recent
examination of young men for conscription in the Army, there
an
was /unusually large percentage of those rejected for reasons
of health. The newspapers reported that 400 per 1000 of the
young men were rejected and that it was discovered that 24
per lo00 have tuberculosis.
Schemes have also been advanced for the merging of various
Ministries in order to unify and coordinate governmental af-
fairs, but it is not believed that the Cabinet will take any
action on these plans for some time to come.
Post-

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.99)
- 12 -
Post-Incident Foreign Policy.
The ASAHI reported on June 19, 1936, that, as envy
and suspicion had been aroused among the Powers by Japan's
national advance, and as the improvement of the international
position of the Soviet Union and the propaganda of that
Power had increased the misunderstanding of Japan among the
nations, the following plans would be adopted on the basis
of the "independent and positive" foreign policy of Mr.
Hirota: (1) the readjustment of relations between Japan and
China and Japan and the Soviet Union, on the basis of the
inseparable relations between Japan and "Manchukuo" and the
recognition of Japan as the stabilizinginfluence in the
Far East; (2) the expansion of trade diplomacy, on the
basis of free trade and reduction of tariff walls; and (3)
revision of the organization of the Foreign Office.
On June 23, 1936, DOMEI reported that, at a conference
of Foreign Office staff members, it was decided that, in ac-
cordance with Mr. Hirota's plan for an independent, positive
foreign policy, the following should form the basis of Japan's
diplomacy: (1) maintenance and development of the right to
racial existence through establishment of a continental
policy centering on China and Soviet Russia; (2) strengthening
of the nation's commercial policy in order to cope with the
worldwide tendency to restrict imports of Japanese goods.
Effect of the Incident on Commercial Firms.
A somewhat peculiar result of the February 26 Incident
lies in the effect which it has had upon the great financial
and

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.99)
- 13 -
and commercial houses, especially the Mitsui, Mitsubishi,
and Sumitomo interests. Evidently realizing from this
incident, as well as that of May 15, 1932, that there existed
a strong antipathy against the great financial houses among
the military and the people as a whole, these interests have
apparently agreed upon a concerted line of action to meet
the situation. As a first step, they retired some of the
older and leading figures in their organizations, substitut-
ing for them younger men, who, it apparently is hoped, will
be more in step with the times. As a second step, they an-
nounced that, instead of monopolizing the shares of some of
their profit-making subsidiary concerns, they would sell a
portion of these shares to the public. These steps were
obviously taken for the purpose of conciliating the general
public, principally by removing the control of the interests
from the hands of a few men, and by allowing the public to
participate in the profits of their enterprises.
Later the financial houses took a third step, apparent-
ly intended to conciliate the military. This took the form
of an announcement that they would in the future use a part
of their capital in state defense projects, disregarding
the question of profits on their investments. Thus they
stated that they would give financial aid, regardless of pro-
fit, to private airplane manufacturing companies or civil
aviation companies, with the view of assisting the Govern-
ment, and especially the Army, in developing civil aviation
in Japan. It has also been renorted in the newspapers that
the Mitsui interests have appropriated Yen 2,000,000 as a
preliminary

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.98)
- 14 -
preliminary expenditure in embarking upon an automobile
manufacturing enterprise. This enterprise, it is hoped,
will produce 5,ooo cars per annum at first, with a goal
of 10,000 cars per annum later. The concern is to be
established with no intention of gaining a profit but solely
to assist in the national defense program, according to the
press, and is a part of the Mitsui plan of utilizing its
vast resources for the materialization of national policies.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

Among the privates, Yokeel
came to find in them bosom friends
with whom he shared the same views.
They arrived at the conclusion that
the authorities concerned lacked ap-
propriate measures to cope with the
situation, that Japan's domestic and
foreign policies were deadlocked, that
the political parties were thinking only
of their own interests and ignoring the
danger in which the country was plac-
ed and that the so-called financial cli-
que was slighting the straitened cir-
cumstances of the people.
"They also were of the opinion that
the prerogatives of the Supreme Com-
mand had been violated when the
London naval treaty was concluded.
They attributed this state of affairs to
insincerity on the part of the states-
men close to the Throne (jushin), the
bureaucrats. the military clique. the
political parties and the financial cli-
que toward the principles of the na-
tional structure. They thought that to
give lustre to the real nature of the
Japanese Constitution. which provides
for unbroken rule throughout the
ages. it was necessary to renovate the
nation by striking at the so-called pri-
vileged classes.
Encouragement Given
"Their sentiments were stimulated
further when. in 1934. Koji Muranaka
and Asaichi Isobe were dismissed from
military service on the charge of hav-
ing distributed 'mysterious documents.'
When their siperior officers intended
to suppress their movement. the re-
action was great. This tended to en-
courage the movement. Still further
encouragement was given by the clari-
fication of the national polity in con-
nection with the co"stitutional theory
of the Emperor.
"When the Inspector-General of Mili-
tary Elucation (General Jinzaburo
Mazaki) was transfer: ed. they listened
to an i: flammatory :uggestion made in
a certain quarter of the public and
concluded that the Supreme Command
had been violated. Their indignation
at this neared the breaking poit.
They were further move! by Lieute-
nant-C lon:l Aizawa's assassination of
Lieutenant-Gene al Tetsuzan Nagata.
the: dire tor of the military affairs
bureau of the War Office. They came
to the conclusion that behind the vio-
lation of the prerogative of the Su-
preme Command were the bloe of se-
rior statesmen and the financial clique.
They had the idea that the senior
statesmen were responsible especially
for th violation of the Supreme Com-
mand since the conclusion of the Lon-
don nava! treaty.
"They thought that these men exist-
ed above the national Constitution and
that to str:ke at them by lega! means
would serve no purpose in gaining
their obji etive. To strike at the senior
statesmen, they concluded, it would be
necessary to transcend the Constitution
anl use part of the Army in direct
action. They thought this a popular
way to cop with the existing emer-
gency. By so acting. they planned to
clarify the national polity. replenish
national defense and stabilize national
living. They intended in this manner
to urge high elements in the army to
bring about the Showa Restoration.
"Tsuguo Takeshima, Seichu Nibu,
Naoshi Sakài. Hachiro Hayashi. Masaru
Tanaka. Kanji Nakajima. Masaru Ya-
suda. Taro Takahshi. Minoru Tokiwa.
Toshihiko Ikeda (former officers) and
Mata Yamamoto (civilian) also arrived
at the conclusion that the situation in
Japan ran counter to the principles of
the national structure. They had long
felt the necessity for elimination of the
so-calle! privileged classes and for
urging realization of the Showa Res-
toration. From 1933. they were influ-
enced by the ideas of Koji Muranaka
and others, association with whom led
to their recognition of the righteous-
ness of direct action."

GRAY. The War Office issued a communique last night
894.00/666
to the effect that the army court martial now sitting in
Tokyo has sentenced six other military officers to varying
terms f imprisonment from life in one case to four years
for their connection with the February incident. END GRAY.
The Embassy is informed on what seems to be rellable
authority that the investigaticn wil? continue and that
other officers probably including Gee al Mazaki will be
brought to trial.

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 15, 1936.
RCV
SKH:
Tokyo's despatch No. 1952 of July 22,
1936, supplements a previous despatch in
which there was discussed the plans of the
present government to implement the policies
which were announced at the time of the
setting up of the present government
immediately after the February 26 incident.
The Embassy confirms a statement made
in the previous despatch that the Japanese
public has overwhelmingly approved of the
punishment of the February 26 conspirators.
Martial law was ended on July 17.
In a previous despatch it was stated
that the Cabinet was giving consideration
to a number of projects of reform and
improvement. It appears, however, that so
many projects have been suggested that
friction developed within the Cabinet.
This method of procedure has been dropped,
and there has been substituted instead the
plan whereby the various projects have been
submitted by the Individual ministries to
the Prime Minister and to the Minister of
Finance, who are then to select those
projects which they consider merit con-
sideration by the Cabinet as a whole.
The

=== Page 250 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
The principal conclusion drawn by the
press from these developments is that the
overnment has capitulated to the army and
navy with regard to the approval of the
national defense estimates, and that the
needs of the other ministries will be
satisfied so far as possible after the
requirements of the army and navy have
been met.
If the conclusion above referred to is
correct, it is obvious that a number of
projects for social and economic reform
wili have to be either abandoned or
drastically curtailed. The army is said
to have taken the position that if
revenues are not sufficient to meet defense
requirements, there should be a drastic re-
form of the economic structure of the
country.
Military and naval estimates: I
suggest that you read this portion of the
despatch, beginning on page 6.
The Embassy's comment on page 9 of
the despatch is also worth reading.

OF STATE
With reference to the Embassy's despatch No. 1935,
AC/C
ated July 8, 1936, and to previous despatches in regard
OF
o the political situation in Japan resulting from the
1LED
C2
ncident of February 26, 1936, I have the honor to report
A
SSRY
E
that several important developments have occurred during
the past two weeks.
Execution of Officers and Civilians Implicated.
At 6 p.m., July 12, 1936, the War Office issued a
brief statement to the effect that fifteen persons sentenced
to death on July 5, 1935, for participation in the uprising
of February 26, had been executed on July 12, 1936. There
followed the names of the fifteen persons, thirteen of whom
were
F/FG

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
were former officers of the Army and two of whom were
civilians. No mention was made of the other two civilians
sentenced to death on July 5, and it is presumed that
their execution is being postponed for the purpose of
eliciting further information from them. This inference
is supported by the fact that the two who were not executed
were designated in the Court Martial judgment as "ringleaders",
while the two who were executed were designated as partici-
pants".
The execution of the sentences upon the fifteen partici-
pants in the incident created practically no excitement
in Japan. The public was well prepared for the sentences
and their execution, and there were no public outcries
against the punishment and no signs (as far as the Embassy
is aware) of any public resentment. There have been no at-
tempts, as yet, to make martyrs of the persons executed,
and such small comment as has appeared in the newspapers has
been favorable to the judgment of the Army authorities and
the execution thereof. The public appears to be pleased
that the incident was settled as it was and to be satisfied
that the sentences were imposed and executed in good faith
and with justice.
Martial law in Tokyo, which, according to rumors, would
not be lifted until after the execution of the death son-
tences on the participants in the incident, was lifted at
midnight of July 17, 1936, approximately four months and a
half after imposition. Literature critical of the existing
authority, public meetings, etc., which were prohibited under
martial

=== Page 253 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
martial law, will again be permitted, but it has been an-
nounced that the police will cooperate with the gendarmerie
to eliminate seditious utterances and that seditious litera-
ture will be controlled under the *Seditious Literature Law
recently approved by the Diet.
Political and Social Reformation.
As was reported in despatch No. 1935, it had been de-
cided that the Cabinet as a whole was to consider the various
administrative and social reforms proposed by the various
Ministries, beginning with the Cabinet meeting of July 10.
However, after the meeting of that date, it was announced
that the Cabinet had abandoned the policy of consideration,
by the Cabinet as a whole, of the national policy plans of
the Ministries, because of the excessive number of such plans
(the newspapers stated that there were over thirty plans of
importance), of friction among the members of the Cabinet,
and of the dissatisfaction of the defense Ministries, who
wanted priority given their plans and budgets. Instead of
joint consideration of the various plans, therefore, the
Ministries were to submit their individual plans in writing
to the Premier and to the Minister for Finance, who would
judge the importance of the plans and submit those deemed
worthy of consideration and feasible to the Cabinet Council.
It was reported in the newspapers that the plans for
administrative and social reformation submitted by the
various Ministries consisted principally of the usual acti-
vities of those Ministries in an amplified form, requiring
more

=== Page 254 === (confidence 1.00)
more funds for execution. This is illustrated by the
following list of measures proposed by the Ministries,
according to the HOCHI of July 18:
"Nationalization of the electric power
industry and promotion of aviation and
shipping, proposed by the Communications
Ministry; increase in the number of judi-
cial officials and higher salaries, Jus-
tice Ministry; extension of the period
of compulsory education, Education Minis-
try; enactment of the farm law, the farm
debt re-adjustment law, mutual aid farm
insurance law, a forestry insurance law
and a policy for the promotion of sheep
raising, Agriculture and Forestry Ministry;
a health promotion program and a five-
year plan for public works, Home Ministry;
perfection of national defense facilities,
administrative reform, stabilization of the
national livelihood and establishment of
Japan's Manchukuo policy, War Office; a
defense perfection plan from the Navy Office;
measures for strengthening the East Asia
policy and economic policy, Foreign Office;
promotion of foreign trade, acquisition of
important ources of raw materials and re-
lief of th small and moderate-sized business
and industrial interests, Commerce and Industry
Ministry; encouragement of emigration and develop-
ment of the South Seas, Overseas Ministry, and
tax readjustment, Finance Ministry.
The change in procedure of the Cabinet was interpreted
by the newspapers as a sign of surrender by the Hirota
Cabinet to the defense Ministries, inasmuch as the Cabinet
has sacrificed the plans for the "stabilization of the
national livelihood" to the demands of the defense forces.
It appears more likely, however, that the Cabinet, with
a plethora of plans calling for the expenditure of large
sums of public money, decided that it would be the part
of wisdom to allot first the minimum which the defense
services could be induced to accept, and then to consider
the

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.99)
一 5 -
the plans of the other Ministries in the light of the
remaining amount of revenue available.
There has been considerable criticism of the enormous
defense plans of the Army and Navy in the newspapers and
other periodicals. As one writer expressed it, when the
Cabinet was first organized, its policies were supposed
to be the clarification of the national polity, the stabi-
lization of the livelihood of the people and the replenish-
ment of national defenses, but the clarification of the
national polity has taken the form of the arrest or sup-
pression of a few liberal thinkers; the stabilization of
the livelihood of the people seems to have been forgotten;
and in the end it has all boiled down to the replenishment
of national defense. Various newspaper editorials have
questioned the ability of the nation to pay for the enormous
requirements of the Army and Navy, and questioned the wis-
dom of applying all of the nation's revenue to armed defense
without regarding the larger matter of the nation's economic
strength, which is, in the end, the real test of a nation's
power of resistance.
There seems to be little doubt, however, that the de-
fense Ministries, and the Army in particular, are asserting
control over the Government. The KOKUMIN of July 13 stated
that the Army desires action in regard to administrative
reform and a complete understanding on the part of the
Cabinet of the nature of the situation confronting the
nation

=== Page 256 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
nation, including the need for replenishment of national
defenses and the stabilization of the livelihood of the
nation. To this end, the Army believes that the individual
Ministers of State should sacrifice their personal ambi-
tions. If defense expenditures and state revenue cannot
be made to balance, the Army wants a drastic reform of the
economic structure of Japan. The newspapers have also re-
ported that the War Office authorities, including General
Terauchi, the Minister of War, are dissatisfied with the
progress being made by the Hirota Cabinet, and there have
been rumors that General Terauchi will resign when the
annual transfers and promotions take place in August. If
such is the case, and the Army continues dissatisfied with
the Hirota Cabinet, it may mean the downfall of the Cabinet.
The Army's Program.
According to the ASAHI, General Terauchi on July 14
submitted in writing to the Premier definite Army plans
for the replenishment of national defense, administrative
reform, stabilization of the livelihood of the people, and
Manchurian policy. The plans for replenishment of national
defense (Army only) are reported to cost some Yen 3,000,000,000,
to be spread over twelve years, but most of which would be
needed in the first six years. (Later reports are to the
effect that the Army wants Yen 3,300,000,000 in the first
six years of its replenishment program.) The plans for
administrative reform include the creation of a Ministry
of Air ( the Navy is reported to disagree with this last
plan). For the stabilization of the livelihood of the
nation,

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
nation, the Army, according to the newspaper, recommends
a general increase of the economic strength of the nation,
the decrease of the burden on the farming communities,
the reform of taxation, the equalization of the tax burdons
of the urban and the rural districts, the adjustment of
farmers' debts, and the promotion of the health of the
nation. For the development of "Manchukuo", the Army pro-
poses the encouragement of the emigration of Japanese to
Manchuria, the development of industries in that region,
and the security of Japanese investments there. According
to the ASAHI of July 21, the Army is also reported to de-
sire the reorganization of Japan on a "national defense
basis". This plan envisages a National Mobilization Law,
a National Mobilization Secrets Protection Law, the re-
vision of the present Munitions Industry Mobilization Law
and revision of the existing Resources Research Law. The
National Mohilization Law would emphasize the organization
of industrial workers in peace time, in order that there
might be adequate labor in time of war, without the dis-
organization caused by industrial workers being called to
the colors.
The Navy Ministry has been maintaining strict secrecy
in regard to its building program after the abrogation of
the Washington Treaty becomes effective, and it is reported
that detailed plans will not even be divulged to the Cabinet,
because of the fear of leakage of building secrets. It has
been reported, however, that the naval replenishment plan
contemplates

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
contemplates the expenditure of Yen 2,000,000,000 during
the nert five years.
Taxation Plans.
The ASAHI of July 19, 1936, reported that the Finance
Ministry has decided to postpone consideration of all
taxation reform schemes for some time, and to submit to
the next session of the Diet ( which will convene next
December) only the Ministry's tax increase plan. This
plan contemplates increases in practically all classes of
taxes and imposes some new taxes, including one of five
sen per gallon on gasoline. The plan will, it is reported,
bring in additional revenue of about Yen 343,500,000 per
annum. In the meantime, consideration will be given to
taxation reform schemes, with the object of lessening the
burden on the rural communities and increasing the burden of
the financial groups and the industrialists, without, how-
ever, increasing the burden to such an extent as to tar
industry out of existence.
It has also been reported that General Terauchi desires
the Government to formulate a "positive bond policy" in
order to meet the requirements of national defense. What
General Terauchi has in mind is apparently something like
the policy of the present Administration in Washington,
whereby public loans will be issued unstintingly in order
to overcome a national emergency, except that in Japan the
emergenoy is not one of national relief but of national
defense. The Finance Ministry is reported to be opposed
to

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.98)
to the General's bond scheme.
Comment.
Although there has been some newspaper criticiam
of the abandonment by the Premier of his original scheme
for more or less public discussion by the Cabinet Council
of all national policy plans of the various Ministries,
the scheme, carried as far as it was, seems to have ac-
complished the purpose for which it apparently was de-
signed, namely, to give the public a better understanding
of the intricate problems of government and to enable
them to discuss those problems comprehendingly. Much of
the trouble which has arisen in the past, such as the in-
cidents of May 15, 1932, and that of February 26 of this
year, can be traced in some degree to a lack of comprehen-
sion of the complicated problems of a modern industrial state
by the young visionaries in and out of the Army. The mass
of conjecture and discussion which has appeared in the news-
papers of Japan since the Premier inaugurated his system
of full Cabinet discussion of national policy plans, with
the publication, in more or less accurate form, of the plans
of some of the Ministries, has tended to educate the public
generally, and, it is believed, the Army in particular, in
the problems of gogernmental administration and finance.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:

E. R. Dickover
First Secretary of Embassy.

Tokyo, July 23, 1936.
SUBJECT: THE POLITICAL CONSEQUENCES OF THE TOKYO INCIDENT
OF FEBRUARY 26, 1936.
894.10
936 M  3 3
AND LURDS
C
COPIES SENT TO
U.N.I. ANDM. I.D.
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
894.00/668
OF STATE
FAB LASTEN ARS
Fab
A0G111936
Department of Frate
Gn
The Honorable
SEr 2 = 1936
The Secretary of State,
FILED
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to submit the following study of
the political consequences of the incident of February 26,
1936.
As reported to the Department in numerous telegrams
and despatches, on the morning of February 26 at Tokyo
a group of young officers in the Japanese Army led several
hundred troops to the residences of a number of the
F/FG
highest officials of the country, several such officials
were assassinated, the insurgents seized a number of public
buildings, martial law was declared by the Government, a
few

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.99)
12
- 2 -
few days later the insurgents surrendered and submitted
to arrest, they were tried by court martial extending
over several months, the leaders were sentenced to death,
other conspirators were sentenced to various terms of im-
prisonment, on July 12 most of those sentenced to death
were executed, and on July 18 martial law was lifted.
Confusion was the first effect of the incident. The
people were at a loss to know what had occurred and what
was about to occur, particularly inasmuch as a strict cen-
sorship made them susceptible to rumors as their source
of knowledge of what was actually going on. There were
frequent expressions of resentment against the censorship,
and one newspaper went so far as to say that the incident
itself would not have occurred in a community enjoying
freedom of speech.
But the populace quickly regained its composure by
virtue of its political inertia, its general indifference
to political ideas. Although the insurgents began their
operations by assassinations, their use of terrorism ended
there. Confining their use of violence to this dramatic
opening they expected that a simple statement of their
motives and program would call forth widespread popular
support and lead to the changes which they advocated.
They addressed the people through printed statements.
Quietly and confidently they harangued the passers-by
in front of the buildings which they seized. They
explained that they were opposed to privilege, whether
of wealth or of big business or of the misguided advisers
close

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.98)
61
- 3 -
close to the Emperor or of those Army leaders who gave in
to favoritism in military promotions; they advocated a
restatement of the fundamental structure of the state;
they favored stabilization of the livelihood of the pople
of Japan; they argued for recognition of the needs of ade-
quate defense of the country. They urged a Showa re-
storation in the year 1936 as brilliant and comprehensive
as the Meiji restoration. But several circumstances
materially weakened the appeal of the insurgents to the
country. The people did not like the killing of old men
in cold blood; they were opposed to the commanding of
Imperial troops contrary to orders from above; they re-
sented the threat to the Imperial palace and the Emperor
in the movements of the troops controlled by the insur-
gents; and for the first time they were awakened to the
necessity of putting a stop to the long series of poli-
tical assassinations which have taken place in Japan in
recent years.
Stunned that the ideas of the insurgents were not
sweeping the populace, and faced by inevitably heavy
bloodshed if choosing resistance to the overwhelming
loyal force sent against them, the insurgents capitu-
lated. The uprising was at an end. There remained its
punishment and the prevention of the recurrence of similar
danger in the future. The situation called for the forma-
tion of a new cabinet.
At this point the Army stepped forward to assert
a leadership which it has successfully maintained up to
the present time. Through its well-known power of preventing
the

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
the formation of a cabinet by forbidding any officer
to become Minister of War the Army was able to exact
from the new Premier, Hirota, definite concessions to the
wishes of the Army. The Army vetoed the appointment of
Yoshida as Foreign Minister. More important, the Army
undoubtedly required that the new cabinet undertake
fundamental political reform. What is especially start-
ling to the foreign observer is that the fundamental
reforms which the Army has pushed during the tenure of
office of this cabinet are practically those reforms
advocated by the insurgents of ebruary 26. Soon after
the formation of the Hirota cabinet the Army stated to
the press that in the cabinet-forming negotiations it had
insisted on Hirota's recognition of the need for action
on the national polity, livelihood of the people, national
defense, and positive foreign policy. The details which
appeared in this Army statement to the press are now, in
retrospect, astonishingly accurate as a prospectus of what
has in fact occupied the Government in the months since.
The Army took the whip hand and has kept it.
So serious a shock as the incident of rebruary 26
could not pass without causing many intelligent people
doubts of Japanese stability. Deposits in the Japanese
banks associated with clearing houses fell two and a half
per cent between the end of January and the end of Feb-
ruary l936. Pressure to remove investments from Japan to
foreign countries resulted in preventive restrictions by
the rinance Ministry, and many wealthy Japanese were thereby
checked in their plans. Concurrently the Government re-
cognized danger in the covert circulation of politically
disquieting

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.98)
5
- 5 -
disquieting documents (such had been disoovered to have
had a part in all the recent political assassinations of
Japan) and new measures were consequently put into force
to control dangerous and seditious literature.
A direct consequence of the changed public state of
mind following upon the February 26 incident was decisive
action in the trial of Aizawa, the Army officer who
assassinated General Nagata in August 1935. Previous to
the incident Aizawa's trial had dragged out inconclusively
with astonishing explanations of patriotic motive admitted
as evidence in his defense. After February 26 a new trial
was launched for him and he was sentenced to death. on
July 1 he was executed.
When sentences of imprisonment and death were pro-
nounced July 7 on the conspirators of the February 26
incident popular approval was prectically unanimous.
The
conviction was wisely based on the insurgents' use of
Imperial troops without Imperial authority. This ground
of conviction in a way justified the punishment meted out,
so heavy by comparison with that of the conspirators of
May 15, 1932. It had the further virtue of explaining the
severe penalties imposed without reflecting upon the reform
measures advocated by the insurgents. This was desirable
from the Army's point of view because of the great similar-
ity of the reform measures which the Army is backing.
The Army has gained rather than lost by the incident
and its sequel. At first, to be sure, the fact that the
insurgents were Army men had its reaction, and there was a
noticeable resistance to military service in some districts.
But the Army itself has been the executor of the measures
taken against the insurgents and has discharged the re-
sponsibility with finality in a manner which is approved
by

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.98)
61-
-6 -
by the people. In addition, the Army has succeeded
in maintaining itself in the role, under the present
administration, of trustee for fundamental reform. No
organization realizes as well as the Army itself how
widespread is military sentiment in favor of basic poli-
tical change. The movement promoted by the February 26
insurgents was no passing fancy. That General Masaki
himself was much more than a passive sympathizer of
the insurgents is scarcely open to doubt. The urge
to political change is ingrained in the Army, and in
spite of the failure of cabinet national policy dis-
cussions (these discussions degenerated into special
requests by the various departments for increased appro-
priations, and were abandoned in July after a few sessions)
the Army, which is still the crucial factor in the future
of Japan, can confidently be expected to continue in its
search for a new political order for this country.
The settlement of the incident of February 26, 1936,
has strengthened government in Japan without in any way
weakening the movement for social and political reform
which has been gaining weight in the Army over a period
of years. The incident was in large part a protest
against privilege - privilege enjoyed by the wealthy,
privilege within the Army, privilege close to the Throne,
privilege in economic life. It was a protest drawn in
the terms of the military code. The incident was de-
cisively put down, but it was put down as a disciplinary
measure only, without prejudice to the cause protested.
It was put down by the Army itself which has not failed
to reiterate the same protest. As a result of the incident,
the

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
一一7
- 7 -
the country's stand on any political assassinations that
might occur in the future is clear and will no doubt serve
as a check on conspiracy; but at the same time the in-
creasing adoption of the principles advocated by the dis-
turbers of the peace of the last years has become a more
active issue, and an Army-guided expansion of government
control of industry at the expense of private property
is not only likely but also appears to be expected by
most intelligent Japanese.
The lack in Japan of liberal institutions, as is
particularly evident in the absence of those guarentees
grouped in Anglo-Saxon countries under the heading of civil
liberties, should not blind the foreign observer to the
great possibility, even the probability, of fundamental
political change in this country. What is most solidly
established in Japanese political psychology is not a be-
lief in one particular form of government economically and
sociologically defined, but is a belief in the necessity
and authority and rightness of government itself. The
acceptance of the authority of the Emperor is something
that can be counted upon to remain; but along with that
acceptance ideas are changing on the subject of what the
government should bring to the people. Japan's consti-
tution itself is a definition rather of structure of
government than of function of government, and will prove
adaptable to functional changes.
From the February 26 incident, government in Japan
emerges strong and the Army emerges strong. Previous
to the incident there was noticeable a trend under Army
leadership

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.93)
- 8 -
leadership toward increasingly powerful state control
of the economic mechanism. That trend has not suffered
by the incident; rather it has increased.
Respectfully yours,
Poo
Joseph C. Grew.

Sir:
157
With reference to the Embassy's despatch No. 1998 of
1/
August 21, l936, I have the honor to enclose a translation
of the Law Providing for the Protection and Surveillance of
Persons Charged with Dangerous Thoughts, which was passed
at the recent session of the Diet and was promulgated on
May 28, 1936, as Law No. 29.
This

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
This law provides that persons who have committed
offenses against the Law for the Maintenance of Peaee
and Order shall be placed under the proteetion and
surveillance of the competent authorities for e period
of two years if the sentences of such persons are pend-
ing or if they have served their terms of sentenee or if
they have been granted a provisional release.
The Law for the Maintenance of Peace and Order to
which the present law refers was promulgated as Law No. 46
on April 21, 1925, and provides the death penalty or life
imprisonment or extended prison terms for persons guilty
of plotting an alteration of Japan's national political struo-
ture. Apparently, after the occurrence of the February 26
Incident, it was felt by the authorities that measures should
be taken to establish a closer surveillance of such persons
as the civilians and ex-Army officers who had been involved
in the Incident.
It will be recalled that most of the persons who had
planned a change in the political struoture of Japan prior
to the February 26 Incident had received lenient sentences
or else had had their sentences postponed.* For example,
former Captains Koji Muranaka and Asaichi Isobe, former
Lieutenant Zei Nishida and Kazuteru Kita, a civilian, were
all prominently mentioned in the Aizawa trial as close
associates of the defendant and mubsequently were said by
an official of the Japanese War Office to have been the
actual ringleaders in the insurrection of February 26.**
All of these men had been guilty at one time or another of
conniving at attempts to overthrow the existing order, yet
894.20/165
thoz
* Embassy's despatch No. 1821, May 12, 1936.
** Embassy!s despatch No. 1746, March 20, 1936.
894.00/635

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
they were left free to plan a similar offense, the recent
insurgent movement. The untrammelled actions of such past
offenders werefurther illustrated by the fact that Captain
Muranaka, accompanied by Zensuke Shibukawa, a civilian, at-
tended seven sessions of the Aizawa trial and openly took
notes of the trial at each session. Hence, these facts may
have been instrumental in causing the enactment of this
legislation.
The provisions of the law apply not only to chauvinists
and rightists but also to Communists and other persons of a
leftist complexion who have essayed an alteration of "Japan's
national organization" through violence. In neither case is
it clear from what or from whom the persons under protection
and surveillance are to be "protected."
A companion law whose enactment was also inspired by
the recent disturbance was the law for the Temporary Control
of Seditious Documents, a translation of which was trans-
mitted to the Department with the Embassy's despatch No. 1912
of June 23, 1936.
Respectfully,
Kdwin L. Neville,
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

OFFICIAL GAZETTE, May 29, 1936.
Law No. 29 (promulgeted May 28, 1936).
A Law Providing for the Protection and
Surveillance of Persons charged with
Dangerous Thoughts.
Article 1. In the case of a person or persons who
have committed offences against the Law for the ainte-
2.
nance of Peace and Order (Promulgated April 21, 1985),
if the execution of their sentences has been postponed,
or if no legal action has been taken ageinst them simply
because there is no necessity for instituting a supple-
mentary action or suit, such persons may, in accordance
with a decision of a deliberative committee for protection
and surveillance, be placed under protection and surveillance.
This clause may elso be applied to cases where such persons
had either served their terms of sentence or had been
granted a provisional release.
Article 2. Under protection and surveillance, the
thoughts and actions of such persons shall be watched for
the purpose of protecting them on the one hand, and on
the other, of preventing them from committing further
offences.
Article 3.

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Article 3. Such persons shall be placed under the
surveillance of officials of the Protection and Sur-
veillance Office, or be handed over to protectors or
guardians, or be placed under charge of public protection
organizations, temples, churches, hospitals and other
proper establishments.
Article 4. In the case of such persons who have
been placed under protection and surveillance, certain
restrictions may be enforced against them as to their
places of residence, their social relations, and their
exchange of communications with other persons, and in
addition other restrictions may be deemed necessary.
Article 5. The term of such protection and sur-
veillance shall be for two years. If it is deemed
necessary in special cases, the term of such surveillance
may be extended by a decision of a deliberative committee
for protection and surveillance.
Article 6. If the circumstances stipulated in
Article l should exist, such persons may, if necessary,
be provisionally subjected to the provisions of Article 3,
pending a decision to be reached by a deliberative com-
mittee for protection and surveillance.
Article 7. The procedure stipulated in Article 3
or Article 4 may be either cancelled or altered at any
time while the procedure is in operation. This clause
shal1

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
shall also apply to the procedure in the foregoing
Articles.
Article 8. The Protection and Surveillance Office
may, if necessary, order the appropriate officials to
take such persons with them to any place.
Article 9. The Protection and Surveillance Office
and the officials in charge shall be empowered to order
public offices or members of public offices to act as
agencies for them or to render other necessary assist-
ance for the execution of their duties.
Article 10. In case such persons have actually
been placed under charge of public protection organi-
zations, temples, churches and other proper establish-
ments, either the whole or a part of the expenses in-
curred by those who have taken charge of such persons
shall be paid for.
Article 1l. The expenses in the foregoing Article
may, according to orders to be issued by the Frotection
and Surveillance Office, be collected either wholly or
partially from such persons or those who are duty bound
to look after such persons. As regards the procedure
above referred to, the provisions of Article 208 of the
Code of Procedure in Non-Litigious Cases shall be applied.
Anyone who is dissatisfied with the Government's
orders to pay the expenses mentioned in the foregoing
paragraph may institute an action in an ordinary Court
within

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
within a period of one month computed from the day
on which such orders were received. This action
shall have no binuing force upon the suspension of
execution.
Article 12. The provisions relating to the procedure
of protection stipulated in the Law Relating to Minors
shall not be applied to minors who have committed of-
fences against the Law for the Maintenance of Peace and
Order.
Article 13. The present Law shall not apply to
those persons mentioned in Articles 8 and 9 of the
Army Criminal Law and in Articles 8 and 9 of the Navy
Criminal Law.

Protection and urve. lance Office and of a delibera-

Ordinances to be issued later.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 25, 1936.
EHD:
JUm.
SKU
Tokyo's No. 2019 of September 2, 1936,
reports further in regard to the much dis-
cussed "southward advance policy" of Japan.
The despatch quotes statements by various
officials and civilians expressing views in
regard to the meaning of this policy.
While contributing a variety of opinions
concerning the "southward advance policy",
the despatch does not succeed in clarifying
the meaning of the phrase. The reason seems
to be that the Japanese people themselves
have no clear conception of the meaning of
the "southward advance policy" and interpret
it according to their individual ideas. Thus,
opinions range from economic penetration of
nearby regions in the southern seas, to
military expansion in and occupation of
territories in the entire western Pacific
and Indian oceans. One can best understand
the phrase by realizing that it originated
in the navy, which is searching for arguments
to reenforce its budget demands and is seek-
ing to obtain a share of the prestige gained
by the army on the continent. Although most
users of the phrase disclaim any suggestion
of forceful measures in the carrying out of
their ideas, nevertheless, the fact that the
policy originated in the navy and that it
envisages the backing, if not the use of the
navy, Is significant.
Certain

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
Certain steps taken by the government
have been interpreted by the public as
implementing the "southward advance policy":
the formation of a semi-official company,
the Taiwan Development Company, which will
act as a financing agent for Japanese
economic expansion southward from Formosa;
the reorganization of the South Seas Develop-
ment Company; and the appointment of a re-
tired admiral as governor general of
Formosa.

SUBJECT: JAPAN'S "SOUTHVARD ADVANCE" POLICY.
