CONFIDENTIAL.
Department of State
The Secretary of State,
Hashington.
Asir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's de-
52
spatches No. 1798, dated April 30, 1936, and No. 1847,
dated May 15, 1936, both in regard to the recent agi-
tation in Japan for a "southward advance" policy, and
to report the following developments in this situat on.
Character of the "Southward Advance" Policy.
At a meeting of the committee appointed by the
Lower House of the Diet to consider the Formosa Develop-
ment Company bill on May 18, 1936, Mr. Nagata, Minister
for Overseas Affairs, Mr. Arita, Minister for Foreign
Affairs,

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Affairs, and Admiral Nagano, Minister of the Navy,
spoke on the subject of the "southward advance" pro-
jects of the Government. According to the TOkYo
NICHI NICHI, Mr. Nagata stated that, although Japan
has up to the present been expanding on the Asiatic
continent and to the north, the time had arrived for
the nation to start work to the south, the need for
such expansion being urgent. Mr. Arita stated that,
in his opinion, southward expansion was essential
to the welfare of the nation and consequently the
Government was considering means for the securing
of resources in that part of the vorld. He added
that this policy of the Government implied no poli-
tical or territorial designs. Admiral Nagano stated
that the Japanese policy of expansion to the south
was peaceful in nature and designed only for economic
purposes. At other discussions of the "southward
advance" policy in the Diet the purposes of the policy
were stated to be the development of fuel and "other
national defense resources", the acquisition of sources
of cotton, lumber and other raw materials, the facili-
tation of colonial emigration, the opening of new markets
for Japanese goods and the development of fisheries.
In an article published in the May, 1936, issue
of KAIZO and translated in CONTEMPORARY OPINIONS, Pro-
fessor Tadao Yanaihara of the Tokyo Imperial University
explained some of the reasons for and the character of
the "southward advance" policy. The Professor stated that
"Manchukuo" had been a disappointment as a source of raw
materials

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
materials and a market for Japanese commodities; that
expansion on the continent had redounded to the credit
of the Japanese Army, at the expense of the prestige
of the Japanese Navy and that the Navy therefore is
strongly advocating an overseas program; that the Navy
intends to control the Western Pacific from the Sea of
Okhotsk to the South Seas in order to strengthen national
defense and to provide opportunities for overseas ex-
pansion; and that, whereas expansion on the Asiatic con-
tinent has been the outcome of military operations, Japa-
nese development in Oceania has been due not to naval
activities but to economic causes. The Professor closed
his article with a plea for a better understanding of
Japan's pacific intentions in the South Seas, a part
of the article having been translated as follows:
"One fact must always be remembered in
connection with our southern expansion; it
is economic in its nature and our naval
power has no direct influence upon it. The
whole history of our South Seas policy de-
monstrates that it is our intention to expand
only by peaceful means and not by military
weight. The Navy Office, in advocating the
movement, intends only to protect the peace-
ful activities of Japanese nationals; it is
unthinkable that there is any intention of
carrying out the South Seas policy by mili-
tary operations. A purely economic expansion
such as this, however, is entirely justifiable
from the standpoints of world peace and the
current financial situation of this country.
It is unfortunate that the Government of the
Dutch East Indies and the Commonwealth Govern-
ment of Australia, inspired by groundless fears
of Japan's oceanic policy, have closed their
doors to Japanese capital, commodities and immi-
grants. They do not intend to develop their
natural resources themselves, nor will they
permit Japan to do so, and thus the exploita-
tion of the great southern regions, which
would play an important part in world economy,
is left neglected. The main reason for this is
doubtless

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
doubtless that Australia and the Dutch East
Indies are forever seeing territorial designs
in our southward policy.
Therefore, in order to carry out success-
fully her justified expansion to the south,
Japan should declare to the world that she has
no territorial designs, refrain from all speech
and action likely to incite the feelings of
other nations, place a high value on world
peace and international justice in order to regain
the world-wide honour formerly extended because
of her love of peace, and try by every possible
means to eradicate Japanophobia from the minds
of her neighbors in the south."
There are enclosed clippings from the JAPAN TIMES AND
1/
MAIL, issues of August 28, August 29 and August 30, 1936,
containing the complete text of an article by Mr. Masanori
Ito, the well-known writer on naval affairs, on the subject
of Japan's southward expansion and the connection of the
Navy therewith. Mr. Ito defines the policy as economic,
pacific and maritime in character. He points out the ad-
vantageous position possessed by Japan both as regards
imports from the South Seas and exports to those regions
and expatiates upon the benefits to be derived, not only by
Japan but by the inhabitants of the South Seas, from in-
creased trade with Japan. He defines the South Seas,
for economic purposes, as including Siam, the Netherland
Indies, French Indo-China, the Philippine Islands, the
Straits Settlements, and perhaps New Guinea and the Solomon
Islands. In a larger sense, he includes British India,
Australia and parts of Africa in the term "South Seas.
Mr. Ito's theory is that Japan's economic advance into these
territories, probably with the opposition of the present
holders of the territories, should be smoothed by diplo-
matic negotiations, backed by Japan's naval strength.
Although

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Although he does not specifioally mention the
subject, Mr. Ito would appear to have in mind, in dis-
cussing Japan's "southward advance", the recently-ad-
vanced theory that there should be a redistribution
of the raw material resources and of the markets of
the world - that the "haves" should consent to give
something of their resources to the "have nots".
The OSAKA MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI NICHI, English
Edition, of May 31, 1936, in an editorial, further de-
fined the "southward advance" policy as follows:
"The term 'advance is frequently asso-
ciated with territorial ambitions. But, in
fact, territorial expansion has ceased to be
a problem as far as Japan is concerned. What
Japan desires in that connection does not go
beyond assurance that her nationals will be
allowed to go and live where they choose,
that adequate protection will be afforded
them, and that they may pursue lawful occu-
pations in peace in any land of their choice."
Despite the numerous protestations that Japan's
advance into the South Seas area is to be peaceful and
economic in nature, however, occasionally a hint of a
threat creeps into the discussions. Thus, the editorial
from the OSAKA MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI NICHI, quoted
above, included the following paragraph:
"In the event, however, that the powers con-
cerned are not disposed to respect our life-line
and elect to ignore our right to existence, we
must have at our command sufficient means to
make them listen to reason."
Likewise, Mr. Ito, in the article referred to above,
stated:
"It must, however, be noted that the first
line of advance consists of business and diplomacy,
and all that the Navy is concerned with is in-
direct assistance and support in a general sense.
This trade line is the life line for Japan, as it
were, and it is obvious that, in order to pro-
tect this life line, the Navy should be kept
ready for action."
The

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
The implication is that the Japanese intend to
advance into the South Seas for economic reasons,
peacefully if possible, but to advance anyway, using
force if necessary to overcome opposition to their
advance. It is probably this line of reasoning which
lies behind, in part at least, the Navy's advocacy
of the "southward advance" policy. The Navy is search-
ing for arguments to reinforce their naval budget demands.
If Japanese emigrants and tradesmen are to cbtain the
right freely to exploit the lands to the south of Japan,
the Navy must be sufficiently powerful to impose Japan's
will upon the present holders of the lands in question.
Hence Japan will need a navy second to none of those of the
Powers interested in the South Seas.
There has been some discussion of the desirability
of a more lucid definition of the "southward advance"
policy, in order to dispel the feeling of uneasiness
aroused among the Powers by the publicity given to the
policy. Thus, the editorial in the OSAKA MAINICHI AND
TOKYO NICHI NICHI, quoted above, advocated the formulation
of a "cautious and far-sighted" policy in regard to ex-
pansion southward. Professor Yanaihara, in the article
referred to above, also advocated a clear definition
of Japan's aims. The MIYAkO, in its issue of August 19,
1936, published an editorial stating as follows:
" It is most important that the Government
will in this connection clarify the real nature of
Japan's southward advance policy so that any
further unnecessary trouble, as was experienced
in the Davao land question, may be averted. The
fact that Japan has no other desire than to make
economic exploitation must be made clearly known."
The

=== Page 286 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
The political parties are also reported to desire a
clear definition of the "southward advance" policy,
but as yet the Government does not appear to be pre-
pared to issue any statement on the subject.
Implementing the "Southward Advance" Policy.
Certain steps have been taken by the Government
which have been interpreted by the newspapers as a part
of a general scheme of implementing the "southward ad-
vance"policy. In the last session of the Diet a bill was
passed authorizing the formation of the semi-official
Taiwan Development Company (Taiwan Takushoku Kaisha),
which, it was explained in the newspapers, will act as
a financing organ for Japanese economic expansion south
from Formosa. The Company is to be capitalized at
Yen 30,C00,000, to be subscribed by the Government, public
corporations, and Japanese subjects and juridical per-
sons; the affairs of the Company are to be under the
control of the Government; and the Company is to undertake
projects for the development of Formosa and to supply funds
for that purpose. The newspapers report that this Company,
operating from Takao, Formosa, will conduct various trad-
ing, mining and fishing enterprises in the South Seas.
On August 29, 1936, the reorganization of the South
Seas Development Company (Nanyo Takushoku Kaisha) took
place. This Company formerly operated the phosphate mines
on the island of Anguar and other mines on the island of
Fais. It has been reorganized with a capital of Yen 20,O00,Oc0
of

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
of which Yen 10,546,000 represents the Government's
share (consisting of the mines on Anguar and else-
where) and of which Yen 9,454,000 will be subscribed
by the public. The objects of the Company are phosphate
mining, fishing, marine transport and financing in
the South Seas. It will start operations from January 1,
1937.
On August 31, 1936, Mr. K. Nakagawa, Governor General
of Formosa, resigned. One newspaper reported that he was
894
forced to resign, against his will, by Mir. Nagata, the
Minister for Overseas Affairs, in accordance with an
agreement entered into by the Cabinet with the defense
forces, at the time of the formation of the Hirota Cabinet,
to the effect that a naval officer would be appointed
Governor General of the island. The newspapers now report
that Admiral Seizo Kobayashi, on the reserve list, has
been appointed Governor General and will be formally
installed in the near future. Admiral Kobayashi was
formerly Maval Attaché in London and 'ashington and served
a term as Vice inister of the Navy. The ASAHI of August
31, interpreted this change from a civil governor to a
naval governor of Formosa as indicating an acceleration
of the industrial and naval defense program for Formosa,
and linked the move with the exniration of the naval
limitation treaties at the end of this year. It also
stated that the change marks the definite establishment of
the "southward advance" policy.
It appears likely that the principal meaning of the
change in the governorship of Formosa lies in the assumption
that

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.96)
- 9 -
that the Japanese Navy is planning, upon the ex-
piration of the Washington Naval Treaty at the end
of this year, to extend its sphere of operations further
to the south and is making preparations accordingly.
Respectfully yours,
Edim I.Ncill
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS

JUN 2 4 1937
Japan: Roonomic Developments: Trade:
NOTED
DIVISION OF
Economio Reperoussions and
Political Developments and
Possibilities.
894.00
At intervals since l93l, observers of economie and
political developments in the Far East have come forward,
here and there and now and then, with the thesis that
894.00/67
there is approaching a breakdown of Japan's internal and/or
external economy. Likewise, at intervals, officers of this
Department concerned with Far Eastern relations have given
consideration, in the light of such predictions, to the
facts in the situation; and, at intervals, we have expressed
211
the view that the facts available give no evidenoe of a
crack in or an impending oracking up of Japan's economy.
During the past year the affirmation has repeatedly
been made that Japan's foreign trade has reached or is near
to its peak and that there may be expeoted soon a leveling
off or even a deoline in the figures of that trade. Offi- 
cers of the Department are at present studying the evidence
3
with regard to this affirmation. It may be some time before
we are ready to submit an expression of our views in that
F/F
connection.
Meanwhile,
G

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.97)
2
一2 -
Meanwhile, however, it may be worth while to give
thought at this time to certain of the political impliea-
tions of recent developments in conneotion with Japan's
foreign trade. During the past five years Japan's trade,
both in exports and in imports, with a perfeotly logical
relationship between those two elements, has inoreased
by leaps and bounds. With an amazing coordination and
with consequent great comparative efriciency, the Japanese
have shown themselves able to make large purohases from
abroad of raw materials, to produce manufactured artioles,
to erport these articles and to sell them abroad at prices
which have created a substantial demand for the Japanese
product. This has brought the Japanese products into suo-
cessful competition with similar products of other countries
both in the home and in the export markets of the said other
countries. In consequence, the producers and the sales
agencies of other countries have demanded of their govern-
ments protection against the newly developed Japanese
competition; and there have been raised all over the world
various obstacles to the free and natural flow of Japanese
exports.
At the same time, Japan's population has gone on
increasing; the Japanese are, like other peoples, resorting
to labor-saving devioes; the demands of the Japanese popula-
tion

=== Page 297 === (confidence 0.98)
23
一3 -
tion for the essentials of livelihood constantly inerease;
this population looks more and more to manufacturing and
sale of manufactured commodities for its livelihood;
Japan's oapacity to produce and to export increases. The
more the exports of Japan are increased, the greater the
fear abroad of Japanese competition and the more the steps
taken abroad to proteot home industry against the lower-in-
price Japanese products.
Thus, while the economio pressure outward of Japan
is constantly increasing, there is being developed by
other countries an economic iron ring in opposition to
that pressure.
At the same time, in the political field, the Japa-
nese militarists have embarked, on behalf of the nation,
on a course of imperialistic expansion the prinoipal
instrument of which is armed foroe. In this field, as
in the field of trade, the Japanese effort has met with
initial successes. But, it has aroused fear -- not alone
among Japan's immediate neighbors but all over the world.
China, the Soviet Union, the British Empire, the Netherland
Empire and the United States have viewed with anxiety the
military moves and the excessive efforts to oreate a
superior army and a superior navy in which the Japanese
have engaged. The Soviet Union has established defensive
land, air and submarine forces in the Far East, which
foroes

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.97)
43-4
一 4 -
forces might readily on occasion be turned to offensive
purposes. China is making every effort of which she is
capable toward armament, now for defense against Japan,
but which might on occasion be turned at some time in the
future to offensive purposes. Great Britain and the
United States, confronted with the refusal of Japan any
longer to be bound by naval limitations constructed on the
principle of equality of security, are looking earnestly
to the perfection of their armaments. In the cases of China
and the United States, the policy of armament flows princi-
pally from solicitude in the presence of evidence of
predatory inclinations on the part of the Japanese. In the
cases of the Soviet Union and the British Empire, the
policy of armement flows partly from solicitude with regard
to situations and problems in Europe and partly from solici-
tude with regard to situations and problems in the Far East.
The great military establishments at Vladivostok, at
Singapore, and in the Hawaiian Islands, however, are estab-
lishments which relate primarily to the problem of defense
against Japan. And now General MacArthur and President
Quezon are arming the Philippines, for defense against --
Japan. Thus, it may reasonably be said there has been
created and there is being hardened a military iron ring
around Japan.
Both

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.96)
5-s
- 5 -
Both in the economic field and in the military field
there exists the phenomenon of a nation that is erowded,
dynamically energetic, convinced that it must expand and
making every effort to expand, and a world that is limited
in area and is filled with other sovereign nations most
of which are fearful of that nation's expansion, convineed
that they must resist it, and taking steps to resist it.
In such a situation, the obvious question is: Will
the foroes of expansion prove greater than the forees of
resistance? If so, when and at what point will the former
break through?
The weakest points in the economic and military rings
around Japan are to the southwestward and the southward:
China and the Philippines (and the Dutch East Indies).
There was a time when China's natural resources were
rated as comparatively limitless and China's military
strength as negligible. But, in recent years the estimates
of China's resources have been revised downward and China's
efforts in the direction of military preparedness have
made some impression. The old ideas of China as a potential
Eldorado no longer prevail. Meanwhile, estimates of the
natural resources of the Philippine Islands and portions of
the Dutch East Indies are constantly being revised upward,
and there is not within these areas themselves any substantial
organization for resistance to possible pressure from without.
If and when the aegis of American authority and responsibility
18

is withdrawn from the Philippine Islands, there will lie

On August 25, 1936, the basic national policies
proposed by the various ministries vere brought before
the Cabinet for its eproval. The Cabinet approved the
following seven policies and announced them officially
F/FG
on

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)

on that dey:
1. Replenishment of national defense.
2. Adjustment of the national and local taxation
systems.
3. Reform and improvement of the educational system.
4. Stabilization of national livelihood.
a. Improvement of health and sanitation
facilities.
b. Amelioration of natural disasters.

6. stablisiment of a definite policy tovard Kanchukuo
by the promotion of emigration and encouragement of

7. Improvement and :djustment of administrative

The an ouncement of these policies was not received with
any creat enthusiasm in Japan. The Army is reported in the
newspapers to have criticized the policies as being too
abstract. The SAliI of August 26 stated editorially that
the

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
the policies were hardly worth discussion. It also
criticized the Cabinet for not having included the
formulation of a definite foreign policy in its list
of national policies, as a definite foreign policy is
very necessary at the moment, when relations with China
and the Soviet Union are as delicate as they are. The
JIJI of August 26 stated editorially that the enforcement
of plans is better than their enumeration and that, un-
less the gorerrment clarifies the exact nature of the
policies to be pursued, there will be no cessation of
unrest in the minds of the people.
The YOMIURI of
August 26 stated that the policies were about what ..as
to be expected from the Hirota Ca inet und expressed
the fear that the national defense policy would even-
tually absorb all the other policies. The KOlTIN
of August 27 ex ressed regret that the Cabinet took
five months to formulate its policies and speculated
upon how lon it would therefore take to carry them out.
It elso regretted that administrative reform, which
should have been the primary policy, was relegated to a
very incorspicuous position.
The actual enforcement of the announced national
policies has not yet been carried very far. The Army's
plan for the replenishment of national defense has been
reported in the newspa ers. It will require, in addition
to the usual Army expenditures, about Yen 3,300,000,000,
to be ex ended over the next six years, for the purpose
of improving the air forces, mechanization of the Army,
improvement of equipment, end strengthening of Japanese
forces

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
forces in Manchuria. The first instalment ofyen 300,000,000
was included in the Army budget for the 1937-38 fiscal year,
and the Army will ask for Yen 600,000,000 during each of
the succeeding five years, in addition to its ordinary budget.
It is reported that the Finance Ministry's program
for the adjustment of the national and local taxation systems
will be placed before the Cabinet on September 22. Details
of the proposed adjustment, however, have not yet been
made public.
In regard to the reform and improvement of the educa-
tional system, it is reported that a commission of inves-
tigation is proposed. This commission vill consider general
revision of the national educational system, and will re-
port its findings to the Cabinet.
As was reported by separate despatch, the control of
the electric power industry has become a major political
uestion in Japan, and is occupying the attention of the
Ministry of Conmunications, the Railways Ministry and other
ministries interested. The plan originally was to nationalize
the industry, but it has apparently been found that this
would be too ex ensive and the plan has been changed to control
by the Government of the generating and transmitting systems,
which will still be owned by private interests.
No concrete steps have as yet been announced for the
carrying out of the other policies listed. The Embassy
will endeavor to keep the Department informed as develop-
ments occur.

Tokyo's No. 2048 of September 18,
1936, reports in regard to current labor
questions in Japan. As most of the
information contained therein has reached
FE from other sources, you will probably
not wish to read.
On September 10, the army authorities
ordered all employees of the three large
military areenals to sever their connections
with labor unions, on the ground that
such membership was against the spirit and
principles of military discipline. Resolu-
tions protesting against this measure were
passed by several labor organizations.
One union declared that tre action was a
step toward fascism and as such against
the good of the people.
That this fear is not entirely un-
founded is indicated by a press report that
the Home Office is preparing a bill which
would outlaw unions, abolish the right to
strike, and establish government "protec-
tion" of labor.
In recent years the army has come to
regard itself as champion of the working
and farming classes. It therefore resents
the growth of any other organization or
movement aiming at the benefit of the
masses.
Commenting

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.97)

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
Commenting on the army's action
against labor unions, the Military Attaché
in Tokyo wrote: "This, coming so swiftly
on the heels of the numerous protests
against the nationalization of electric
power is a clear indication that the
people recognize no existing emergency
that demands the sacrifice of ordinary
civil liberties; and that in the opinion
of the nation at large, even devout
nationalism does not necessarily imply
an enthusiastic compliance with any
suggestion whose sole virtue consists
in the fact that it emanates from
military circles."

FAR LASTERN AFFAIRS
OCT6-1936
Uepartment of State
The Honorable
FILHD
1936
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report that during the past
month labor groups have asserted their position more
strongly than is their wont, but that the Army, with
which they have come into conflict, appears to have
F/FG
successfully maintained its position, desp ite repeated
criticisms by the labor groups of its budget and its
labor policy.
First

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
First of all, on August 16 the Shakai Taishuto or
Social Mass Party issued a statement expressing opposition
to increases in the defense expenditures, criticising the
Government for not having advanced a fundamental policy for
the stabilization of the people's livelihood, and pointing
out that military expenditures under capitalism were of
benefit only to capitalists at the sacrifice of the working
masses. Although the Shakai Taishuto has consistently
criticized the Hirota Cabinet ever since the latter's forma-
tion and has attacked certain aspects of the capitalistic
system in Japan, this is the strongest statement yet issued
by the Party, particularly the assertion that military ex-
penditures are beneficial to capitalists and detrimental to
the working masses.
There has been some talk of a popular front movement in
Japan along the lines of similar movements in Spain and France.
However, the hope of establishing a popular front in this
country seems doomed to failure for some time to come. On
September 7 Mr. Miyano, Chief of the Public Safety Section
of the Home Office, and Mr. Kayaba, Director of the Police
Bureau of the same Department, delivered speeches in which
they declared that no popular front movement, no attempt to
divide the nation into Fascist and anti-Fascist camps would
be tolerated in Japan, even if the movement should be free
of Communistic characteristics. Mr. Kayaba added that the
Communist movement had seemed to be dying out but that
stimulated by underground activity from abroad and changes
in the domestic situation it was showing signs of revival.
The

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
The Procurator General of the Justice Ministry, Mr.
Mitsuyuki, expressed a similar opinion on September 9,
namely, that the spread of Communism must be carefully
watched.
It is not known by the Embassy whether in fact there
has been a resurgence of the Communist movement in Japan.
However, in again bringing the Communistic bogey to the
fore, the Home and Justice Ministries have adopted a stand
that should meet with popular approval in view of the
"emergency nature" of the times and the national antagonism
toward Communist Russia. In this connection, it may be
reiterated that the Social Mass Party is by no means a
Communistic organization but one somewhat similar to the
British Labor Party.*
On September 10 the Army authorities ordered all em-
ployees of the military arsenals at Osaka, Nagoya and Kokura,
which number about 5,600 laborers, to sever their connections
with all labor unions, on the ground that it was against the
principle and spirit of military discipline for such workers
to retain membership in labor unions. According to the
ASAHI of September 1l, every arsenal worker thus approached
by the military authorities without exception took an oath
to withdraw from his union and never to join another one.
It will be recalled that several years ago the Navy Ministry
took similar action with the workers in naval arsenals.
As already reported to the Department, the Army auth-
orities have proven the sincerity of their announced in-
tention to establish stricter discipline in the Army by
dismissing
* Embassy's despatch No. 1706/of March 5, 1936.
894.00
625

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.99)
4-
dismissing higher officers who had been prone to meddle too
much in politics, by appointing moderate officers of the
orthodox army group to high positions, by executing or im-
prisoning the leaders of the February 26 Incident, and by
taking definite measures to isolate and suppress the ad-
vocates of direct action methods. Having thus chastized
rightist groups, the Army now feels justified in turning
its attention to elements considered to be of a leftist
character and likewise dangerous to the Army's discipline
and to the security of the State. An Army spokesman des-
cribed the measure taken against labor unions in military
arsenals as another step for the improvement of military
discipline and compared it to the orders given soldiers
after the February 26 Incident to discontinue all relations
with groups outside the Service.
Resolutions protesting against this military measure
were passed by the Social Mass Party, the Labor and Farm
Council, and the Tokyo Traffic Labor Union. The resolution
of the Social Mass Party stated among other things that,
as the military authorities knew only too well, the strength-
ening of national defense in the full sense of the term could
not be realized unless labor and the nation in general co-
operated with the Army "from the bottom of their hearts".
Going further, the Labor and Farm Union declared that the
present measure by the military authorities might possibly
set an example for the capitalist class to follow and that
it was a step toward the realization of a system of Fascism .
and as such was against the good of the Japanese people.
That

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
That the Labor and Farm Union's fear is not entirely
unfounded is indicated by a report in the NICHI NICHI of
August 25 to the effect that the Home Office was preparing
a bill, to be submitted at the next session of the Diet,
for a final "settlement" of Japan's labor problems by out-
lawing unions, abolishing the right to strike, and establish-
ing Government "protection" of labor. This newspaper stated
that the measure would be similar to the Nazi law for State
control of labor in Germany, would be unique in that it would
have no trace of Socialistic ideas, and would make the State
an impartial arbiter for the settlement of disputes arising
between labor and capital.
The Army's action with regard to military arsenals
placed the Home Office in an embarrassing position, inas-
much as that Department is said to be apprehensive lest the
Army's move be imitated in other Government factories and
even in private munitions factories. It was reported by the
JAPAN CHRONICLE of September 15 that the Home Office, whose
labor policy is based on the recognition of labor unions, had
communicated its point of view to the Navy, Finance, and Com-
munications Ministries, to the effect that the Army's action
is acceptable only as a part of the Army's work of enforcing
discipline in the service and that imitation of the step by
other Government factories would be undesirable.
Formal protests in writing were delivered by the Social
Mass Party and the National Council of Labor Unions to the
War and Home Offices on September 14. The principle points
raised by the Social Mass Party were that the formation of
Labor

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
Labor Unions was based on the right of association guaranteed
by the Constitution, that the Home Office had recognized the
legality of such unions, and that henoe the Army's persecu-
tion of labor unions showed a discrepancy of policies within
the Government.
It was reported by the TOKYO NICHI NICHI of September 16
that the authorities of the War Office had rejected the Sooial
Mass Party's protest, stating that the War Office stood for
vertical formation of the whole Army organization and would
therefore, under no circumstances, tolerate "horizontal combi-
nations" of Army arsenal workers. The Army's reply reportedly
contained the statement that the Imperial Army differed radical-
ly from capitalistic commercial organizations and that em-
ployers and employees alike in non-governmental plants would
do well to take this fact into consideration.
Amid all the tangled developments of the military ar-
senals situation, it is interesting to no te that the Army's
action has caused dissension between the so-called Fascist
and anti-Fascist elements in the Social Mass Party. The
former elements, according to the HOCHI of September 15,
maintain that the Japanese Army is essentially different
from those of other countries in that it is in a position
to become an agent of reform along socialistic lines, and
that, therefore, it is unnecessary to form a popular front
through the merger of the Social Mass Party with the National
Council of Labor Unions, which is headed by Mr. Kanju Kato.
On the other hand, the "anti-Fascist" elements in the Party
are said to have adopted the following attitude: the Party
should have the courage to refrain from blindly following the
lead of the Army and should not hesitate to criticise the
j
Amy

=== Page 314 === (confidence 0.94)
-7-
Army; the agency for social reform should be the proletarians,
not the Army; and the establishment of an anti-Fascist popular
front through a merger with Mr. Kato's organization is impera-
tive. While it appears probable that the"anti-Fascist" group
will win out in its struggle with the opposing group, it seems
extremely unlikely that either the Home Office or the Army
would permit the formation of any sort of popular front.
In conclusion, it may be said that the Army has its
own ideas concerning the improvement of economic conditions
among the working classes, including both laborers and farm-
ers. It is a part of the Army's credo that any improvement
effected in this connection must have the cooperation of the
Army. Thus, the military resent the growth of any separate
proletarian movement designed to benefit the masses. In a
word, the Army has no intention of being put in a position
where it can no longer act as the champion of the people,
for it is this association which partly explains the con-
tinued support of the Army among the unthinking masses.
Respectfully yours,

E. R. Dickover,

SUBJECT: TRIAL OF FORMER RAILWAY MINISTER FOR BRIBERY.
894.00/673

The Honorable
T
The Secretary of State,
比

DEC 3
FILED
Sir:
I have the honor to report the arrest of Mr. Shinya
Uchida, Minister of Railways in the Okada cabinet, charged
receiving bribes.
Mr. Uchida on October 31 voluntarily appeared for question-
ing before the procurators of the Tokyo District Criminal Court.
By the morning of November 4 the examination had yielded grounds
for an indictment, and Imperial consent (necessary because of
Mr. Uchida's rank) was obtained to allow his prosecution.
Mr.

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Mr. Uchida was thereupon indicted and confined in Ichigaya
prison.
It is expected that the trial will turn chiefly on the
defendant's ability to explain some Yen 150,000 received
from contractors in the form of contributions to campaign
funds. The case thus far seems to have had no harmful ef-
fect on the standing of the present Hirota cabinet, al-
though it has been feature news in all the newspapers.
The public is inured, two previous railway ministers
(Heikichi Ogawa and Chuzo Mitsuchi) having. been involved
in bribery scandals in the past year; but a conviction
will probably be exploited by persons who are interested
in bringing the political parties into disrepute.
Mr. Uchida was a member of the Seiyukai from the time
of his entry into politics in l924 until the formation of
the Okada cabinet in 1934, when his acceptance of a cabinet
position was in defiance of the Seiyukai. He withdrew
from the party and became a charter member of the Showakai.
Respectfully yours,
E.R. Dickover

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
JÁN 2 6 1937
DIVISION OF
RECETVED
January23,1937.
JAN 2 6 1937
Mr. Secretary:
JAN 25 1937
894.
The Parliamentary Crisis in Japan.
NOTED
When the Diet reconvened on January 21, strong
00
opposition to the Covernment was shown by the political
844.
parties, and consequently the Diet was that evening
prorogued for two days. The conflict between the
political parties and the Cabinet appears to be due
894.00/683
principally to the opposition of the political parties
to the Army and its policies. Today the Ambassador
reported that the members of the Cabinet had presented
their resignation to the Emperor.
American papers commenting on the Japanese
parliamentary crisis appear to be generally of the
opinion that the crisis was brought about by economic
FILED
and financial difficulties arising out of excessive
1937
naval and military expenditures. However, in November
and December, last, the Embassy in Tokyo took note of
the growing opposition on the part of the Japanese
public to the dominant position asserted by the Army
in political matters, and stated that this trend was
due to the unpopularity of the China policy of the
Cabinet, the conclusion of the anti-communist agreement
with Germany, and failure to improve relations with the
G
United

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
United States, Great Britain and Soviet Russia, as well
as to economic and financial difficulties arising out
of increased expenditures for naval and military purposes.
The situation today is still obscure. In accordance
with Japanese practice in situations such as the present
one, the Emperor will await the results of negotiations
which are doubtless taking place between the various
important political elements, and, in the light of such
results, will either order the Cabinet to remain in
office or will accept the resignation of the Cabinet and
order the constitution of a new cabinet. The possibility
of the Diet being dissolved, which was yesterday considered
likely by press observers, is now apparently remote.
In our view, editorial comment in the United States
seems to attach exaggerated significance to the develop-
ments of the past few days in Japan. There is no doubt
but that there has been during the past few months a
definite and growing tendency on the part of the liberal
elements in Janan to challenge the undue influence of the
Army in nolitical matters: it is our estimate that this
trend will continue to grow, but that it should not be
expected to mature for some time to come.
FE:EHD/DLY
S

OFFICE OF THE SEGRETARY
Mr. Becretary:
Department of State
The Parliamentary Crisis in Japan.
JAN 26 1937
894.00
Yesterday we heard informally from the Japane
NOTED
Ambassador that General Kazushige Ugaki had been designated
894.
Kokei
to form a Cabinet to succeed that of Mr. Hirota, whose
resignation was placed in the hands of the Emperor on
OB1
January 23, 1937. This information was confirmed by
894.00/687
despatches from Tokyo. The situation existing immediately
before the designation of General Ugaki to form a Cabinet
was:
The Army had demanded the dissolution of the Diet
and the holcing of a general election, in the belief that
if the issue of the Army versus the political parties were
submitted to the electorate the weight of public opinion
would be favorable to the Army. However. the Navy,
deploring any move toward creating a fissure between the
armed services and the political parties, declined to
support the Army. In accordance with past practices in
times of political stress, the advice of Prince Saionji,
the Elder Statesman, was sought.
In the light of yesterday's development, it may be
assumed that Saionji recommended the nomination of General
Ugaki as Prime Minister and against dissolution of the Diet,
F/FG
for the reason that, in his opinion, the weight of public
opinion

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
opinion is on the side of the political parties and against
the holding at this time of a general election.
General Ugaki is now retired from the Army, but he has
a considerable following in the Army and will probably be
able to find a general officer willing to take up the
portfolio of War -- the first problem which General Ugaki
will have to resolve in forming his Cabinet. As Minister
of War, in 1925 he reduced the military establishment by
four divisions, and his administration of Korea as Governor
General was, on the whole, an enlightened and progressive
one. He is regarded as a moderate liberal. He has for
some years been associated with the Minseito, the majority
political party, and it is probable that as Prime Minister
he will be acceptable to that party.
In the light of information thus far available, the
princinal significance of the developments of the past few
days is the estimate of the Emperor and of his immediate
advisers that public opinion is now favorable to a return
to what is known in Janan as a "constitutional cabinet",
or a cabinet resting on a political party or a coalition
of political parties. It is probable that no effort will
if and
be made by the Ugaki Cabinet,/when formed, substantially
to reduce the naval and military estimates. Nor do we
look for any extensive modification of Japan's foreign
policies except by long and gradual processes.
FED/DLY.m.H

One. Political observers ascribe formation of Moderate
Cabinet to army realization that it had gone too far in
opposing will of rest of nation. The army's wrecking of
the Hirota Cabinet resulting from the former's quarrel
with the political parties and its successful obstruction
of Ugaki who was the almost unanimous choice of the nation
exclusive of the army for Premier brought about a sugges-

interference in politics which led army leaders to encourage
the fornation of a Cubinet with less military and Fascist
leanings than w s expeeted. Hence the choice of Nakamura
instead of the strong Itagaki as War Minister.

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.99)

U -2- #43, Feb. 6, 11 a.m. from Tokyo
would gradually enforce necessary reforms.
Three. Such is the Hayashi Cabinet, without decided
complexion, without the support of the political parties,
and without marked convictions or theories although more
mature public appraisal is inclined to regard it as less
colorless than at first believed. While somewhat more
under military influence than the preceding Cabinet it is
probable that this influence will be exerted with restraint
owing to the attitude of the military leaders described in
paragraph one. There are indications that before further
asserting their power the army leaders will await the
development of the new reactionary political party which
will support and be supported by the army. The facts
concerning this new party which had its origin in a grouping
of military and other patriotic societies under th incentive
of the retired General Tatekawa will be reported by despatch.
Four. The Premier is exclusively a soldier, honest,
rational, straight-forward, not a forceful character, in-
fluential in army circles though with little experience in
politics and government.
Makamura and Yonai are good service men but almost
unknown outside the army and navy.
Yuki is generally believed to be the strong figure in
the Cabinet. A protege and follower of former Finance
Minister Takahashi, he can be expected to follow tenaciously
the cautious financial policies of his renowned predecessor.
It

the financial burden on the people as far as the military
will permit and will avoid sudden or drastic moves which
might endanger the financial structure:
o other members of the Cabinet are outstanding in
reputation and few are well known to the public.
Five. The indeterminate character of the Cabinet is
illustrated by the vagueness of its supposed policies as
published in the press:
(a) Thorough clarification of the national polity.
(b) Establishment of a definite diplomatic policy.
(c) Repletion of national defense and development of
national industry.
(a) Righteous administration under the Imperial
constitution:
Six. The Hayashi Cabinet is generally regarded as
a transitional and temporary government meant to tide over
a political crisis and perhaps constituting a short step
on the road to some sort of Japanese Fascism. The absence
of support from the political parties (no members of the
Cabinet are members of the parties) and its general weak-
ness would appear to presage a short life but such Cabinets
ofter survive for an unexpectedly long time in Japan. Some
observers are of the opinion that it will live for about a
year unless it is overthrown earlier by some
unforeseen cause: It will undoubtedly meet with opposition

February 2, 1937.
CONFIDENTIAL
Mr. Secretary:
FEB 4
1937
入
Cabinet crisis in Japan.
894.
NOTED
Mr. Grew telegraphs today from Tokyo that the Cabinet
99.
formed by General Hayashi was installed this morning and
that the reaction in Japan to.the new Cabinet is favorable.
894.00/692
The Prime Minister will be concurrently Minister for
Foreign Affairs and Minister of Education. There are ten
other Cabinet posts, each one of which is ordinarily as-
signed to a Cabinet officer, but, according to Mr. Grew,
these posts have been distributed among seven Cabinet of-
ficers, three of whom will hold concurrent posts. This
fact lends color to press reports that the Army obtained
General Hayashi's promise to make extensive administrative
changes before it permitted one of its officers to enter
the present Cabinet.
General Hayashi is known to be a man of resolute
FED 15.1937
character. He was decorated for bravery in the Russo-
Japanese War, and in 193l, while in command of the Japa-
nese forces in Korea, he despatched his troops to
Manchuria as soon as he heard of the Mukden Incident,
without awaiting orders from the authorities in Tokyo.
In l934, he replaced the notorious General Araki as
Minister of War and forced out of key positions a number
of

=== Page 366 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of officers who were unduly involved in political matters.
Following the coup de main of February 26, 1936, when
several leading statesmen were assassinated by the mili-
tary, he resigned his office and shortly thereafter re-
tired from the Army.
General Hayashi may be regarded as a "moderate" Army
man. He is a close friend of General Ugaki, who was
unable to constitute a Cabinet, and it is not unlikely
that he will lean on General Ugaki for guidance.
in tte Dapartat
Nothing definite is known of the character and
temperament of General Nakamura, the new Minister of War.
It may be mentioned that he was recently commander of the
Japanese forces in north China. Admiral Tonai, the new
Minister of the Navy, is reputed to be one of the most
able and forceful fleet commanders in the Japanese Navy.
He has not taken a prominent part, if indeed he has taken
any part, in political agitation.
Mr. Grew reports that the appointment as Minister of
Finance of Mr. Yuki, recently Governor of the Industrial
Bank (semi-official), has served to quiet the fears in
Japanese economic circles.
Of the other Cabinet officers it may be said that
only one, the Minister of Agriculture and Forestry, is
associated with any political party. The others may be
classified as "bureaucrats".
Press

=== Page 367 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
Press reports have indicated that it is the intention
of General Hayashi to appoint Hirosi Saito, Japanese Ambassador
to the United States, as Minister for Foreign Affairs. The
Embassy at Tokyo reports that it has no information in
confirmation of this story. A discreet inquiry made orally
and confidentially to an officer of the Japanese Embassy
here by Mr. Dooman of FE has elicited a reply indicating
that there is warrant for some doubt whether this appointment
will be made.
Prophecy or estimate with regard to the probable
policies of the new Cabinet would not be warranted on the
basis of the information thus far available. Our Embassy
at Tokyo states that it will send us an analysis.

Embassy's 43, February 6, 11 a.m.
Two appointments of significance were made yesterday:
-1
One. General Nakamura has resigned as War Minister
because of illness (apparently real) and last night
General Hajime Sugiyama, Inspector-General of Military
Training, was appointed as his successor. Sugiyama has
a stronger character is better known, and enjoys the
confidence f the army to a greater extent that Nakamura.
He has had wide experience both at home and abroad and
1937
has successfully held important posts in the army organi-
zation. He is an advocate of an augmented military machine
His appointment will undoubtedly strengthen the military
influence in the Cabinet and on the other hand will en-
hance discipline within the army. The Cabinet will be
strengthened by the acquisition of Sugiyama but as he will
probably insist upon the approval of the proposed military
budget the Cabinet's financial difficulties will not be
lessened. Sugiyama was succeeded as Inspector-General of
F
Military Trai ing by Terauchi, former War Minister.
G
Two.

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.99)
56-2
U -2- £52, Feb. 10, 7 p.m. from Tokyo
Two. Fukai resigned on the 8th as Governor of the
Bank of Japan and yesterday Seihiu Ikeda, formerly managing
director of the Mitsui Organization, was appointed as
his successor and Juichi Tsushima, formerly Vice Minister
of Finance and financial commissioner at New York and
London, was appointed Vice Governor. Both are well
known to American financial circles. It is reported that
Ikeda will follow a more positive policy of cooperation
with financial and industrial circles than did Fukai who
was regarded by financiers here as more of a scholar and
theorist than a practical banker. The appointments of
Ikeda and Tsushima are welcomed by financial circles in
Japan which believe that with Yuki a strong triumvirate
has been formed which while giving all possible assistance
to industry will be able to steady the dis  rbed
financial situation in Japan.
