=== Page 380 === (confidence 0.99)
b
58-1
- 3 -
POLITICAL
Foreign
The New Year for Japan, so far as her international
relations are concerned, opens in an ominous key. Not
only has her reputation in the world suffered an important
deterioration during the past year, but her relations with
Great Britain, Soviet Russia and China are on a far from
satisfactory footing and recently have been growing worse.
With the Dutch East Indies relations have improved. Only
with the United States, among her nearer neighbors, can it
9
be said that the status quo has been maintained, but with
the expiration of the Washington Naval Treaty, due to Japan's
intransigence, and the risk of a race in sea power and
fortifications, the long future as contrasted with the
immediate present holds out no evident grounds for optim-
ism. For this unhappy situation Japan herself is primarily
to blame, for she has played her cards unwisely and is now
reaping the logical result. It is the old story of the
defects arising out of a dual control of foreign policy
wherein the civil authorities of the Government, including
the Prime Minister and the Foreign Office, are overridden
by the military and are subject to the behests of the Army
and Navy which know or care little about developing good
relations with foreign countries but without whose support
the Cabinet could not long survive. We saw very much the
same thing working out in Germany in 1914.
Un i t e a s t a t e s
General Araki observed to the British Ambassador a
year or two ago that if it were not for the exclusion
clause

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.99)
58-2
- 4 -
clause of the Immigration Act of 1924, the relations
between Japan and the United States might be considered
as thoroughly satisfactory. While it is true that no
current controversies of prime importance are at present
sufficiently acute seriously to disturb those relations,
nevertheless we should not close our eyes to the fact
that several current issues may in due course become
acute and are potentially hazardous. First and foremost
is the naval issue which has automatically arisen with
the termination of the Washington Naval Treaty. How the
future will shape up in this respect we cannot yet fore-
see, but it is safe to say that if a race in naval build-
ing and in the construction of fortifications results, the
suspicion and uneasiness engendered will inevitably make
for tenseness in those relations. Other issues which
appear to be potentially disturbing are Japan's aggressive
policy in China with the possibility of that policy even-
tually interfering with American interests, and the flood-
ing of American markets with Japanese low-priced goods
against which we may ultimately be obliged to protect
ourselves with resultant international friction and irri-
tation. It appears unnecessary, however, to cross these
bridges until we come to them. The forthcoming unofficial
visit of representatives of American cotton textile manu-
facturers, who will endeavor to arrange a gentlemen's
agreement for the voluntary restriction by the Japanese
of their textile exports to the United States is a step
in the right direction to avoid the necessity of crossing
that particular bridge at all.
The Philippine Islands also remain as a potential,
although

=== Page 382 === (confidence 0.99)
58-3
- 5 -
although not an immediate, source of danger to the rela-
tions between Japan and the United States. The Davao land
question, quiescent for the moment, may arise again and
necessitate intervention by the American Government. The
strengthening of American naval and army bases in the
Philippines, and the further development of American air
services to and within the islands (thus tending to check
Japan's southward advance") would certainly act as an
irritant to the relations between the two countries. How-
ever, this again is a bridge which need not be crossed at
present.
Other current controversies such as the petroleum
problem, fisheries, et cetera, are susceptible of adjustment
by negotiation and in any case are not, at present at least,
of sufficient seriousness to disturb the even tenor of our
general relations. There is no good reason to believe that
these general relations may not maintain their present
satisfactory status for some time to come. On the contrary
there is very good reason to feel that the Japanese Govern-
ment values American friendship, especially in view of
Japan's increasing difficulties with other nations, and
will not purposely alienate the United States unless situa-
tions arise where Japan considers her own national interests
to be acutely involved. The outlook for l937, so far as
Japanese-American relations are concerned, therefore would
not at present appear to justify pessimism.
C h i n a
A recent series of articles in the Osaka MAINICHI by
Kumataro Honda, ex-Ambassador to Germany, castigates the
Government

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.99)
58-4
- 6 -
Government for what he calls "diplomacy by slogans, and
so far as China is concerned the term is apt. In 1931
and l932 the current slogan was Manchuria the Life-Line
of Japan. Once the Manchurian situation had been dealt
with, a new slogan shortly was heard: Japan the Stabi-
lizing Factor in East Asia". This latter slogan soon
developed into Hirota's three points for settling Japan's
relations with China, namely, the checking by the Chinese
Government of anti-Japanese activities and propaganda,
Sino-Japanese cooperation against communism and the
stabilizing of China's factual relations with "Manchukuo.*
Obviously these points were susceptible of a very broad
interpretation. Later, as various anti-Japanese incidents
took place in China, one series of "demands" after another
were formulated and publicly announced in a truculent and
aggressive tone. The failure of Japan's diplomacy and
consequent loss of face in China arise from the fact that
save in a few instances where local incidents have been
settled through minor concessions, few if any of these
general demands have been met. Protracted conferences
have taken place in Nanking and official announcements
have frequently been made in Tokyo to the effect that the
negotiations were proceeding, but so far as the Embassy is
aware they have led nowhere. Their failure has been ascribed
to China's "insincerity", a somewhat overworked expression
habitually and indiscriminately used by the Japanese to
characterize those foreign governments which fail to con-
cede Japan's desiderata by signing on the dotted line.
While
These points have been variously reported and variously
interpreted. They are set down here as described to me
by Mr. Hirota himself. J.C.G.

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.99)
58-5
- 7 -
While this Embassy is not informed of the specific results
of the Nanking conversations, it would seem that the
astuteness rather than the insincerity of the Chinese
Government has succeeded in playing the Japanese negotia-
tors along without surrendering Chinese sovereign rights.
Moreover serious military involvement in China has been
avoided by the Japanese Army because of the remarkably
improved morale and military forces of China and of the
changed situation in Siberia--a very different Eastern
Asia from 1932.
It does not appear necessary, for the purpose of this
report, to deal extensively or in detail with Japan's
diplomacy and activities in China during the past year.
Suffice it to say that the overt intention and efforts
of the Japanese military to detach the five northern pro-
vinces from the jurisdiction of Nanking largely miscarried;
that the support of the Japanese military of the widespread
smuggling operations not only became an international scan-
dal but went far to bring down on Japan the censure of
foreign countries including the United States and Great
Britain; and that, far from cooperating with Nanking in
an effort to control the anti-Japanese sentiment rapidly
developing throughout China, the Japanese have constantly
intensified that sentiment by their truculent and aggressive
attitude and tactics. Thus the Chengtu incident, wherein
Japanese lives were lost, was caused by their shortsighted
attempt to force the opening of their consulate in that
place which had been closed for some five years, in the
face of Chinese opposition, and this incident, whether
directly or indirectly, led to similar incidents at Pakhoi,
Hankow,

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.99)
58-6
- 8 -
Hankow, Shanghai, Changsha and Tsingtao. Each of these
several incidents, involving the death or injury of
Japanese subjects, led to violent demands for satisfaction
which in the majority of the cases have not been fully met.
Thus Japan has lost face, further anti-Japanese sentiment
has been evoked and a thoroughly vicious circle established.
Japanese support of the invasion of Suiyuan by Mongol troops
appears similarly to have miscarried and to have served
only to increase anti-Japanese feeling throughout the
country.
As Mr. Takao Saito, a leading member of the Minseito
Party, recently wrote in the ASAHI:
What is to be Japan's relation to China?
It is said ad nauseam that Japan's policy is to be
China's friend, that Japan's policy is to cooperate
with China for the sake of peace in the Far East,
and so forth. These phrases have been preached
for years, but I have yet to see any manifesta-
tions of actual friendliness in Sino-Japanese
relations.
"Had there been such manifestations there
could hardly have occurred the anti-Japanese
incidents which have taken place all over China.
Probably the real cause of animosity between the
two countries is that Japan, while asserting a
policy of friendship and understanding, takes
the attitude of continual readiness to knock
her neighbor down. Whether or not this is a
true characterization of Japan's policy is beside
the point; the fact is that real cooperation
requires abandoning the attitude. I believe it
is time for us to look into our continental
policy fundamentally."
Now, at the opening of the new year, the Japanese
program in China, whatever it may be, appears to have
come to a temporary halt owing to the new developments
arising out of the capture and subsequent release of
Chiang Kai-shek by Marshal Chang Hsueh-liang. The
Japanese are clearly marking time. Whether these develop-
ments will lead to an intensification of the anti-Japanese
movement

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.99)
587
-9 -
movement in China and whether, and to what extent, they
will induce a stiffening of the future policy of Nanking
remains to be seen. Similarly, there is as yet no indica-
tion as to how they may affect the immediate development
of Japan's policy and tactics in China. Japan has seriously
lost face through the failure of her negotiations with
Nanking. The Japanese Cabinet will be taxed with that
failure in the coming session of the Diet and while
Japanese Cabinets have a way of carrying on in spite of
censure and lost confidence, it is not inconceivable that
the Cabinet may fall. If it does fall there will be every
probability of the appointment of a more reactionary cabinet,
but whether the present Cabinet falls or not, no one can yet
predict with any assurance if the trend of policy during
the present year will aim to develop friendship with China
on a more stable basis or will move in the direction of
more positive aggressiveness. The outlook in that respect
is still quite nebulous.
The Japanese nation seems to be somewhat thunderstruck
by the sudden and unexpected determination of China to yield
no more to Japanese pressure. The nation is, figuratively,
scratching its head and wondering what it should do next.
There has been some discussion in the newspapers of a
reorientation of policy toward China, but there has been
no indication as yet of the direction which that reorienta-
tion will take. It is strange but true that Japan appears
to have been the last to appreciate the changed conditions
in China. Now that Japan realizes that its bluff of military
pressure no longer works, some other aggressive method of
dominating North China may be tried. Economic cooperation,
with

=== Page 387 === (confidence 0.99)
58-8
- 10 -
with emphasis on its aim being, "improvement of the
condition of the people", is an old army plan that is
being advocated increasingly by army spokesmen which
might be an outlet for Japanese expansionist activities,
not too objectionable to the Nanking Government. For
the present it appears that Japan is not attracted by
the thought of a frontal attack, on her own volition,
against Nanking. More probable is the method of peri-
pheral penetration and digging in in North China and
along the Mongolian frontier.
There can be no doubt as to the soundness of the
statements contained in the final paragraph of our des-
patch No. 1630 of January 7, 1936, surveying the general
situation facing Japan at the commencement of the last
year, which said, in relation to Japan's policy with China:
"The procedure to be followed and the methods
to be pursued are open to influence by some or
all of the factors suggested in this despatch,
but the expansionist urge is fundamental, and
I think there is no doubt that whether quietly
and gradually or openly and aggressively Japanese
energies will be found, from now on, steadily
directed towards consolidating Japan's control
in North China and Mongolia as a primary axiom
of her future strategic safety and economic
welfare."
Sov i et R us s i a.
Good relations between Japan and Soviet Russia in the
present political era are an anachronism. Apart from
frontier incidents and other minor troubles it is per-
fectly obvious that Russia's well justified fears of
eventual Japanese expansion into Mongolia and Siberia on
the one hand, coupled with Japan's fear of the spread of
communism in neighboring territory on the other hand, are
in

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.99)
58-9
-11 -
in themselves sufficient to prevent the development of
any basis of mutual confidence. Nevertheless, up to the
month of last November, diplomatic efforts had succeeded
in elaborating a fair modus vivendi under which Soviet-
Japanese relations might be carried along for the time
being without too great risk of critical developments.
The appointment of a mixed commission to consider frontier
questions had been accepted in principle and a fisheries
treaty, distinctly favorable to Japan, had been initialled
and was ready to be signed on November 20. The Soviet
defenses in Siberia and Vladivostok, coupled with the
double-tracking of the Siberian Railway, had been brought
to a point of effectiveness where Moscow could afford to
regard the bluster and sabre-rattling of the Kwantung Army
with a degree of cynical tolerance, while the Japanese on
the other hand were too busy modernizing their army and
aiming to strengthen their forces in Manchuria to a point
of reasonable balance with the Soviet forces to the north
to seek further grounds for early conflict.
This temporary amelioration in Soviet-Japanese rela-
tions, however, was rudely shattered by the conclusion of
the Japanese pact with Germany. While ostensibly the pact
aims only at cooperation in combating the activities of the
Comintern in spreading communistic propaganda and practice
abroad and is therefore not openly directed against the
Soviet Government, nevertheless the existence of a secret
understanding or agreement between the General Staffs of
the two countries is generally accepted as fact and the
Soviet Ambassador in Tokyo asserts that Moscow is in pos-
session of precise evidence to that effect. Political
opponents

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.99)
58-10
-12 -
opponents of the Cabinet in Japan maintain that if the
news of this pact had not been permitted to leak before
November 20, the Soviet-Japanese fisheries treaty would
have been signed and all would have been well and they
therefore charge the Foreign Minister with a grave blunder
in allowing this leakage. But it is inconceivable that
Moscow was not well aware, long before that date, of what
was going on in Berlin, and the blunder, if such it was,
would seem to lie not with the leakage before a certain
date but in entering into any such pact at all. The
explanation is simple. The pact and whatever secret
agreement may be attached to it were concluded by the
Japanese military, the negotiations with Germany having
been largely carried on by Major General Oshima, the
Japanese Military Attaché in Berlin. We do not know
whether these negotiations were conducted with the blessing
of the Japanese Foreign Office, but since the Foreign
Office has long been assiduously working to improve and
stabilize Japan's relations with the USSR as a matter of
major policy, it is reasonable to question whether the
shattering effect on those relations of a pact with Ger-
many would not have been abundantly clear in advance and
whether the civil Government in Tokyo could have been in
sympathy with so sharp a divergence in the political
orientation of Japan's diplomacy. Certainly the pact has
called forth widespread criticism throughout Japan. Here
again the dual system of policy control in Japan would
seem to have manifested itself. At any rate, the die is
now cast and it is obvious that Japan's relations with
Soviet

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.98)
58-11
- 13 -
Soviet Russia have suffered a rude setback which is not
likely to be overcome in the near future. Moreover we
may be sure that the Soviet Government will continue to
act on the principle that the only language understood
by the Japanese is force, and that when struck, whether
by a minor frontier incursion or by some broader form of
aggression, the wisest policy to follow is promptly to
strike back with double the force.
G e r m a n y
That the treaty between Japan and Germany envisages
anything in the nature of a pact of military mutual assist-
ance in case of war seems highly unlikely. It is said that
Japan did her best to get Poland to participate in the
arrangement with Germany but without success, and with
Poland independent of commitments, and with France at
Germany's back, it is hardly likely that Germany would
undertake to attack Russia in case of a Soviet war with
Japan. Nevertheless the existence of an agreement for an
exchange of military information and for the supply of
arms and ammunition and technical aid to Japan in return
for commercial commodities from Manchuria is a reasonable
hypothesis. Whatever the precise nature of the agreement
concluded, it is evident that a new orientation has arisen
in Japan's policy, that her relations with Germany are
likely to be strengthened as time goes on and that this
new orientation is bound, whether intentionally or other-
wise, to react unfavorably on any improvement of Japan's
relations not only with Soviet Russia but with Great Britain
and other democracies as well.
I t a 1 y

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.99)
58-12.
- 14 -
I t a 1 y
While there is no good reason to believe that Japan
is purposely aligning herself with a Fascist bloc, never-
theless her recent almost simultaneous pacts with both
Germany and Italy have naturally given rise to such a
theory. In actual fact, the agreements by which Japan
will withdraw her Legation from Addis Ababa and substitute
therefor a Consulate, while Italy opens a Consulate General
in Mukden, thereby implying de facto recognition respectively
of the Italian acquisition of Ethiopia and of the inde-
pendence" of "Manchukuo", would appear to envisage reci-
procally privileged trade relations rather than to have
any important political bearing.
Great Britain
Japan would like good relations with Great Britain
but, as the British Ambassador recently said to me, she
would like them on the basis of "all take and no give".
Sir Robert Clive says that his Military Attaché is con-
stantly telling him how very friendly and cordial the
Japanese army officers are to him, to which the Ambassador
replies that this cordiality is but skin-deep and is in-
duced solely by the consideration that the Japanese Army
has at last awakened to the fact that in the event of a
Japanese war with Soviet Russia Great Britain might not
preserve even a benevolent neutrality. This fact gives
them pause. A large element of the British people today
are anti-Japanese in sentiment as a result of the Man-
churian episode, Japan's aggressiveness in China, trade
questions, the Keelung incident and other controversies.
In the meantime no serious setbacks in Great Britain's
relations

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.99)
58-13
-15 -
relations with Soviet Russia have occurred during the
past three years. These relations are not cordial but
they are at least satisfactory. The Soviets know that
communistic propaganda in Great Britain will not be
tolerated. Naval and trade agreements have been concluded.
Thus the former enmity of the two countries is largely
evaporating while Britain's relations with Japan have
steadily grown worse, and the Japanese Army has at last
grasped the possible future implications of these develop-
ments which are beginning to cause anxiety. Under these
circumstances it is not surprising that Japanese army
officers should go out of their way to be cordial to
their British confreres.
Potentially serious is the Japanese program of con-
trol in various parts of China because such control is
certain, sooner or later, to interfere with British in-
terests in that country. The notorious Amau Staten it
and other pronouncements of Japanese policy relating to
China have not served to render any less determined Great
Britain's intention to support and develop those interests,
and it is reasonable to surmise that, if carried to their
logical conclusion, the respective policies of Great Britain
and Japan in China are bound to clash.
In the meantime Anglo-Japanese relations have been
rendered difficult by trade controversies in India, Australia,
and elsewhere, and by the oil question, while the Keelung
incident, wherein British naval seamen are alleged to have
been assaulted and injured by Japanese police in Formosa,
has further embittered these relations. As the Japanese
Government has, up to the present, refused to make adequate
amends

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.99)
58-14
-16 -
amends for this incident, the situation has come to an
impasse. The Commander-in-Chief of the British East
Asiatic Fleet has postponed his official scheduled visit
to Japan while embarrassing questions have been asked and
resolutions introduced in the British Parliament. Although
Prince Chichibu, the Emperor's brother, is to represent the
Emperor at the forthcoming Coronation in London in May, it
would appear, under present circumstances, that he may not
receive so warm a welcome as would have been the case a few
years ago.
In Japan itself, much outspoken criticism is heard by
those who dislike the agreement with Germany and who would
prefer to strengthen the old Anglo-Japanese friendship, a
friendship which at present is distinctly on the decline.
N e t h e r 1 an d s
Relations with the Netherlands have improved measurably
during the past year due to the ironing out of the trade
and economic difficulties with the Dutch East Indies. The
first obstacle to good relations was removed by the final
settlement of the protracted shipping dispute and with that
out of the way satisfactory progress is now being made in
connection with the much desired commercial treaty.
It will be recalled that in 1934 the Japanese sent a
commercial mission to The Hague, headed by a man of am-
bassadorial rank, for the purpose of inaugurating negotia-
tions for a commercial treaty. This commission so antago-
nized the Netherlands Government by its inept use of
publicity and its attempt to address the people directly,
with promises of cheap goods, that although six months
were spent in Holland they were at length forced to return
with

=== Page 394 === (confidence 0.99)
58-15
-17 -
with no result. When the shipping dispute arose the
Japanese attempted to use the negotiations on this ques-
tion as a lever to force the matter of the commeroial
treaty negotiations but the Dutch stood firm and refused
flatly to take up the question of the commercial treaty
until after the shipping matter was out of the way. They
eventually won their point and the Japanese ceased their
dilatory and obstructionist tactics with the result that
last year the shipping question was settled to the satis-
faction of both parties.
Negotiations were then resumed on the question of the
commercial treaty and are now in progress between the
Japanese Consul General at Batavia and a member of the
East Indies Government appointed for that purpose. It
was agreed that publicity should be confined to the
issuance of a simple communiqué stating that conversations
were under way and that nothing further should be given
out until the negotiations had reached a conclusion one
way or another. I am informed that they have now reached
a stage where a draft has been submitted to the home
government at The Hague for approval. In this connection
the question of sugar is a most important one, and it is
the object of the East Indies Government to arrange that
Jepan shall continue to purchase 90 per cent of their
totel sugar imports from Java; at the same time they
hoped to obtain a guarantee that Japan will not increase
the production of sugar in Formosa, although it is doubt-
ful whether they will be successful in this direction.
Much of the sugar imported by Japan from the East Indies
is re-exported to China. At first the Dutch objected to
this

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.99)
58-16
- 18 -
this but recently they have been willing to give way on
this point and have come to the conclusion that it is
just as profitable to sell sugar to Japan for re-export
as to attempt to increase their Chinese market at the
expense of the Japanese.
It will therefore be seen that considerable progress
has been made during the past year and that relations have
improved between the Netherlands and Japan and while, of
course, a wary and watchful eye is being kept upon Japan's
new policy of "Southward Advance", there is nothing on the
immediate horizon which should interfere with continued
improvement of relations between these two countries.
A tentative invitation from the Japanese Government
to the Netherlands to send its fleet on an official visit
has been tacitly left in abeyance for the present owing
to the desire of the Netherlands Government to afford at
least moral support to the British Government in its
attitude towards the Keelung incident, mentioned elsewhere.
Domestic
Protest from the people is the significant new factor
in Japanese politics. As the year 1937 opens, popular
opposition to the Government's policies is making itself
felt with greater emphasis than at any time since before
1931. Cabinets in the past have been subjected to oppo-
sition pressure, but for many years no opposition has so
directly derived from the people themselves.
This does not mean that public opinion is even approach-
ing control. Far from it. There is, however, an encouraging
growth of popular sentiment that the people should have a
voice in current discussion of political questions.. This
sentiment

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.98)
58-17
-19-
sentiment, growing by reason of the economic and
political difficulties brought on the nation by the
military, lacks unification and leadership; but there
is in the movement some prospect of its eventually
developing into a check on the belligerent attitude of
the country now dictated by the military who are still
in the saddle. Observers have often felt that the
greatest international danger from Japan is the vul-
nerability of her system of government to the provoking
of a war not initiated by the desires of the people. Any
9
tendency for the people to demand the right to be con-
9
sulted is salutary. It is a long road to a mature ap-
preciation on the part of any population, no matter how
advanced in political experience, of the cost of war; but
the first stretches of that road -- possibly to be aban-
doned later -- have at least appeared in the virulence of
recent protest against the secret diplomacy which faced
the country with an accomplished fact. The country has
recognized the agreement with Germany as holding serious
dangers to Japan's position in the world. The protest
has rapidly spread to other subjects also. Public opinion
in Japan has a way of dissipating itself ineffectually,
and that may be the upshot of the present protest, but
as the year opens it is conspicuous and real.
In addition to the disapproval of the agreement with
Germany, of the failure of negotiations with Nanking, and
of the miscarriage of relations with the Soviet Union,
there are domestic factors contributing to the insecurity
of the Hirota Cabinet. When Hirota formed the Cabinet
after the incident of February 26, 1936, the military
apparently exacted from him a promise of action on clari-
fication

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
- 20 -
fication of the national polity, improvement of the
livelihood of the people, strengthening of national
defense, and the adoption of a positive foreign policy.
On the latter two points Hirota has been obliging: the
military budget is sharply increased, and the army-favored
anti-communism agreement is certainly a concession to a
"positive" foreign policy. On the other two points, how-
ever, Hirota has temporized. Certain of the military were
pushing, under the first point, various proposals of ad-
ministrative and Diet reform, which Hirota effectively side-
stepped by endless reference to special commissions and
committees. Playing for time, he was finally aided by
other and more pressing political problems to distract
attention. The second point, improvement of the liveli-
hood of the people, has not been accorded even the pretense
of fulfillment. Rising prices, rising debt, and rising
taxes contradict the commitment; and what has been done
in the direction of control of the electric power industry
is poorly comprehended by and of doubtful advantage to the
people.
The military appear to be in position to oust the
Hirota Cabinet whenever desired, explaining their action,
when they decide to take it, on the ground of the Cabinet's
failure to execute the platform; but thus far there is no
indication that the military agree that any other cabinet
could do better. The military are still the dominant
influence in control of the Government and may continue to
be so during l937, but as evils caused by military domina-
tion continue to accumulate, sentiment against that domina-
tion increases in volume and force. One field of notable
opposition

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.99)
- 21 -
opposition is in financial and industrial circles, where
business is more and more openly resentful of fascist
tendencies of control by the Government. Another result
is increased confidence in the political parties as spokes-
men for the people. The parties are beginning to enjoy a
respect which they have not enjoyed for half a dozen years
past.
Meantime the Diet will meet with 44 per cent of the
seats of the lower house held by the Minseito, 36 per cent
by the Seiyukai, and the remaining 20 per cent divided
among Labor, other small parties, and independents. The
Minseito are represented in the Hirota Cabinet by the
Minister of Communications and the Minister of Commerce
and Industry, the Seiyukai by the Minister of Railways and
the Minister of Agriculture and Forestry. The most im-
portant ministers of the Hirota Cabinet do not have party
connections.
At the opening of 1937, to recapitulate, domestic
politics in Japan are characterized by popular criticism
of the Government (particularly for its foreign policy),
and public dissatisfaction with the mounting cost of living,
budget increase, and pressure of taxes. Against this op-
position the advantages enjoyed by the Hirota Cabinet in
its effort to stay in office are, first, that the military
(still in control) are not yet determined that a change
would be an improvement, and second, that the political
parties still lack unity of attack.
MILITARY
While there is no doubt that the Japanese Army
suffered a severe setback in public estimation as a
result

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.99)
- 22 -
result of the incident of February 26 last, nevertheless
subsequent developments have served to offset this un-
favorable opinion and to bring the Army back to a position
of strength and popularity in public opinion to a degree
even greater than formerly. The quick and bloodless
suppression of the insurrection and the efficient manner
in which order was restored by the Army authorities met
with public approval. In addition, the resignation of the
generals who held themselves responsible for the incident,
the drastic punishment of the officers convicted of direct
responsibility and the announced determination of the
military to purge the Army of the "direct actionists"
and to exact discipline throughout all ranks created a
very favorable impression on the public at large. General
Terauchi, the War Minister, has undoubtedly gone a long
way toward the suppression of the "direct action" elements
and the establishment of stricter discipline in the Army
and has also had a large measure of success in curbing
the political activities of officers on the active list.
Furthermore, the Army is credited with the definite in-
tention of forcing reorganization of the Government and
of effecting various social measures for the good of the
farming community and other elements of the people, all
of which has served to enhance its popularity.
Although the Army suffered a temporary loss of prestige
at the time of the February 26 Incident, its continued
influence on the political life of the nation was demon-
strated by the fact that, headed by General Terauchi, it
was the bulwark around which the new Cabinet was formed
and was the principal factor in shaping the new Government's
policy. In return for General Terauchi's promise of stricter
military

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.99)
- 23 -
military discipline, mentioned above, Mr. Hirota incorporated
four desiderata of the Army, and also of the insurgents
themselves, in the Government's platform: (1) clarification
of the national polity; (2) repletion of national defense;
(3) the adoption of a "positive" foreign policy; and (4)
stabilization of the livelihood of the people through the
establishment of state control of industries and finances,
the readjustment of taxation, the enactment of social legis-
lation, administrative reform, et cetera. It would seem
that the first three desires of the Army, above-mentioned,
have been fulfilled, although very little has been done
toward the "stabilization of the livelihood of the people.
The War Minister has repeatedly urged the Government to
expedite the adoption of the reforms comprised in this
shibboleth and the blame for slow progress in this line
has fallen on the civilian members of the Cabinet.
Thus, under the skillful and diplomatic leadership
of General Terauchi, the Army has been able to regain the
sympathy and support of the populace by espousing the
popular aims of the February 26 insurgents and on the
other hand by rigorously suppressing the unpopular "direct
action" elements in the Army.
While there is a current outcry in the press against
various blunders and failures in Japanese diplomacy, for
which the military are largely responsible, most of this
publicity comes from the politicians who are threatening
dire attacks on the Cabinet in the coming session of the
Diet. With the public at large however, these politicians
are in general discredited, as is the Diet as a whole,
owing to the many scandals arising out of individual cor-
ruption and the fact that few deputies after election make
any effort whatever to carry out their election promises.
The

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.99)
- 24 -
The fact that these parliamentarians are at present allowed
to attack the Cabinet in the press in unbridled terms is
in itself an indication that the military have full con-
fidence in their strength and popularity and there is every
probability that a good deal of the present fire and slaugh-
ter aired in the press by these politicians will peter out
when it comes to actual debate in the Diet.
Past experience with the Diet has shown that the
attacks against the military made in that body by isolated
though courageous liberals are apt to have little or no
effect on the policy of the Government.
So far as foreign policy is concerned, it is generally
conceded that the Army was responsible for the following
developments: the setting up by the North China Garrison
and the Kwantung Army of the Hopei-Chahar Political Council
and the East Hopei Autonomous Régime, nominally autonomous
but in reality under the control of the Japanese military
(it is reported that Japanese influence in the Hopei-Chahar
Political Council has been decreased through the efforts
of the Chinese Government); the instigation of the East
Suiyuan Mongols against the Chinese forces of that province;
and the conclusion of the Japanese-German anti-Comintern
agreement which was brought about largely through the
efforts of the Japanese Military Attaché in Berlin and Mr.
Toshio Shiratori, present Minister to Sweden, former tru-
culent spokesman of the Foreign office, and well-known for
a number of years for his close collaboration with the Army.
In the opinion of competent observers, the Italo-Japanese
modus vivendi in Ethiopia and Manchuria probably includes
a provision for technical military assistance to Japan.
Thus, the conclusion of the agreement with Italy fitted in
with

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.98)
- 25 -
with the desires of the military, whatever may have been
their part in fostering the agreement. Japanese opinion
varies as to the advisability of the above-mentioned moves
from the point of view of this country's general diplomatic
policy.
The public at large is aware of the announcements of
the military that there will be no aggression by force or
territorial acquisition in China end that the expansion of
Japanese interests will be effected by peaceful economic
penetration. This commends itself to the public. While
the conclusion of the anti-Comintern agreement with Germany
has undoubtedly aggravated diplomatic relations between
Japan end the USSR, the Kwantung Army's strongly entrenched
position in Manchuria did not seem in any way to be menaced
at the close of the year.
1
The people in general are inclined to hold the Foreign
Office rather than the Army responsible for the patent
failures in diplomacy which have occurred in various direc-
tions. On the other hand, the enlightened minority feels
perhaps more than ever that the Army is responsible for
the unpleasant diplomatic situation with which Japan is
now faced.
The important fact should not be overlooked that the
Cabinet's approval has been secured of military appropria-
tions in the 1937-38 budget of ¥727,965,556, the largest
Army appropriations in the history of Japan and an increase
of ¥219,648,856 over the 1936-37 figure. There is very
little likelihood that this amount will be materially
reduced by the Diet.
With regard to technical matters, the Japanese Army,
including

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.99)
- 26 -
including its air force, expanded numerically somewhat
during the year 1936. The North China Garrison was in-
creased to a strength of five or six thousand men. There
was an increase in the tank and air units of the Kwantung
Army, which otherwise was probably not increased signifi-
cantly in strength. Progress was made in the Army's moderni-
zation program, the Air Corps and Infantry and Field Artil-
lery regiments were reorganized, arms and equipment were
increased and improved, and the military forces familiarized
themselves with the technique of the new materiel. However,
no perceptible progress was made in the tactical employment
of new weapons or organizations.
As regards the outlook for l937, there are indications
that there will be further substantial increases in aviation
units, in weapons for infantry and artillery, and in the
strength of the Army, particularly in Manchuria. In general,
the Army will end the year 1937 stronger and better equipped
than ever but still behind the times in tactical training
and combat efficiency.
In conclusion, there seems to be no diminution in the
Army's influence on both the domestic and foreign policies
of Japan.
NAVAL
With the expiration on December 31, 1936, of the
Washington Naval Treaty of 192l-22 and of the London Naval
Treaty of 1930, Japan enters the new year in a non-treaty
status vis-à-vis the major naval Powers of the world. She
faces the possibility of unlimited competition in naval
construction from the United States and Great Britain and
she is confronted with the possibility of greatly increased
fortification

=== Page 404 === (confidence 0.99)
- 27 -
fortification by these two Powers of their Pacific and
Far Eastern possessions. This situation has been, of course,
brought about by the action of Japan (1) in giving notice
in December, l934, of her intention to abrogate the naval
treaties; (2) in withdrawing from the London Naval Conference
of l936 and refusing thereafter to adhere to the Three-
Power Treaty; and (3) in permitting the treaties to lapse
on December 31, 1936, without entering into any agreements
with the other naval Powers either with respect to quanti-
tative or qualitative restrictions or with respect to the
restriction of fortifications in the Pacific and Far East.
The main reason which impelled Japan to withdraw from
the naval treaties and to refuse to enter into further
commitments was, and is, her passionate unwillingness to
bind herself legally and contractually to a position of
inferiority; in short the 5-5-3 ratio had, in the minds of
the Japanese, so humiliated this country and had so wounded
its amour-propre as to have become intolerable and the nation
made up its mind, probably shortly after the signature of
the London Treaty of 1930, to pay whatever price might be
necessary to rid itself of the stigma of inferiority.
Another, and perhaps no less cogent reason, was the
conviction that the Washington ratios no longer provided
Japan with the measure of security which she felt she
enjoyed at the time the treaties were signed. It cannot
be denied that conditions have greatly changed since 1922
and, especially with regard to aviation, developments have
undoubtedly reduced Japan's security from attack. Further-
more the adoption in recent years by Japan of a role of
stabilizing factor in East Asia has rendered it necessary
that

=== Page 405 === (confidence 0.99)
- 28 -
that this country free itself of restrictions which might
hamper the effectiveness of this new policy.
In appraising the results of Japan's action in casting
the naval treaties overboard and choosing to remain in a
non-treaty status with the other Powers, the following may
be listed among the possible benefits to Japan: (1) Due to
her geographic position and her natural defenses arising
from the insular character of her possessions and mainland,
with the many harbors, inland waterways and so forth, Japan
feels that even with her relatively slender resources she
is in a position, if free from both quantitative and qua-
litative restrictions, to build a navy of such a kind as to
render her secure from attack; (2) Japan feels that her
position in the Orient, especially vis-à-vis China, will
be immeasurably strengthened by this evidence of her un-
willingness to submit to restrictions imposed by Western
Powers; in short she hopes to gain "face" with the Chinese;
(3) Japanese naval experts are confident, and there is some
evidence to support this view, that they can attain a measure
of parity by the qualitative method, that is, by building
vessels of new design, or great size (45,000 tons to 60,000
tons) with gun calibers of possibly as high as 2l inches,
thus rendering existing battleships obsolete. They recall
that Germany vastly improved her relative naval position
vis-à-vis Great Britain when the British brought out the
DREADNOUGHT class in 1906, thus rendering obsolete al1 her
own pre-DREADNOUGHT battleships.
The disadvantages to Japan of the non-treaty status
may, however, prove to outweigh the benefits. In the first
place, the Washington Naval Treaty was an integral part of
a

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.99)
- 29 -
a political group of treaties, and the Japanese by abro-
gating these have destroyed the collective system of security
which has governed the Pacific and Far Eastern area since
1922. In order to correct this situation she may be forced
to seek security through new political agreements, perhaps
of a bilateral character. But the former Anglo-Japanese
Alliance is probably forever lost to her and there appears
on the horizon at present to be no political arrangement
which can possibly replace either that alliance or the
collective system which she has just abandoned.
Furthermore Japan's action in respect to the naval
treaties has completed her isolation, an isolation which
was begun with her withdrawal from the League of Nations
in March, 1934. In addition to this, Japan will now be
faced with the threat of a possible increase in Pacific
and Far Eastern fortifications of American and British
possessions and territories, a threat which if not immediate
or actual, at least will prove a costly bargaining point in
any negotiations for naval or other political agreements
which Japan might be in the future forced to seek.
As far as the acquisition of "face" vis-à-vis China is
concerned, recent events have proven that expectations on
this score have abjectly failed to materialize. Japan was
not only checkmated in her negotiations at Nanking last
fall, but she is at present faced with a stiffer resistance
and a more united China than she has ever before encountered.
Other results have not been happy. The realization
that isolation constitutes a real danger to a country,
especially in time of war, has made itself keenly felt
during the past year and Japan has consequently made real
efforts

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.99)
-30 -
efforts during the past six months to emerge from this
condition. In November the Japanese Government announced
the anti-Comintern agreement with Germany, the arrangements
with Italy involving at least de facto recognition of
territory brought by extra-legal methods under the domination
of the other, and shortly thereafter the agreement with Po-
land for the increase of cultural relations with that
strategically important buffer state between Germany and
Russia. The reaction in Moscow was immediate and vigorous
with the result that relations with that heavily-armed
neighbor have been badly strained.
In the light of evidence now available it therefore
may be argued that in a political sense Japan has not
improved her position by failing to renew or to enter
into new naval agreements with the great Powers; in the
technical naval sense, however, bearing in mind chiefly
considerations of defense, it is quite possible to believe
that Japan may have been the gainer, although this remains
to be seen.
ECONOMIC
Japanese economic circles view the new year of 1937.
with mixed sentiments. On the one hand it is expected
that there will be increased domestic economic activity,
caused by the huge governmental expenditures, with the
resultant credit inflation and rising prices. On the
other hand, the threat of further governmental control
of industry and finance under the "quasi-wartime economic
policy" desired by the military, gives rise to a sense of
uncertainty and insecurity in business circles. Conse-
quently, as Mr. Ogawa, the Minister of Commerce and
Industry

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.99)
- 31 -
Industry himself stated in his New Year's message to the
nation, "The situation at home and abroad does not admit
of unqualified optimism".
Among the favorable elements in the situation, perhaps
the most important is the industrial activity of the nation.
Most of the manufacturing industries of Japan operated at a
high level of activity throughout l936 and some industries
promise to show even more activity in 1937 than in 1936, due
largely to the so-called "munitions boom". The huge govern-
mental expenditures for replenishment of national defense
have greatly benefited certain industries (such as metal-
working, airplane manufacturing, textile manufacturing and
allied industries) and the prosperity of these industries
has given a fillip to industry in general. With the in-
creased expenditures contemplated in the Army's six-year
replenishment program, calling for the expenditure of
Yen 3,300,000,000, the "munitions industries" can regard
the future with confidence. At the same time, Japan's
export trade has been well sustained during the past year,
and, unless further and more drastic restrictions are im-
posed on Japanese goods in foreign lands, it should continue
fairly active in l937. The industries devoted primarily or
partially to the manufacture of merchandise for export there-
fore can expect a continuation of activity. Japanese shipping
is reasonably prosperous, and the shipbuilding industry is
unusually so, some of the yards being booked up with orders
until the middle of 1938.
The farmers had an unusually good year in 1936, with
bumper rice crops and rising prices of agricultural pro-
ducts. This has added greatly to the purchasing power of
the

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.99)
- 32-
the nation and has given an additional impetus to certain
industries, especially the cotton tertile industry. The
farmers are also being assisted by various price control
measures passed by the Diet in its last session, and con-
sequently agricultural communities are more optimistic than
in years past.
Money rates are easy, which fact contributes greatly to
the activity of industrialists and merchants. The policy of
the Ministry of Finance since the incumbency of Mr. Baba,
Minister for Finance, has been to keep money rates low
(principally in order to float Government bonds at as low
rates as possible) and the policy has been successful up
to the present. Even at the year-end, when call-money usually
becomes scarce and rates rise because of the demand for money
for the year-end settlements, money was plentiful and cheap.
There is no reason to believe that money rates will rise to
any great extent in l937, and therefore merchants and in-
dustrialists are reasonably certain of a cheap supply of
funds in the coming year.
There are other factors, however, which are less favor-
able to the economic world of Japan. Chief among these is
the uncertainty in regard to the Government's policy of state
control of key industries. It will be remembered that after
the incident of February 26, 1936, there was a demand on the
part of the military for further control of industry by the
state, in order to prepare for possible war. At the same
time there was such a sharp reaction to the suggestion from
the business world that Mr. Baba, soon after he took office
as Minister for Finance, assured the nation that the Govern-
ment would not undertake financial and economic measures
which

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.99)
- 33 -
which would cause radical alterations in the economic
structure. Despite this assurance, however, business
circles are still uneasy, as it appears that the military
have been steadily and strongly pressing for progress
toward the state control of key industries. (It will be
recalled that state control has already been established
over the petroleum and motor-car industries, both at the
insistence of the military.) During 1936 a plan was formu-
lated to nationalize the electric power industry and the
proposal will be introduced into the Diet at the present
session, in a form which will give the Government partial
ownership and control of the industry. If the measure suc-
ceeds in obtaining the approval of the Diet, it is feared
by economic circles that the plan may be extended to other
basic industries, resulting in state control of industry in
accordance with the system of "quasi-wartime economy". Such
a contingency would not be at all welcome to Japanese eco-
nomic circles.
Another disturbing factor in the situation consists of
the projected taxation reform scheme and the proposed sharp
increase of existing taxes. The Army and the Navy, in the
proposed budget for the 1937-38 fiscal year, have included
enormous (to Japan) sums for replenishment and rebuilding
purposes. It will be impossible to finance these additional
demands by the issuance of deficit bonds, owing to the limited
capacity of the market to absorb such bonds, and consequently
Mr. Baba has had to turn to an increase of taxation and to
an increase of tariff rates. It is not certain that the
taxation measures will pass the Diet in their present forms,
but the practical certainty that some sort of tax increase
will be enacted is reacting unfavorably on business in Japan.
The

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.99)
-34 -
The sudden and sharp rise in prices which occurred in
the latter part of l936 and which continued until the close
of the year is also causing some uneasiness in economic and
official circles. The Bank of Japan wholesale commodity
index shows a rise of 1l.9 per cent between December, 1935,
and December, 1936. Some of the rise has been due to specu-
lation, but most of it appears to be due to governmental
spending in excess of the producing capacity of the country,
causing shortages of commodities; to credit inflation caused
by governmental expenditures; to a reflection in Japan of
increased prices abroad; to arbitrary price increases, such
as the increase in the prices of monopoly tobaceo; and to
anticipation of higher prices when the projected tariff
increase is approved by the Diet. In line with the rise in
prices, the cost of living in Japan is rising, and it is
feared that labor troubles will follow the rise in the cost
of living. Already Government employees are complaining
about their stationary salaries and the increased cost of
living, and Mr. Baba has announced that the Government will
take "appropriate measures" to check the rising trend of
prices should they become so high as to "endanger the national
life". The rise in prices, it is generally feared,will have
an adverse effect upon Japan's export industries and will
disturb the domestic economic system.
Another disturbing element in Japan's economic situation
lies in the prospect of a still larger adverse foreign trade
balance in 1937. The visible foreign trade balance for 1936
was unfavorable to Japan by about Yen 134,000,000, but this
was largely offset by an alleged favorable invisible trade
balance (details of which are not yet available). In 1937,
however, it is expected that the export trade will not
register

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
-35 -
register any appreciable increase. The rate of increase
of Japan's export trade has been declining recently, and
it is possible that, because of further restrictions abroad
against Japanese goods, the trade will become stationary
in 1937. At the same time, it is expected that Japan's
import trade will be considerably larger in l937 than in
1936, due to the heavy governmental purchases caused by
the military replenishment programs, to barter agreements
with other countries requiring Japan to purchase certain
quantities of goods, and to speculative purchases made in
anticipation of the proposed increase in tariff rates.
Toward the close of the year it was feared that it might
become necessary for the Government to institute some sort
of control of imports and exports, in order to avoid too
great an adverse balance of trade in l937.
Although, therefore, on the surface Japan's economic
world appears prosperous and active, there are various
factors undermining this prosperity which may become more
prominent and adversely affect Japanese economic circles
during l937. It will be observed that the present and
prospective economic disorders mentioned above are all due
directly or indirectly to the military and their budget
demands. The military desire "quasi-wartime" state control
of industries; it is because of the military demands that
taxes and tariffs must be raised; prices are rising partly
because of the heavy governmental spending for munitions
and military equipment; and the prospective adverse trade
balance in l937 will be due in part to purchases of materials
for the military replenishment schemes. As long as the
military remain the dominating influence in the Government,
therefore

=== Page 413 === (confidence 1.00)
-36-
therefore, Japan's economic world cannot be considered as
sound and stable.
FINANCIAL
Like the general economic situation, the financial
position of Japan at the beginning of the year appears
fairly strong on the surface, but beneath the surface there
are various more or less ominous factors which tend to weaken
the confidence of competent observers in the soundness of
Japanese finance. In the opinion of some of these observers,
the Japanese financial position has recently been steadily
growing worse and at the close of the year was less sanguine
than at any time during the past twenty-five years. This
opinion is based on a comparison of Japan's annual govern-
mental expenditures with its economic resources, in 1936
and in previous years. Such a comparison reveals the fact
that governmental expenditures have been growing faster than
economic resources -- undoubtedly a dangerous course to pursue.
At the same time, however, the consensus of opinion appears
to be that Japan is in no immediate danger of a financial
collapse.
To consider first the favorable indications, it may be
pointed out that, although the national debt has been growing
rapidly in recent years, because of the issuance of deficit
bonds to cover the unbalanced budgets (unbalanced largely
because of military expenditures in Manchuria and at home),
the total national debt at the close of the year did not
seem to be exorbitant for a nation of the size, population,
resources and power of Japan. Exact figures of the national
debt at the close of the year are not available, but in
November the debt had risen to well over ten billion yen,
and

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.99)
-37 -
and by the end of December it is presumed that it reached
nearly ten and one-half billion yen. This total includes
the external debts computed at the old gold value of the
yen. If the external debts are computed at the present
exchange value of the yen (in which value Japan is paying
interest and principal), the total of the national debt
will exceed eleven billion yen. Even allowing for the fact
that the per capita wealth of the Japanese and the national
resources are very much lower than those of American and
European nations, this total national debt does not seem
large for an active, progressive empire of over ninety
million people.
Although money rates were easy at the year-end, this
was not accomplished by excessive currency inflation. (There
is always a certain amount of inflation of the note issue
at the end of each year, to provide funds to the banks for
the year-end settlements.) On December 30, 1936, the note
issue stood at Yen 1,965,190,000, an increase of about Yen
550,000,000 over the normal issue, which is about Yen 1,400,-
oo0,ooo. It was confidently expected that the note issue
would return to the normal figure soon after the New Year
holidays. Against the note issue there is a specie reserve
of Yen 548,342, 000. The value of this reserve is computed
at the old gold value of the yen; if computed at the present
exchange value of the yen, which is much lower and which it
is not expected will be again raised, it will be found that
the specie reserve affords much more than adequate backing
for the note issue.
Against these factors, however, must be placed unfavorable
factors (chiefly concerned with the unbalanced budgets) which
greatly

=== Page 415 === (confidence 0.99)
- 38 -
greatly weaken Japan's financial position in the eyes of
local observers. The principal factor concerns the so-
called deficit bonds".
During 1936 the Government experienced some difficulty
in disposing of its issues of 3-l/2 percent deficit bonds,
which apparently were being issued in excess of the capacity
of the market to absorb them. Although, as was pointed out
above, the national debt cannot be considered as excessive,
and there is no reason to believe that public confidence
in the solvency of the nation has been impaired to any
considerable extent, the deficit bonds were not readily
taken up by the public and were, to a considerable amount,
left in the hands of the banks. In November the price of
the bonds fell on the open market to 97.70, whereas the
issuing price was 98.00, thus estopping the Government from
issuing more bonds until the market price had been raised
to 98.00. During the 1937-38 fiscal year, the greatly in-
creased budget will require the issuance of even more
deficit bonds than in the 1936-37 fiscal year, the amount
being estimated at between Yen 900,000,000 and Yen 1,000,-
O00,000. When the difficulties experienced in 1936 in
disposing of the bond issues are taken into consideration,
it is not believed that the Government will succeed in
floating its bonds during the coming fiscal year without
some measure of compulsion on the banks, trust companies,
insurance companies and other purchasers and holders of
bonds, to buy the new issues. That it may become necessary
to take some such step has been intimated by the Minister
for Finance. It will be exceedingly difficult for the
Government to maintain the price of its bonds if compulsion
should become necessary in order to dispose of the bonds.
Another

=== Page 416 === (confidence 0.99)
- 39 -
Another angle of the bond situation which is somewhat
disturbing lies in the fact that the banks are unable to
dispose of the bonds to the public. If a bank is able to
dispose of its allotment of bonds to the public, credit
is retired to the amount of the bonds sold, balancing the
credit which must be given the Government. But if the
bank is unable to sell its bonds and thereby retire public
credit, the new credit issued to the Government will not
be balanced and will constitute additional credit thrown
into the financial world. This process will make for credit
inflation, which may become dangerous.
In addition to the difficulties which it is expected
will be found in upholding the price of bonds, it is anti-
cipated that the Government will find it difficult to main-
tain the present exchange value of the yen (one shilling
two pence). The indications are that the import excess,
amounting to about Yen 134,000,000 in 1936, wil1 be larger
in 1937. The great activity of the "munitions" and allied
industries will require the importation of large quantities
of raw materials, while various barter agreements into which
Japan has entered, such as those with Australia and British
India, are conditioned upon Japan purchasing stated minimum
quantities of raw materials. It will therefore be difficult
for Japan to decrease materially its imports in 1937, while
there is not much hope that Japanese exports will increase
to any considerable extent during the year. The presumption
therefore is that the trade balance will be more adverse in
1937 than it was in 1936, and that this factor will cause
further pressure to be exerted upon the yen.
At the same time, foreign bankers in Tokyo are of the
opinion

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.98)
- 40 -
opinion that the foreign exchange reserves abroad (mostly
held in sterling in London) of the Yokohama Specie Bank
have been somewhat depleted by the struggles of that bank
to maintain the value of the yen through 1936 at its present
rate, namely, one shilling and two pence. The foreign ex-
change reserves, maintained as a stabilization fund, normally
amount to about three or four hundred million yen, but it
is now suspected that they stand at a considerably lower
figure, although the Yokohama Specie Bank does not publish
the amounts of the reserves, and exact information is not
available. The Bank's difficulties in bolstering up the
yen in December, when, under the sudden pressure of import
bills covering some four hundred thousand bales of raw
cotton, the yen exhibited a tendency to decline, indicates
a serious weakness in exchange reserves.
Faced with the prospect of a still larger adverse
balance of trade in 1937 and with (presumably) insufficient
foreign exchange reserves abroad, it is expected that the
Government will experience difficulty in 1937 in maintaining
the yen at one shilling two pence. Indications are that the
Government may be forced to exercise stricter control over
foreign exchange transactions, or even to resort to a licen-
sing system for imports.
The adverse factors in Japan's financial position are
due in large measure to the unusually heavy governmental
expenditures (principally military), which result in un-
balanced budgets, large issues of deficit bonds, inflation
of credit and rising prices. These expenditures, which
threaten to become even larger in succeeding years, are
placing burdens upon the nation faster than the increase
of

=== Page 418 === (confidence 0.99)
- 41 -
of economic resources can take up the additional load.
Consequently the financial structure is being shaken,
although not seriously as yet. It is expected by ob-
servers, however, that the Government will be forced to
take measures, much along the lines of those adopted by
Dr. Schacht in Germany, to control the exchange value of
the yen, to maintain the price of Government bonds, and
to control foreign trade. The Yokohama Specie Bank has
announced definitely that it will maintain the yen at
one shilling two pence -- a step taken, it is believed,
in order to avoid further opposition abroad to Japanese
exports should their prices be still further reduced
through a lowered value of the yen.
Opinion differs as to the efficacy of the artificial
measures which it is expected that the Government will take,
some observers believing that it will be impossible for the
Government to bolster the yen for long in the face of the
rapidly increasing governmental expenditures. The prevailing
opinion, however, seems to be that the Government will be
able to avoid a dangerous crisis for some months and perhaps
through 1937, although few observers dare to predict the
future of the yen and of the price of Japanese Government
bonds beyond the end of the year.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

BUROPEAN AFFAIRE
There is attached Mr. Grew's despatch No. 2209,
MAR
OF STATI
dated January 1, 1937, entitled "General Situation
Facing Japan at the Opening of the Year 1937". It is
in effect a general survey of Japan's foreign relations,
of the domestic political situation, of the position of
the Army as a political factor, of the naval policies
of Japan, and of economic and financial conditions in
Japan.
Of Japan's foreign relations in general, Mr. Grew
observes that the new year "opens in an ominous key".
9
He states that Japan's relations with "Great Britain,
Soviet Russia and China are on a far from satisfactory
footing and recently have been growing worse"; and
that, with reference to relations with the United States,
"the long future as contrasted with the immediate present
holds out no evident grounds for optimism". He ascribes
this state of affairs to the control exercised by the
Army over foreign relations.
The relations of Japan with the United States,
China, Soviet Russia, Great Britain, Germany, Italy and
the Netherlands are then diseussed.
F/FG
With

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
With regard to relations with the United States, the
Ambassador cites the situation arising out of the termina-
tion of the Washington Naval Treaty and Japan's aggressive
policy in China as capable of creating acute and hazardous
issues. The Philippine Islands, in the Ambassador's
opinion, also remain as a potential, although not an
immediate, source of danger to these relations. Ho
feels, however, that the Japanese Government values
American friendship, and that it "will not purposely
alienate the United States unless situations arise where
Japan considers her own national interests to be acutely
involved". He concludes this section with the observation
that "The outlook for 1937, so far as Japanese-American
relations are concerned, therefore would not at present
appear to justify pessimism".
With regard to relations with China, Mr. Grew states
that the plans for the extension of Japanese influence
have miscarried, due to the "sudden and unexpected deter-
mination of China to yield no more to Japanese pressure",
and that Japan is now in process of re-formulating its
program in China. Whatever may be the method employed -
whether by economic penetration or by a "frontal attack"
-, the Ambassador believes that "Japanese energies will be
directed towards consolidating Japan's control in North
China and Mongolia".
With regard to relations with Soviet Russia, it is
Mr. Grew's opinion that the improvement recently discernible
in

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.99)
2
- 3 -
in those relations "was rudely shattered" by the conclusion
of the Japanese-German convention to combat communism.
He believes that the adverse effects of that convention
on Soviet sentiment toward Japan are not likely to be
overcome in the near future.
With regard to relations with Great Britain, the
Ambassador observes that a large element of the British
people are anti-Japanese in sentiment as a result of the
Manchuria situation, Japan's aggressiveness in China,
trade competition, and other controversies.
The domestic political situation is summarized by
Mr. Grew as follows:
"At the opening of 1937, to recapitulate,
domestic politics in Japan are characterized
by popular criticism of the Government (partic-
ularly for its foreign policy), and public dis-
satisfaction with the mounting cost of living,
budget increase, and pressure of taxes. Against
this opposition the advantages enjoyed by the
Hirota Cabinet in its effort to stay in office
are, first, that the military (still in control)
are not yet determined that a change would be an
improvement, and second, that the political
parties still lack unity of attack."
The Embassy points out, however, that the Army has
regained, through stricter control of the radical
elements within itself, a large measure of the popular
support which it had before the military revolt of
February, 1936.
The

=== Page 422 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
The economic and financial situation in Japan is
not one which gives the Ambassador cause for great
optimism. Although industrial activity stimulated by the
so-called "munitions boom" may be expected to continue
in l937, the possibility of there being established
state control of key industries and of substantial in-
creases in taxation is a disturbing factor. There has
been a sharp increase in commodity prices, which may lead
to labor troubles, have an adverse effect upon Japan's
export industries and disturb its entire economic system.
Further, it is anticipated that the Government will
have increased difficulty in floating bonds to cover the
unbalanced budget - there having occurred toward the end
of last year a fall in the price of Government bonds - and
it is expected that there will be further falls in such
prices if compulsion is used to effect absorption by the
banks of new bonds. It is expected that the Government
will also experience difficulty in maintaining the present
exchange value of the yen, due to the fact that the country
is committed to the purchase abroad of large quantities
of raw materials, whereas there is little hope of imports
being increased. It is not believed, therefore, that
measures which the Government may take to support the
yen would be effective for very long. The opinion prevails
1937
that a dangerous crisis may develop after the end of the
year.
Comment:

=== Page 423 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Commont:
The picture of Japan, insofar as its foreign relations
and economic and financial condition are concerned pre-
sented in the despatch is, with certain limited exceptions,
one of unrelieved pessimism. The impression is given to
the reader of the year just ended having been for Japan
one of retrogression: the despatch unfolds a succession
of events some of which emphasize Japan's unwillingness
to cooperate with other nations toward the maintenance of
peace, others which reflect determination to pursue a
policy of aggression, and still others which point toward
a deterioration of its economic well-being. On the other
hand, events which may be estimated to have countervailing
effect have in certain instances been overlooked, and in
other instances they have not been presented in accurate
perspective. It is our opinion that, if there has been
any change during l936 in Japan's international position,
those evidences of a trend away from an attitude of
aggression outweigh evidences of a trend in the opposite
direction. To illustrate:
The despatch cites the situation arising out of the
termination of the Washington Naval Treaty as a possible
source of international controversy. The action taken by
Japan to terminate that treaty was taken in 1934 and con-
firmed in 1935. There have recently been given out in
Japan expressions of desire to avoid giving cause for naval
competition,

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
competition, the last being a statement to such effect
made on February l6 in the Diet by the Minister of the
Navy.
Other possible causes of controversy with the United
States cited in the despatch are commercial problems and
the interests in Japan of American oil concerns. With
regard to the former, the Japanese are voluntarily impos-
ing restrictions on exports to the United States of many
competitive goods, while, with regard to the latter, cer-
tain assurances desired by the foreign oil concerns rela-
tive to their future operations in Japan were recently
given by the Japanese Government.
In the economic and financial field, the Embassy
reported in a telegram dated February 19: "The general
financial outlook in Japan is much brighter than it was
a month ago.... Foreign exchange control is operating
effectively and the yen is firm".
Although there has been disposition among press
writers and publicists to exaggerate the significance of
the recent parliamentary crisis in Japan, it is our
estimete that (a) there is obviously a growing dissatis-
faction on the part of the public over the manner in
which the Army has causeā Japan's foreign relations to
be conducted, and that (b) although the Army is now
more firmly entrenched -- through the set-up of the
recently

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.91)
- 7 -
recently created Cabinet -- in a position of "control",
the Army is by no means confident of its being able to
maintain a position of dominance, realizes that it has
led the nation into positions which are of doubtful
advantage, and seems to have been somewhat sobered by a
belated consciousness of the great responsibilities which
it has taken upon itself and of the increasing obstacles
to the success of its activities in the field of foreign
relations.

THE FOREIGN SERVICE

SUBJECT: POLITICAL DEVELOPMENTS IN JAPAN, JANUARY 1937.

The Honorable
