Sir:
During the month of January 1937 the political
situation in Japan has been marked by a mounting
political opposition to the Japanese Army. Although
January has closed with a request to an Army officer,
FILHD
General Senjuro Hayashi, to form a cabinet, the
dominant development of the month has nevertheless
1937
been the focusing of the people's attention against
the military.
As the Embassy pointed out in despatch No. 2209,
January 1, 1937, the year opened in a tone of popular
opposition
F/FG

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
opposition to the Government's policies, more em-
phatic than at any time since the Manchurian incident
of 1931. On January 21, when the Diet met after the
holidays to take up business, the Lower House imme-
diately became active. In the course of interpella-
tions, Mr. Kunimatsu Hamada of the Seiyukai, formerly
Speaker of the House, launched a violent attack upon
the Hirota Cabinet and the military in politics. The
Minister of War handled the ensuing debate awkwardly,
and the session ended in disorder as the Government
obtained approval of the Emperor for the proroguing
of the Diet for two days. During that interval
Hirota's negotiations with the political party leaders
for terms on which the Diet might agree to quiet its
attack were unsuccessful, and on January 23 the re-
signation of the entire Cabinet was submitted and ac-
cepted. Upon recommendation by the Genro, Prince
Saionji, the Emperor on January 24 requested General
Kazunari Ugaki to form a cabinet. The Army refused
absolutely to permit an officer to serve as minister
of war in an Ugaki cabinet and for this reason General
Ugaki on January 29 reported to the Emperor his in-
ability to form a government. After consultation
again between the Genro and the Court, the Lord Keeper
of the Privy Seal and Baron Hiranuma had a conversation
during which the press reports that Baron Hiranuma
stated his inability to accept the responsibility
of the premiership. Following this, General Senjuro
Hayashi

=== Page 430 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Hayashi was summoned to the Palace and received the
Imperial command to form a cabinet. He asked
for two days' time before submitting his reply.
By the end of January General Hayashi's cabinet
was not completed.
In spite of the Army's success in blocking the
choice of a premier opposed by the military and in
forcing the selection of a premier whom the military
favor, the Army has suffered a number of rebuffs during
January 1937. The principal rebuffs number seven as
follows:
(1) The attack on the Army in the Diet on
January 2l was a severe blow. The interpellator,
Mr. Kunimatsu Hamada, included in his remarks, ac-
cording to the press, such statements as:
The Army has taken upon itself the role
of moving force of the nation, end in all the
major incidents of recent years it has made this
force felt. This is true of the May 15(1932)
incident, of the February 26(1936) incident,
of the dictatorial views broadcast by some
sections of the Army from time to time, of
the political views expressed by the War Minis-
ter at the meeting of the Diet System Inquiry
Commission, and of the statement of the com-
mander of the Kwantung Army on the Manchu-
kuo Concordia Society. Dictatorial political
ideas are at all times the undercurrent of
thought within the Army, and there is danger
that this current will destroy the dam set
between the civil and military in accordance
with the Imperial will. The people realize
this and are afraid .. . . The Hirota Cabinet
must lift the heavy pressure brought to bear
on the people by the Army. . . In disregard
of the capacity of the people to bear the
burden, it has compiled an enormous abnormal
budget
A general economic panic
threatens. Because of rash measures of
economic

=== Page 431 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
economic control, industrial confusion is immi-
nent...
The Emperor Meiji granted the
people the privilege of forming legal political
parties, in accordance with His Majesty's will
that a single sovereign should reign over the
welfare of all the people; to the fighting
services, on the other hand, it was His Ma-
jesty's instruction tnat they be not perturbed
by outside opinion and that they refrain from
concerning themselves with politics. . . We
feel that the ideology embodied in the mani-
festo of,the commender of the Kwantung Army
on the subject of the Manchukuo Concordia Society
indicates that politics in anchuria will not
be uided by democratic principles, and that
the Society will simply be the face of the
fabric of which the Government is the back
When it became difficult for the bill for aug-
menting the Army by two divisions to pass the
Japanese Diet, the military clique Government
scattered money among the Diet members and
bought them off. Bribers and bribe-takers
sometimes energe among party men, but the
same can be said of the military. . . A budget
of war dimensions, three times the size pre-
vailing prior to the Manchurian incident, is
being atteapted. With industry scarcely twice
its former capacity, is it surprising that
prices should soa ana the peonle's liveli-
hood be jeo ardized?
Judging by their behavior during the debate, the
majority of members of the House supported the attack.
(2) The Diet was prorogued on a definitely
anti-Army note. This was certainly poor politics on
the part of the Minister of War. The newspapers of
the country ere flooded with dramatic accounts of
the Diet's being prorogued in disorder during a debate
in which the Army was severely attacked, and the public
inescapably received the impression that the Army
was unable to hold its own.
(3) During the prorogued period, the Minister
of War was responsible for several press releases
demanding

=== Page 432 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
demanding the dissolution of the Lower House, but in
this demand the Army lost out. The Hirota Cabinet was
not willing to assume the responsibility for disso-
lution in the face of so unified a front as was pre-
sented by the Diet in its session of January 21. A
better politician than War Minister Terauchi, Hirota
no doubt recognized that for the Government to choose
that time to go to the people would have been poor
strategy. The War Minister's insistence was overruled.
(4) The resignation of the Hirota Cabinet was not
asked for by the military and was an important con-
cession to the political parties. Hirota came into
office with the Army's blessing and has been amenable
to Army direction. For his Cabinet to go out of
office, leaving a large question-mark over the re-
lations between the Diet and a succeeding military
cabinet, is at least a temporary loss to the mili-
tarists.
(5) In the choice of General Kazunari Ugaki
as the first person invited to form a cabinet, the
Army was directly rebuffed. Ugaki was identified
with the moderate element in the pre-1931 Army,
and has shown no sympathy with the Army radicals of
the last years. He was Minister of War in five cabi-
nets between 1924 and 1931, and was in office when
the Army, subsequent to the Washington Conference,
was reduced by four divisions. No doubt he was one
of the individuals held in special opprobrium by
the

=== Page 433 === (confidence 0.99)
-6 -
the radicals in the Army when they were beginning to
organize. From 1931 to 1936 he was Governor-General
of Chosen. General Ugaki is an administrator rather
than a soldier, and he has no sympathy with the poli-
tical group of the Army. He was openly opposed to
the Mazaki-Hata-Araki faction. He enjoys the con-
fidence of Japanese business, and has never lacked
for backing from important business families. Al-
though not a party member his service in Minseito cabi-
nets has given him practical Minseito connections.
In brief, Ugaki came to the cabinet-forming task with
the backing of the political parties, financial circles,
and the majority of the public, and his designation,
in the face of known Army opposition, has been an
important factor in loosing pent-up public opinion
against the Army's political pretensions.
(6) Trroughout the five tense days of Ugaki's
effort to form a government, the newspapers all over the
country played up the Army's responsibility in the frus-
tration of the popular candidate. During this period
the Army was forced to take the defensive, and a more
and more moderate tone in its press releases, con-
trasting with its initial arrogance, was observable.
(7) Immediately before proceeding to the Palace
to inform the Emperor of his inability to form a cabinet,
General Ugaki gave out to the press a strong denunciation
of political interference by the Army. The statement was
in

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
in the form of a letter explaining resignation from his
military commission (effected the same morning), and
included the assertion that Japan is at the point
where she must decide between fascism and parlia-
mentary government, that the Army has become a poli-
tigal organization, and that the Emperor deserves
sympathy for this state of affairs. So insubstantial
is political freedom of speech in this country that
the Government immediately placed a ban upon this
statement. Several of the newspapers had printed
parts of it as extra editions (which were soon with-
drawn from sale), and it has already been reported
abroad, so its existence is videly known in spite
of the censorship.
So much for the blows suffered by the Army during
January. In the selection of General Hayashi to be
premier, has the Army gained a means of recovering
the month's losses? Ugaki was blocked by the Army's
refusal to supply a way minister; Hayashi, who was
himself in that office as recently as the time of the
Okada Cabinet, might be expected to find no difficulty
in that regard. But his task is not as simple as it
might appear at first glance, for he has on his hands
a group in the Army still clamoring for fundamental
political reforms and convinced that now is the time
for the Army to sweep forward. The conditions under
which Hirota was admitted to office after the incident
of February 26, 1936, are ample evidence that the poli-
tical rebels of the Army vere by no means crushed by
their

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
Atheir consent to strict disciplinary measures against
those who had resorted to violence. Hirota brought them
no satisfaction in the matter closest to their hearts,
and they see the present as an opportunity to advance.
Of no importance to them that the Diet has clearly
asked for less of the military in the nation's govern-
ing, and of no importance to them that protest from the
people is appearing - or if of no importance, simply
to point the need for immediate action and to indi-
cate the direction of change. Hayashi's most serious
problem is not to bring into being a government which
can face the adverse criticism evoked by the agreement
with Germany, the failure of negotiations with China,
the mishandling of relations with the Soviet Union,
and the other specific points which brought the Hirota
Cabinet into disrepute. Hayashi's problem is more
fundamental. It is created by the antagonism be-
tween the far-from-dead political rebels of the Army
on the one hand, and on the other hand those civilians,
particularly in business, who fear the economic and
political philosophy of the Army.
General Hayashi brings to his task talents which
are strictly limited. As was pointed out in the Embassy's
994.00549
despatch No. 1414 of July 26, 1935, he is primarily a
soldier, one of the moderates, the man who as war minister
removed Masaki from the high office of inspector general
of military education. So definitely had Hayashi
committed himself against the direct-actionists that
his

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.96)
- 9 -
his retirement from the Supreme Military Council was
considered a necessary part of the compromise by which
the February 26 incident was closed. On discipline in
the Army General Hayashi's position is well known and his
efforts have been effective; but his career has so far
shown no special aptitude in political matters outside
the Army. Himself a neutral, everything depends on the
effective control of his government. The Army hopes
to manipulate him in the direction of an increasingly
nationalistic policy backed by wider government control
of the economic system. Business hopes he can be brought
to block this trend.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

but at the same time has a stern character and a "samurai-

1937

THE FOREIGN SERVICE
OF THE

SUBJECT: POLICIES OF THE HAYASHI CABINET.

With reference to the Embassy's telegram No. 49,
1937
February 8, 7 p.m., in regard to a statement of policy
issued by the Hayashi Cabinet, I have the honor to
report that General Hayashi, on February 8, 1937, after
approval by the Cabinet, issued a statement containing
five points of policy to be followed by the Cabinet.
The statement was extremely vague and nebulous in sub-
stance and was phrased in the ancient classical Japanese
language
F/G
1.

=== Page 443 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
language, which rendered it even more ambiguous. The
language used caused the statement to be incomprehen-
sible to the great mass of the people, but it is be-
lieved that it was thought best to couch it in classi-
cal language in order to make it more impressive and to
emphasize the critical nature of the conditions which
brought the Cabinet into existence.
The preamble of the statement, after the usual loyal
expressions, declares that "in view of the current situation
both at home and abroad, and in cognizance of the urgent
nature of the times", the Cabinet presents its platform
to the nation. The text of the platform is difficult of
translation, owing to its abstract nature and the language
used, and all translations differ considerably, but it
is believed that the following translation, prepared in
the Embassy, accurately conveys the meaning of the five
points of policy.
1. To clarify the concept of the national polity,
to cultivate the great principles of reverence for the
gods and respect for the Emperor, and to enrich the
sources from which the development of the national des-
tiny springs by inculcating the spirit of unity between
worship of the gods and State administration.
2. To ensure the healthy development of the
constitutional government peculiar to Japan in strict
accordance with the provisions of the Imperial Con-
stitution and to conduct State administration fairly
and uprightly with due regard to the wishes of the
people and to public opinion.
3. To execute a national foreign policy with
the united support of the nation, thereby bringing
clarity into international relationships by pro-
moting the stability of ast Asia and the mutual
prosperity of all nations in accordance with inter-
national justice.
4

=== Page 444 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
4. To provide the armaments necessary for the
security of the Empire, for the safeguarding of its
development and for realization of national policies,
and at the same time to foster the national power by
promoting the productive power of the nation.
5. To encourage the collective development of
industry in accordance with economic conditions at
home and abroad, to enforce appropriate measures of
control of industry while devising necessary measures
of protection, and at the same time to develop the
creative power of the people and to encourage their
spirit of enterprise.
The statement of policy concludes vith the following
elucidation, as translated for the press by Domei:
"In order to realize these aims, it is in-
cumbent upon the Government not to resort to the
extreme and to be on its guard against conven-
tionalism. Only by so doing, the Government is able
to carry out reforms in accordance with the trend
of the times. Justice must follow the trend of the
times. Namely, the Government must effect adminis-
trative reforms and stabilize the national livelihood
and thereby to remove the unrest of the public mind.
It is incumbent upon the Government to carry out its
policies vhich it has in contemplation. The existing
situation both at home and abroad is complicated
and does not know where it is leading. The situation
is characterized by individualism and selfishness.
Cooperation is more valuable than anything else under
the present circumstances. Reason will have its own
way. The present Cabinet is composed of the members
observing self-negation. It is against the organi-
zation of eny faction in its existence. The Govern-
ment is sincerely determined to devise such measures
as are designed to effect various reforms in accord-
ance with the expertations of the Throne, and by
being faithful toward its assigned mission."
Later, Premier Hayashi announced that he would elu-
cidate his policies in a radio address to the nation.
This he did on the evening of January 1l. The radio
1/
address (appended hereto in the form of a clipping from
the JAPAN ADVERTISER of February 12) added little or
nothing to the nation's understanding of the actual poli-
cies of the Cabinet.
The

=== Page 445 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
The consensus of opinion of the vernacular press,
as expressed in the editorials of the Tokyo dailies,
was that the statement of policy was too nebulous and
not so concrete as the nation could have wished. Some
of the newspapers accepted the five points of policy
as being quite in line with what could be expected of
the new Cabinet, but also expressed the view that "the
proof of the pudding is in the eating" - that abstract
phrases were sufficient for the moment, but that the
nation must wait for the Cabinet's concrete measures
in pursuance of its policies before it could judge the
worth of the Cabinet.
There was also some mild cri-
ticism of the fact that the statement omitted to mention
the welfare of the agricultural class. As the farmers
are more pros erous this year than for several years
past, however, this omission would seem to have been
intentional.
Although the statement is vague and nebulous, if
one reads between the lines several interesting points
appear. In the second item of policy, no mention is made
of the attitude of the Cabinet toward the Diet or the poli-
tical parties. Instead, there is the unusual reference
to "constitutional government peculiar to Japan". Exactly
what is meant by this phrase no one anpears to understand,
but it carries a hint of opposition to the present form
of parliamentary government which is based on the British
system and therefore is not entirely "peculiar to Japan".
There

=== Page 446 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
There is no doubt that the military influence, dominating
the Hayashi Cabinet, is hostile to any parliamentary
system which does or can give power to political parties,
and it can therefore be assumed that the reference to
"constitutional government peculiar to Japan" contains
a warning to the political parties not to attempt to
impose their will upon the Government or the Army. The
reference later in the same item to "due regard to the
wishes of the people and to public opinion" is undoubt-
edly intended to serve as an inaication to the people
that arbitrary and dictatorial methods of government
(of which there has been some complaint in the past)
will not be employed by the Hayashi Cabinet.
The third item, referring to foreign policy,also
contains some interesting points. The phrase, "national
foreign policy with the united support of the nation",
undoubtedly indicates an intention to discard the "secret
diplomacy" which resulted in 1936 in the German-Japanese
anti-Comintern agreement. This agreement was not welcomed
enthusiastically by the Japanese people, and there was
much outspoken criticism at the time of the secret di-
plomacy employed in negotiating the agreement. The new
Government, apparently as a concession to the public,
has now served notice that there will be no more agree-
ments concluded with other nations without the full know-
ledge of the people. Later in the third item of policy
appears the phrase, "the mutual prosperity of all nations
in accordance with international justice". This might
be

=== Page 447 === (confidence 0.99)
-6 -
be taken to refer to the demand of the "have-nots" that
the world's sources of raw materials and markets for manu-
factured goods be re-distributed. If so, the Hayashi
Cabinet would appear to be notifying the world that Japan
intends to align itself with Germany in demanding more of
the world's opportunities.
The fourth item, concerning armaments, is perhaps the
most interesting of the five items of policy. It will be
observed that the purpose of the armaments is stated to be
not only defense of the mpire but also "the safeguarding
of its (the Empire's) development and for realization of
national policies". This does not mean any new orientation
of Japan's armament policy but it does mean that Japan
has now openly stated that its armaments are not intended
entirely for defense but also for safeguarding Japan's
policy of expansion.
The fifth item, concerning the encouregement of industry,
has aroused some criticism in industrial circles because of
an inconsistency contained therein. In one place the item
mentions "appropriate measures of control of industry" and
later states that the Cabinet will "develop the creative
power of the people and .. encourage their spirit of enter-
prise". It is claimed that these two principles are in-
compatible and that further control of industry will tend
to kill the people's spirit of enterprise.
However, as was stated by some of the newspapers, the
statement of policy does not mean much. The real test of the
Cabinet

=== Page 448 === (confidence 0.96)
-7 -
Cabinet will come when it devises concrete measures for
the execution of its declared policies.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
March 8, 1937
EHD:
Despatch No. 2273 from Embassy, Tokyo,
"New Party Movement in Janan".
A movement is gaining ground toward
the formation by rightest elements, prin-
cipally the army, of a nationalist party,
which resembles fascism in that it opposes
democratic institutions and would have a
mononoly of government, but it differs
from fascism in that it has no definite
political or economic platforms.
COMMENT:
In Japan, where political loyalties
are largely personal rather than to
principles, the lack of a platform is
not necessarily fatal to its success.
Conversely, no party hovever nowerful
is so important a political factor as
in the United States or Great Britain
as policies are determined not by parties
but by individual consultations among
leaders.

S
Desnatch No. 2268 from Embassy,
Tokyo, "Pclitical Developments in Japan,
February 1 to 16, 1937":
The despatch discusses circumstances
under which General Hayashi's Cabinet was
formed. He being politically a neutral
his choice was in the nature of a com-
promise between those favoring the moderate
Ugaki and the chauvinistic element in the
army led by Itagaki. There is forecasted
a move toward the development of a
single dominating party, inspired by the
army, tc control the niet.
COMMENT
It would anpear that the mandate
to a war leader to form a cabinet is
the logical outcome of a situation in
which army interference in politics
rendered it impossible for a civilian
nremier to exercise full authority and
discharge his responsibility. Now that a
soldier is Premier, the army group will
find it difficult to shift responsibility
for maladministration, which may well
imrel it to move more cautiously and with
greater moderetion.

In continuation of the Embassy's despatch No. 2241,
February 1, l937, I have the honor to report political
developments from February 1 to February 16 connected
with the formation of a cabinet by General Hayashi.
General Hayashi's efforts to form a cabinet began on
January 30, and on February 2 the Cabinet was installed in
FILED
17 1937
office although not entirely complete. By February 10
the Cabinet was as follows:
Premier
F/G

=== Page 453 === (confidence 0.98)
60-2
- 2 -
Premier and Foreign Minister, General Senjuro Hayashi.
Minister of Education, General Hayashi (concurrently).
dF
Minister of Overseas Affairs, General Hayashi(concurrently).
War Minister, General Gen Sugiyama.
Navy Minister, Admiral Mitsumasa Yonai.
Minister of Finence, Mr. Toyotaro Yuki.
Minister of Home Affairs, Mr. Kakichi Kawarada.
Minister of Justice, Mr. Suehiko Shiono.
Minister of Communications, Count Hideo Kodama.
Minister of Agriculture and Forestry, Mr. Tatsunosuke
Yamasaki.
Minister of Commerce and Industry,. Admiral Tatsuo Godo.
Minister of Railways, Admiral Godo (concurrently).
These appointees are without party affiliations.
Of this Cabinet the outstanding figure is Mr. Yuki,
Minister of Finance. Mr. Yuki is a banker of long
experience, a men of great force, and an effective
leader. Biographical information on each member of the
new cabinet is being submitted in the usual form, without
covering despatch, by the same pouch by which this de-
spatch is forwarded.
The first statement of policy by the Hayashi Cabinet
was the grandiloquent declaration of five principles com-
701
mented upon by the Embassy's despatch No. 2256 of Feb-
ruary 15, 1937. The second importent statement was
contained in the Premier's speech of February 15(Embassy's
telegram No. 57, February 15, 6 p.m.) to the Diet, which
had until then been continuously prorogued to permit the
new government to pre are measures(including the budget)
to

=== Page 454 === (confidence 0.99)
60-3
-3 -
to submit for consideration. The speech showed a desire
for harmonious relations with the Diet, announced that the.
Cabinet would submit a budget ¥270,000,000 less than
that put forward by the Hirota Cabinet before its re-
signation, was firm in tone concerning relations with
the Soviet Unicn(with regard to which the timeliness of
the agreement with Germany against communism was affirmed),
and contained a brief friendly statement about relations
with the United States. A news report of Premier Hayashi's
1/ speech is enclosed.
The present budget for 1937-1938 is in the sum of
¥2,770,000,000, which is stil1 19 higher than the budget
for 1936-1937; but the Hirota budget called for 30% more
than did the 1936-1937 bud et. The saving in comparison
with the Hirota budget is not at the expense of military
a propriations, with the exception of one military item of
¥23,000,000 which is simply deferred until the following
yeer.
The reception accorded by the Diet to the Hayashi
Cabinet has been in the main friendly and smooth, but on
February 16 inter ellations in the Lower House were ad-
versely critical of participation in politics by the military.
Although the debate did not get out of hend it is reported
by the press to have given serious concern to army leaders.
So much for a brief statement of the externals of
Cabinet developments during the period under review. What
was going on behind the scenes? Piecing together the
narrative from the best information available at the present
time, the Embassy submits the following interpretation.

=== Page 455 === (confidence 0.97)
60-4
一4 -
When Ugaki's efforts to form a cabinet were blocked,
it was the Army and solely the Army which was responsible
for the failure. All other important groups in the country
u..0
favored Ugaki. To say that the Army was unitedly against
Ugaki, however, would give a false impression. Both
Terauchi and Sugiyama are personally friendly to Ugaki,
but it was utterly impossible for them to assist in the
formation of an Ugaki cabinet because of one group within
the Army which is bitterly opposed to Ugaki. Although
it is impossible to say that the entire Japanese Army
stands for such and such, this one group is known to be
homogeneous, well defined, and clear in its program.
The group is the Kwantung Army faction, the leader of which
is Lieutenant-General Seishiro Itagaki, Chief of Staff
of the Kwantung Army. The Itagaki group believes that
Japan must take Mongolia by a war against the Soviet
Union; it insists upon rapidly mounting military appro-
priations in preparation therefor; it favors state
socialism as launched in Manchuria; it anticipates changes
in Japan modelled after the new régime in Manchuria; it
backs the agreement with Germany against communism; and it
believes in circumscribing politics by the building up of
a monopoly party, under Army inspiration, of the fascist
type. It is the group within the Army of definite poli-
tical ideas and definite political danger.
To the Itagaki group Ugaki as Premier was completely
beyond acceptance. The choice fell on Hayashi as a moderate
soldier without political ideas, and the Itageki group
consented to that choice because in the last years Hayashi
hes

=== Page 456 === (confidence 0.99)
60-5
- 5 -
has had the good sense to confine himself to military
affairs rather than to politics. By this principle he
has avoided offending the Itagaki group, although not
himself one of them. But Hayashi has been in various
important positions since serving as assistant to Araki
and of course realizes the strength of the Itagaki group.
This realization, as well as Hayashi's shortcomings in
political intuition, was immediately revealed by Hayashi's
proposing Itagaki as his Mar Minister and Suetsugu as
his Navy Linister.
If, in the face of the Diet attack of January 21
on the Army, Hayashi had gone ahead with his desire to
place Itagaki and Suetsugu in his Cabinet, the result
would have been a political u heaval unparalleled in
Japan in many years.
S ner advisers to Hayashi, who
have followed the trend of public opinion, were able to
stop this mistake. In checking this short-sighted scheme
of Hayashi's the Havy played an important part. When
asked to allow Suetsugu to serve, the Navy put forward
Admiral Yonai who is by all odds the biggest man in the
Japanese Navy, the Togo of the present generation, a
moderate, and opposed to the active program of direction
of the nation's affairs by the Army. So high is Yonai's
standing that it was impossible for Hayashi to refuse
the generous gesture of the Navy in offering his ser-
vices as Navy Minister.
Hayashi's desire to have Itagaki as War Minister
suffered a sinilar fate. The moderate "Big Three" of the
Army(Sugiyama, Terauchi, Umezu) balked absolutely. When
Sugiyama himself was offered the position of War Minister
with

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.98)
60-6
-6-
with Itegaki as Vice Minister again Hayashi was voted
down. The compromise reached was the choice of Naka-
mura, a nondescript moderate, who was succeeded after
a week's time by Sugiyama; but by Sugiyama with Umezu
as Vice Minister, not Itagaki. A man named Sogo, a
follower of the Itagaki group, had acted as adviser
to Hayashi in the organizing of the cabinet, and had
pushed the Itagaki-Suetsugu panel. When calmer heads
won the day Sogo withdrew from the scene in disgust
and has taken up duty at Tientsin in an enterprise
which the Kwantung Army is interested in developing.
Superficially the Itagaki group has been excluded
from the present Government, but the importance of
this exclusion should not be misinterpreted. Hayashi
is a compromise called for by the etiquette of Far
Eastern political psychology, but he is not a serious
handicap to the further development of the aims of
the Itagaki group. The Itagaki group definitely
anticipates war with the Soviet Union and is insistent
on increased appropriations to pave the way. Hayashi
is not co mitted to war but nevertheless believes in
larger military a propriations and will doubtless succeed
in getting them. mis very limitations as a political
thinker make him a Premier not unwelcome to those men
in the Army who have very definite political ideas for
Japan's future.
A compromise choice, Hayashi made his first announce-
ment of policy in the form of five non-committal principles
which might be interpreted by opposing factions at will.
Dependent

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.98)
60-7
P.
- 7 -
Dependent on the Diet's approval of a budget making
large appropriations to the military, he has been
forced to go to the Diet in as friendly an attitude
as possible. There is no reason to suppose that Hayashi
has any special leaning to constitutional politics.
He is simply a soldier and because he desires appro-
priations he is approaching the problem in the smoothest
manner he knows.
It should not be supposed that the Itagaki group
has been shunted off by the developments of-the last
month. In an importent res ect that faction of the
Army has been awakened to new effort. An Itagaki
controlled cabinet has been prevented, and it has
been prevented because of fear of the Diet and the
Diet's influence on public opinion. This failure
has pointed with great force to the need for pushing
another side of the Itagaki progrem: the development
of a monopoly political party to control the Diet
and to permit constitutional politics to operate in a
manner which the Army conceives to be of the nature
of state structure eculiar to Jepan, but which in
actuality would probably be not far different from
the Nazi party system in Germany or the Fascist
party system in Italy.
For this purpose the Army has at hand the nucleus
of an organization vhich simply requires expansion.
Under the leadership of Lieutenent-General Yoshitsugu
Tetekawa, retired, with the cooperation of other retired
officers(Colonel Kingoro ashimoto, Colonel Junichiro
Kobayashi, Vice Admiral Shozaburo Kobayashi) there was
held

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.98)
60-8
7
- 8 -
held on November 15, 1936, a conference of military
organization leaders to discuss the formation of a party
temporarily called the "restoration" party. Making use
of already existing patriotic societies and reactionery
orgenizations, this movement has already shown great
activity and will probably be developed further by the
Army in the present political co tingency.
The lessons
to be derived from the monopoly parties so effectively
employed by Eitler and .ussolini have not been without
a hearing among army men in Ja an. Already in lianchuria,
where the Kantung Army is able to try out its political
philosophy, the Concordia party is carrying into exe-
cution political ideas which are undoubtedly entertained
by the Army for Japan itself.
What can be expected for the future? During the last
two months the people, sensitive (like others of the human
rece) in the region of the purse-string, stunned by a
request for approval of a budget thirty percent larger
than the last because of military demands, gathered their
voice in protest. The government fell. Effort to form
a cabinet favored by the entire civilian population was
blocked by the Army, end a military cabinet, though a
moderate one, has been installed. It presents e slightly
smaller budget but with the same increased military expen-
ditures. Because of the necessity of having the budget
passed, its attitude toward the Diet is friendly and
conciliatory. But, under the surface, back of this
stop-gap cabinet, something else is going on. The Army
has learned its lesson. Under the present organization
of

=== Page 460 === (confidence 0.97)
60-9
- 9-
of party politics the Diet can be troublesome. The
Army must work through the Diet also. The next move is
the active development of a single dominating Army-in-
spired party to control the Diet. Concentrated effort
in this direction may confidently be expected. At
present it remains the only point of vulnerability
in the program of the military bureaucracy. In this
connection reference is made to the Embassy's despatch
704
No. 2273,/dated February 18, 1937.
Respectfully yours,
S
Joseph C. Grew
